No. 1.—DERIVATION  OF   THE   NAME   OF   GOURNAY  (page 6).
The derivation of the name Gournay appears to arise from this circumstance;
Gore in Saxon, and I  believe in Celtic, means mud, the Saxon genitive of which
is Gorena;  and Eye, which means waters, or island, is  the second syllable of
the word;  hence Gorena-eye, the muddy waters or island.   The rivers Epte and
St. Aubin  are both of muddy water.  I  owe this derivation to  an Antiquary
learned in the Northern languages.

## p. 726 ##

No. 2.—CarrraLs IN   THE   CHURCH  OF   ST. HILDEVERT  (page 13).
The four large capitals engraved in this page seem all to contain the honey-
suckle ornament, which was much used by the Greeks; and which M. Layard
found in the ruins of Nineveh.  Vol. II. p. 204.
These capitals were possibly brought from the East by Hugh de Gournay,
the Crusader. A reference to the precedingnote may explain the peculiar hiero-
glyphical representation of water on some of  these capitals, which was pointed
out by Mr. Dawson Turner, in Cotman’s Normandy.

## p. 727 ##

No. 3.—CHurcH or NoTRE  DAME AT  GOURNAY  (page 14).
-  Ce clocher est garni d'une bonne sonnerie.   Ce  fut  principalement un bour-
geois nommé Barthelemy de Hevecourt, dont le tombean, et celui de sa femme
nommée Marie, sont dans la chapelle Saint Jacques de la ditte Eglise de Notre
Dame, qui contribua le plus libéralement au batiment de cette eglise; dans la-
quelle du consentement de sa femme, et de Jean son fils, il fonda en 1248 entre
les mains du chapitre, une messe, aperpétuité, pour étre ditte par le curé a I'autel
de St. Jaques.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay).

## p. 728 ##

M. L’Anglois, the author of the Andelys MS., says the church of  Notre Dame
was built about the year 1230, by  Barthelemy de Hevecourt, and other inhabi-
tants of Gournay. This seems true from the architecture of the church.  In such
case the Gournays had nothing to do with  it, as they  had left  Normandy  pre-
viously; and M. de Gondeville clearly mistook the tomb of  Barthelemy de Heve-
court and his wife, for that of Hugh de Gournay IV. and Melisandra. William
Guillelmar was the first curé of Notre Dame, and was appointed by the chapter
of St. Hildevert, on the Saturday after St. Matthew's day, 1250.
(Les Andelys MS.).
No. 4.—LePer House  AT   GOURNAY  (page 14).
The Leproserie was a quarter of a mile out of the town, now on the Paris road;
the Chapel is here represented—

## p. 729 ##

No. 5.—CHAPEL   OF ST. JULIEN   (page 14).
La Chapelle de Saint Julien, scituée proche I'eglise collegialle, étoit l'une des
plus anciennes de Gournay.   Hugues V., qui en fut le dernier seigneur, y fit une
fondation pour I'entretien du luminaire:  cette chappelle étoit desservie par un
chapellain titulaire nommé par le chapitre.   En 1591 Les Réligieuses Corde-
lieres étant obligées de se retirer  dans la ville 4 cause des guerres de la ligue, le
chapitre leur donna la chapelle de Saint  Julien pour y célébrer leur office, avec
la maison du chapellain, et une autre pour leur demeure.
(Paris MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay).
No. 6.—CaisTor (page 17).
Caistor might probably be given to the Gournays as a protecting fortress from
the incursions of the Danes and Norwegians, and was perhaps a lordship having
certain rights over Yarmouth;  since we find  Gerard de Gournay called * baro
de Yarmouth,”—in an ancient pedigree.
Like Counts of the Saxon shore, the lords of Gournay were perhaps placed on
the Norfolk coast for  the same object; *  and the same remark  would apply to
the Albinis at Castle Rising, the Warrens at Castleacre, and the Bigods at Nor-
wich, in their castle of Blanchefleur.
As a proof of the rights of the Gournays over Yarmouth, we find   Gurney’s
Conge,” temp. Richard II., and “King’s Conge”;  the word *“ Conge” meaning
leave or licence; and Conge being a part of  the quay where the King, and at
some period a lord of Gournay, had given leave to the inhabitants of  Yarmouth
to lade and unlade their vessels.           (See Manship’s Yarmouth, page 247).
It is  difficult  to  say when the sand Yarmouth was built  upon, was first
inhabited.
®  The Count of the Saxon shore had under his command 2200 foot, 2200 horse, and 9 prepositi,—one
of whom, with the Stablesian horse, was stationed at Gariononum, and another, with the Dalmatian horse,
at Branodunum, now Brancaster.
(Manship’s Yarmouth, page 211).

## p. 730 ##

No. 7.—FORTIFICATIONS OF  GOURNAY (page 24).
M. L’Anglois, the author of Les Andelys M.S.,  is of a contrary opinion to
that stated in the text.   He thinks the original fortress of Gournay only included
what is  called the ¢    Chateau”, in  the  plan given below,—that it  was not  of
greater size during the rule of  the ancient lords, and that there was a strong
gate at what was afterwards the tour de 1’'Horologe.
It is certain that the church of Notre Dame was not built  until  after the
Gournays had retired into England;  when it, and the faubourg in which it was
placed, were surrounded by a wall, and eventually called the “ ville.”
Philip Augustus appears to  have repaired and extended the  fortifications;
and probably the remains of  those now visible on the boulevards, &c., were of
his erecting.
La Tour Hue was of course of an earlier date;  itis now entirely gone, al-
though remaining a century ago.

Il est vray  que cinque ans apres, en 1074  Canut  fils du feu Roy Swen, de

No. 11.—CaLvELVILLA    (page 45).
Cauville near Montivilliers,  called Cauvilles-les-Eglises, is the ancient Cal-
vavilla or Calvelvilla.
(See Guillémeth, part iv., page 89).
No. 12.—AUSGER  DE   GOURNAY (page 46).
Le plus  ancien de  Gournay dont  nous trouvons quelque chose est nommé
Augerius;  on voit son nom entre les  temoins qui souscrivirent  2  une charte de
I'an 1076, par laquelle Hugues premier seigneur de Gournay donne a l'abbaye
du Bec, la dime de trois paroisses scituées en Angleterre. Apparemment que cet
Augerius de Gournay etoit ou frére, ou proche parent de Hugues, mais nous ne
pouvons rien dire d’assuré a ce sujet.
(Paris M.S. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay).
The term   le plus ancien de Gournay” refers in this passage to the collaterals
of the lords of Gournay.    This manuscript gives a considerable list of their col-
lateral descendants.    It  does not notice the most ancient of these branches, viz.,
that of La Ferté.

deux enfans,* ils auront chacun donné au Bec la portion qui leur en etoit echué.
(Paris M.S. Hist: Les Seigneurs de Gournay).

Anno ab incarnatione 1112, Hugo, filius Girardi de Gornaco concessit ea que
pater suus Girardus, et Hugo de Gornaco avus suus, et avia sua Basilia dederunt
ecclesize sanctze Marice Becci, videlicet quidquid habebant in Longolio, excepto
quod ibi tenebat Hugo de Rivo, et  Ranulphus Sulhus, de quorum tamen terra
dederunt eidem ecclesiae totam moltam, et omnes consuetudines ad molendinum
pertinentes. Dederunt etiam totum dominium quod habebant in Bosco Girardi,
et ecclesiam de Maccio, et ecclesiam de Braimonasterio, cum decimi, et terra, et
omni jure pertinentibus ad eandem ecclesiam, sicut eam tenebant clerici qui  in
ed serviebant. Et dederunt ecclesiam de Wellebouo (Elboeuf) cum toti decim4,
et ecclesiam de Rozerid cum omnibus qua pertinent ad ipsam ecclesiam; dede-
Lungoly;  de dono Hugonis de Gornay, Boscum Gerardi—de dono Girardi de Gurnay, Lisingham.”  At
the same page of the Monasticon is another Charter of Henry II.,—perhaps of  a previous date—in which
is this passage: ‘**Ex dono Girardi de Gornaio et Basilie matris sus, medietatem totius manerii de Lon-
lio,  cum omnibus ad ipsum manerium pertinentibus. Item ex dono ipsius G.  et B. matris  ejus,  ecclesiam
do Brannost, cum terra et  decima et  omnibus qua  ad eam  pertinent, et manerium de Bosco Girardi, cum
omnibus pertinentiis.     And again:  ‘Ex  dono Hugonis de Gornaio decimam de preepositura et portione
sui in   villa de Esthoci et pertinentiis suis.”                      |
By all this it appears that the mother of Gerard de Gournay, Basilia,   gave half of Longueil to the monas-
tery of  Bec—if no error has arisen from Basilizz being written for Basil.    In the charter first  quoted it
is clear there was a Basil de Gournay, doubtless son of Hugh and Basilia.    Esthoci is  Ecouche on  the
Orne.—See pages 55 and  65.— Brannost  is Braimonstier.
Longueil is  near Dieppe.  The monks of Bec exchanged their portion of this place with the Crown.—
See Guillemet.   Histoire des Environs de Dieppe, 124.
Lisingham here given to the abbey of Bec is   Lessingham in Norfolk. Itis said  by  Blomefield that Gerard
de Gournay   founded a  priory there, attached to the abbey of Bec.   Dugdale and   Taylor in his Monasticon
(p. 5) confirm   this.       e priory of l.essingham was made subject to the abbey  of Okeburn in Wiltshire, a
cell to that of Bec,  It  was seized with all alien lands in the wars of Henry    V.      with France.   There are
no traces now left of the existence of this priory at Lessingham.

Hugues de Gournay donna encore au Bec, (Cartulaire du Bec), la moitie du
droit de mouture—dimidiam moltam—de la paroisse du Bosc Girard, dont Basilie
sa femme et Girard leur fils donnerent depuis toute la seigneurie a cette abbaye.
(Paris MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay).
There were other donations made by the lords of  Gournay to the Abbey of
Bec of a later date;  of these Bledlawe near Aylesbury was one;  also a manor
or fortress called Haute Roche, forming part of Chateau Gaillard:  these will
be noticed afterwards. The charter of Hugh de Gournay making this donation
in the year 1200, is contained in a fragment of the Chartulary of Bec, lately dis-
* Fordham, Liston and Ardley in Essex.

No. 15.—RaouL DE   GOURNAY  (page 63).
Raoul de Gournay, a souscrit, en 1103,  a un accord qui se fit cette année en
presence de Henry I. roy  d’Angleterre et  duc  de  Normandie,  et de sa  femme,
Mahaud, entre Guillaume abbé de Fecamp et Philippe de Briense.
(Paris MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay;  from the list of collaterals).
I  do not discover who this Raoul de Gournay was.  It by no means follows
from his being called “de Gournay ” that he and others were of the race of the
lords of Gournay en Brai, although he very probably was of that family.

dregisili, tali conventione ut ipsi participes sint fraternitatis et societatis ejusdem

fratribus in capitulo facta est, et super  altare posita  donatio.   Ex parte  eorum

 Quodam

naco, quia idem Willelmus quemdam militem ejusdem Girardi,  qui contra eum

No. 24.—CHARTER oF  HucH DE   GoOURNAY  V.  (page 87).
Confirmation of this Charter by Hugh de Gournay V.
Notum sit presentibus et futuris, quod ego Hugo de Gurnaio, filius Hugonis de
Gurnaio, (i.e. V.)  concedo, ratamque habeo compositionem que inter Rogerium
abbatem et  conventum  Sancti  Salvatoris, e¢  Henricum abbatem  et conventum
Pratellensem communi consilio et consensu utriusque conventus, anno ab incar-
natione Domini M°C°LXX" II° terminata est in hunc modum.   Rogerius abbas
et conventus Sancti  Salvatoris, communi  consilio,  in perpetuum  concesserunt
ecclesie Pratelli, quidquid beneficii habuerunt in  Nova Villa, juxta opidum de
Drincurt, sita, et in ecclesia, et in molendino, et in nemore, in pratis et in campis,
et in omni alia possessione, hac conditione interposita:  Abbas videlicet Pratelli,
ecclesie Sancti Salvatoris quinquaginta et quinque solidos monete per Norman-
niam generaliter discurrentis, in festivitate Apostolorum Petri et Pauli annuatim
reddet.   Cum igitur prenotatum beneficium in feudo meo, et in protectione me,
consistat, compositionem gratii illius beneficii ordinatam ratam haheo, eamque
amoris intuitu concedo, et cum pater meus carta sua eam diligenter confirmavit,
ego eque diligenter vel diligentius, auctoritate sigilli mei eam confirmo.   Hujus
rei testes fuerunt,  Johannes de Hodenc, Garnerius de Hodenc, Hugo de Bella
villa, Ricardus de Muntenni, Ricardus ad Fosse, et alii multi.
This, and the preceding Charters of Hugh de Gournay IV. and V., to the Abbey
of Préaux, dated 1172, are in  the Chartulary of that house.—MS. of the 13th
century belonging  to M. de  Bosseville, f. 51. V°., No. 115 and 52.   No. 116.

No. 26.—HueH DE  GOURNAY AND  THE  CONVENT OF  ST.WANDRILLE (page 88).
In 1157, the following agreement was made between the convent of St. Wand-
rille and Hugh de Gournay:
Pateat legentibus legentiumque auditoribus, hanc conventionem inter abbatiam
Sancti Wandregisili, et Hugonem de Gornaio esse factam:  quod idem ipse Hugo
habebit de terra et nominibus apud Fontanas, cum appenditiis suis in Braio ma-
nentibus, tam presentibus quam futuris, de singulis terram colentibus, sive bove
give  jumento, unum  sextarium avene, intra  quintum decimum diem post fes-
tum Sancti Remigii, legali mensurd qua emitur et venditur in eadem villa, et hee
avena  jussu monachi  ibi  manentis, et per manum  famuli sui,  famulis  predicti
Hugonis tradetur; nec nunquam precatorias* terre [sic] in anno.  Hac de causa
habebunt homines sancti Wandregisili adjutorium, et consilium, et advocationem
domini Hugonis ubique,  et  herbagiumn in  communi pastura de terra sua.   Si
vero forte aliquis ex hominibus sancti Wandregisili captus fuerit in nemoribus,
pratis sive bladiis, viso damno in curia domini Hugonis apud Machiacum, aut
emendabit, aut per solam manum se disrationabit. De omnibus aliis querelis in
curia sancti Wandregisili, apud Fontanas placitabunt.  Euntes et redeuntes ad
molendina monachorum in  pace ibunt  et  redibunt, exceptis consuetudinibus
domini Hugonis.   Hzec pactio facta est anno ab incarnatione domini M°C°LVII°
®  Precatoria, or precaria, was an instrument by which land was let or given with an annual payment

Hugone de Gornaio.  Hujus pactionis sunt testes, ex parte sancti Wandregisil:i

No. 29.—ABBEY OF   ST. SAUVEUR (page 89).
Among other favours to the church, Hugh de Gournay V. confirmed, in the
following charter, the exchange previously made between the Abbeys of  St.
Sauveur and Préaux, of  the land in  Neuville, originally given by  Payn  of
Elbeuf.—See page 68.   The date of this agreement was 1172.
Notum sit presentibus et futuris,  quod ego Hugo de Gurnaio, filius Hugonis
de Gurnaio, concedo, ratamque habeo, compositionem que inter Henricum Ab-
batem et conventum Pratelli, et Rogerium abbatem et conventum Sancti Salvatoris,

communi consilio et consensu utriusque conventus, anno ab incarnatione Domini

No. 30.—Don~arioNn or  HueH DE   GoURNAY IV. (page 89).
Hugh de Gournay IV. made a donation to the Abbey of Val Notre Dame, as
appears in the Chartulary  of that Abbey—ifol. 4., v°. MS. of the commencement
of the 13th century, preserved in  the national archives at Paris, No. L.  208.
Hugo de Gurnai, omnibus hominibus, baronibus, ministris et fidelibus suis,
salutem.  Notum vobis esse volo me concessisse Deo et ecclesie Sancte Marie
de Valle, per totam terram meam quitanciam a teloneo et passagio, et omni alia
consuetudine. Quod ut semper ratum permaneat, sigilli mei impressione confirmo,
concedente Milesent,  uxore  mea,  et  filiis  meis,  Girardo  et  Hugone.     Testes.
Johannes de Hosdeng, Willelmus de Belsault, Savari de Age,Hugo de Avednis,
et Rogerus frater ejus.
The Abbey of Val Notre Dame was in the diocese ofEvreux.   It was founded
in 1137, chiefly by the Giffarts Counts of Longueville and Buckingham.—Neus-
tria Pia, p. 785.
No. 31.—HueH  DE   GoURNAY  IV.  To   BivaL  (page 89).
Hugh de Gournay and Millicent were benefactors to the Nunnery of Bival
near Neuchatel.  In the Archives at Rouen is a letter of  Philip  Le  Bel,  dated
1305, in which are these words :—
“  Cum Hugo de  Gourneyo et  Milesendis,  ejus uxor, monialibus de  Byvaus
apudPetrum montem commorantibus, boscum mortuum ad ardendum, vivum ad
edificandum, et pasturam bestiis suis,  habenda et  capienda in foresta de Conte-
ville, quam ad presens tenemus, concessissent *    *    ”
No. 32.—HueH DE   GourNay  IV.  (page 89).

between the dates 1164 and 1167.

No. 36.—CHARTER  oF   HUGH   DE   GoOURNAY   (page 90).
Hugh  de Gournay in  1175 confirmed the gift of Robert de Mesnil and his
sons, to the abbey of St Mary and St. Andrew of Gouffern, in Normandy.
Universis ecclesie filiis, Hugo  de Gorneio, salutem.    Noverint omnes tam pre-
sentes quam futuri, quod Robertus de Mesnil et filii ejus Willelmus, Symon et
Galterius, et Matildis uxor  ejus, et filiee  ejus dederunt Deo et  ecclesie Sancte
Marie,  Sanctique Andrie,  et  monachis ibidem  servientibus, quandam partem
terre sue de Maisnil, pro salute animarum suarum, et parentum suorum, in per-

## p. 744 ##

petuam elemosinam, liberam ab omni redditu et  servitio, et exactione seculari,
videlicet bruereta, que sunt inter nemus de Brunlevain, et nemus de Maisnil, sicut
noa*  Aalardi a parte Sancti Bricii, usque ad doitum{  de Mes de bivio  Alneti,
ex inferiore parte Alneti sursum in assensu doiti usque ad duas petras, de petris
recta linea usque ad Muternam sicut  ostensum fuit,  a Muterna sicut nemus fuit
monstratum, usque ad chiminum Sancti Bricii, a chimino Sancti Bricii usque ad
doitum Aalardi, a doito de Mes usque ad nemus de Branlevain sicut monstratum
fuit.    Concesserunt et pasturam communem inter se et monachos.    Si vero pro-
cessu temporis in ipsa terra orietur aliqua calumpnia,  si  non  poterint  eam de-
fendere et tueri, prefati domini vel heredes eorum juxta eandem terram, dabunt
tantumdem terre ad valentiam pro ipsa terra, arbitrio liberorum hominum.   Not-
andum vero quod idem Robertus et Willelmus filius ejus, affidaverunt quod hanc
donationem tenebunt et inconcusse servabunt prefate abbatie, omnibus diebus vite
sue, quam ipsi et predicti Symon et  Galterius in capitulo monachorum conces-
serunt, et super altare posuerunt.    Monachi  vero  concesserunt eis, ut ille qui
heres erit de Maisnil, si etate convenienti, ad portandum ordinem Cisterciensem
voluerit fieri monachus, vel venire ad religionem,  quod suscipietur.   Hanc con-
cesserunt donationem et conventionem duo filii predicti Willelmi, videlicet Adam
et Hugo.     Sane memorie commendandum quod,  pro hac donatione et conces-
sione dederunt monachi prelibato  Roberto unum  palefridum,  et Willelmo filio
ejus alterum, Symoni filio ejus XX  solidos, Galtero unum caballum.  .Matildis
uxor ejus habuit unam vaccam, Aaliza filia ejus primogenita X  solidos Andega-
vensium, Emma filia ejusdem Roberti V solidos, Johanna V solidos.  Duo  filii
Willelmi habuerunt V solidos.  Testibus his; Rogero monacho presbytero de
Seran, Sansone de Neceio Sacerdote, Olivero de Bocheio, et Willelmo filio ejus-
dem, Rogero de Sancto Audoeno, Johanne de Sancto Briccio, Roberto de Sancto
Briccio, Willelmo Bacun, Gaufrido de Locheio, Willelmo Rosel.    Hance autem
donationem et conventionem ratam et liberam in perpetuum esse concedo et sigilli
mei  munimine  confirmo.    Testibus  his:   Guarnerio  de  Hosdenc,  Hugone  de
Bellavilla, Hugone de Aves—anno ab incarnatione Domini M°C°LXXVe,
Chartulary of  St. André de Gouffern—No. 273 in  the  Archives
of the Department of Calvados, at Caen.
®  Noa—a pasture.            ¥ Doitus—a pond or canal,

## p. 745 ##

No. 37.—HueH DE   GourNay IV.  (page 91).
Cette paix fut advantageuse & Hugues de Gournay, qui rentra ainsi en posses-
sion de ses etats, et qui jouissant depuis d'une tranquilité convenable a songrand
age, ne songea plus sans doute, que son salut, et ne s’applica plus dans la suitte
que des oeuvres de piété.   En effet nous trouvons qu'il confirma en 1176, toutes
les donations que ses ancétres avoient faites   'abbaye 
du Bec, et qu’en 1180 il fit
avec le chapitre de Gournay,unechangede quelques biens quepossedoit cechapitre,
pour d'autres revenus, et confirma en meme temps tous les biens que cette colle-
giale avoit recus de la libéralité de ses ancetres.
Paris MS. histoire des Seigneurs de Gournay.
No. 38.—HueH DE   GouUrNaAY IV.  (page 91).
I  est vrai que le célébres généalogiste André du Chesne, et apres lui  Jean
Pillet autheur de histoire de Gerberoy ont divisé en deux ce Hugues, fils de
Girard, et ont cru que celui dont le chateau de la Ferté fut brulé en 1151, par
Henry II. duc de Normandie, qui fut depuis Roy d’Angleterre, n’etoit pas le meme
qui avoit vecu sous le regne de  Henry premier, et qui en 1118 et en 1127 etoit
entré dans le parti  du Prince Guillaume, fils de Robert Courte-heuse; mais
outre qu'on ne pourra  jamais rien trouver dans les ecrivains de Normandie et
d’Angleterre, d’ou on puisse prouver cette distinction de deux Hugues, qu'on n’a
apparement fondée que sur la  longue vie de Hugues IV., nous avons de quoi
détruire absolument cette opinion.   Dans une charte du Bec, dattée de 'an 11786,
par laquelle Hugues de Gournay en confirmant les donations de ses fréres, se
nomme  expressement fils  de Girard, et  qui  prouve  sans réplique  que  c'est le
meme Hugues qui depuis le commencement du 12° siecle a vecujusqu'en 1181 :
voicy les termes de cette  charte :—* Approbata  consuetudinis esse dignoscitur
ut  memorize commendentur litterarum quae necessario transmittenda sunt  ad
notitiam posterorum. Ka propter sciant presentes et posteri, quod ego Hugo de
Gomnaco, filius  Girardi, et simul  mecum Hugo  de  Gornaco, filius  meus, con-
cedimus monasterio Beate  Marie  de Becco, et  hoc sigillorum nostrorum
munimine in perpetuam possessionem confirmemus omnia illa quae antecessores
nostri, id  est Hugo  de Gornaco, et  Basilia, uxor ejus, et Girardus eorum filius
dederunt eidem monasterio, videlicet totum quod habebant in Longolis, &c.

## p. 746 ##

Ainsi Hugues IV. : qui des I'an 1112 avoit ratifié¢ les donations faites au Bec par
son pére Girard, et qui par consequent ne pouvoit pas avoir alors moins de 20
ans, aura apparemment vecu environ 90 ans.
Paris MS. histoire de Seigneurs de Gournay.
No. 39.—CHARTERS  oF   HucH  DE   GOURNAY  IV. (page 92).
The two following charters by Hugh de Gournay, mentioning Beatrix his first
wife, and their son Hugh, are in the Bibliotheque Imperiale at Paris.—Collection
Baluze, Vol. 46, pages 8, 9, and 10.
Carta Hugonis Gornascensis domini, de renta manselli apud Caufiniacum.
Quoniam vita mortalium labilis, eorumque actio variabilis cito  antiquatur et
senescit,nisi memorizelitterarum studeat traditum iri,inter cetera donaque ecclesie
Sancti Lupia  fidelibus Christi largiuntur, Ego Gornascensis dominus, et dilectus
Hugo filius meus eidem ecclesie, tam pro remedio anime nostra, quam prede-
cessorum nostrorum salute, et maxime Beatricis venerabilis uxoris mee, concedi-
mus in elemosynam,  omnem rentam manselli apud  Caufiniacum  siti, solute  et
libere cum omni consuetudine ad se pertinenti, et ne hoc donum per novos suc-
cessores et imperitos relatores aliquando videatur annullari, sigilli mei impressione
volo confirmari.—
Carta domini Hugonis Gornascensis, de masura Rogeri presbiteri apud Cal-
finiacum.
~               Quoniam vita mortalium labilis, eorumque actio  variabilis  cito  antiquatur et
senescit, nisi memorize literarum studeat traditum iri.  Inter cetera dona que  ec-
clesie sancti Lupi de Ascerento a fidelibus Christi largiuntur, Ego  Hugo Gor-
nascensis dominus, et dilectus Hugo filius meus, eidem ecclesie, tam pro remedio
anime nostre, quam predecessorum nostrorum salute, et maxime Beatricis venera-
bilis uxoris mee, concedimus in elemosynam, masuram Rogeri presbiteri, et ejus
sororis, apud Caufiniacum sitam, solute et libere cum omni consuetudine ad ipsam
pertinente.   Et  ne  hoc donum per  novos successores et imperitos relatores ali-
quando videatur annullari, sigilli mei impressione confirmo et roboro;  videntibus
testibus quorum nomina pro  testimonio placuit annotari.  Willelmus de Agia,
Willelmus Pratellus, Willelmus filius Seoldi, Radulfus  de Agia capellanus, Ar-
nulfus qui hanc cartam sigillo impressit.

## p. 747 ##

By the  term “venerabilis” applied to Beatrix, I  am disposed to think she had
embraced the monastic life, or was not  living when these charters were given.
No. 40.—PorriONs OF  THE  DomAIN OF  Boves (page 93).
Out of these Hugh de Gournay V. made a donation to the Abbey of Bucelli,
near Laon, in 1205.
No. 41.—EsToUTEVILLE   (page 94).
There were two Estoutevilles in Normandy:  Estouteville  sur Cailly, and Es-
touteville sur-me;r   this latter was the one erected into a duchy in 1534.    Possi-
bly the Kimberley Stutevilles may have belonged to the former ;—hence the two
branches.
No. 42—HueH DE   GOURNAY  IV. (page 96).
The following is from the MS. Histoire des Seigneurs de Gournay :—
Au reste Hugues IV. augmenta beaucoup par ses belles actions la gloire de sa
famille, dans laquelle il semblait que la valeur et le courage fussent hereditaires ;
et la part qu'il eut aux affaires publiques, et aux guerres de son temps contribua
encore plus que ses illustres alliances a transmettre son nom a la posterité.  Mais
ce genereux seigneur ne se livra pas tellement au tumulte de la guerre, et a
I'embarras des affaires quil  ne pensa au bien et a I'avantage de ses sujets.
L'amour quil avait apparement pour les lettres, le porta a entretenir dans sa ville
de Gournay des Maitres habiles, pour instruire les habitants,  et pour les former
dans la connaissance des sciences, et dans la  literature;  c'est ce qui paroit par
une lettre qui lui ecrivit Raoul seigneur de Clermont, celui apparement, qui fut
depuis Connetable de France envers I'an 1170.   Du Chesne nous a conservé cette
lettre, dont voici la traduction :—
“ Raoul par la grace de Dieu compte de Clermont, a Hugues Seigneur de Gour-
nay a qui il souhaite qu'il choisisse avec la  simplicite de la colombe ce quil y a
de meilleur, sachant que l'ardeur de l'amitié rend presque possibles les choses
memes impossibles, que l'avantage que l'on doit  retirer de la possibilité des
choses est d’executer ce qu'on se demande les uns aux autres,  je n’ ai pas hesité
a vous addresser cette lettre, a vous, et a vos ecclesiastiques. J’ ai a Clermont un

## p. 748 ##

Maitre  qui  y  a  enseigné pendant cette  année qui est encore actuellement em-
ploié a conduire les ecoles publiques.  Cette personne exciteé par la regulation
de votre ville, et par le desir de meriter votre amitié souhaite que je lui fasse ob-
tenir ce qui fait 'objet de ses veeux. Elle est tres versée dans la connaissance des
auteurs, et dans la science de la philosophie, et des differents arts, tres consommeé
dans la maniere d’enseigner la grammaire, assez instruite des Saintes Ecritures, et
ce quil y a de principal, elle est d'une conduite tres grave et tres reglée.  C'est
pourquoi  je vous prie de lui accorder, I' année prochaine, la conduite et I'instruc-
tion de votre ville, tout obstacle leve et c'est ce que  je vous demande, instament
a vous, et a votre clerge.”
I1 est visible par cette lettre que les sciences et les belles lettres etaient cultiviés
a Gournay sous les auspices de Hugues; car Raoul ne se seroit point avisé de
recommander a ce seigneur un maitre dont il connoissoit I'experience et I'habilité
8'il  n y avoit eu deja a Gournay des écolles formés pour l'instruction public.
The  above very  curious document proves that  there was more love of
letters amongst the great nobles of the middle ages than is generally supposed.
No.  43.—GranT or  HugH  DE  GoUrNAY  V.  (page 110).
In 1187 il donna a I’Abbaye des Bernardius de Beaubec la terre et seigneurie
de 1a Roziere avec le droit  de haute justice qui y etoit attaché.   C’est apparem-
ment la paroisse dont Girard son aieul avoit donné le patronage a  I’Abbaye
du Bec, comme on le peut voir cy-dessus.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay.)

Gournay IV., in consequence of his journey to the Holy Land.   It is from Pope

“ Veterum scriptorum et monumentorum historicorum, dogmaticorum, mora-
lium, Amplissima Collectio.”
Tomus II. opera dom. Edmundi Martene et dom.  Ursini Durand.
fol. Paris, 1724.    Col. 635-636.
Epistola XI. Hugoni Rothomagensi Archiepiscopo.
Eugenius episcopus, servus servorum Dei, venerabili fratri Hugoni Rothoma-
gensi Archiepiscopo, salutem et Apostolicam benedictionem.
Venerabilis fratris nostri Henrici  Belvacensis episcopi relatio nos instruxit,
quod  Hugo   de  Gornaco,  parochianus  tuus,  cuidam  civi  burgensi, Erquerico
Nomine, LXXII  libras Belvacenses et duas marcas argenti ab eo, preestito jura-
mento accipiens, mentita fide reddere contradicit.   Quo circa per presentia
scripta fraternitati  tue  mandamus, quatinus si dicta ejus super hoc veritate ni-
tantur, preedictum Hugonem districte commoneas, ut  ipsam pecuniam preefato
Erquerico sine contradictione restituat.   Si  vero ammonitioni  tuz  obedire neg-
lexerit, ecclesiastica cum districtione ita cohibeas, ut non debeat exinde ad aures
nostras pro justiciee defectu querela preferri.

No. 49.—SIEGE OF   ACRE   (page 129).
Richardrangeal’armée chrétienne enbataille, etlapartageaencinqcorps; Hugues

No. 50.—HueH DE   GOURNAY  V.  OBTAINED  INDULGENCES (page 130).
Celestin III. by his bull granted 40  days indulgence for these visitors at the

de Gournay also brought with him some hair of St. Mary Magdalen.

No. 51.—CHARTER  OF  HuGcH DE   GOURNAY  V.  (page 130).

for a daily mass, at the altar of the Holy Cross in the Church of St. Hildever;t
taken from Paris MS. Histoire des Seigneurs de Gournay.                              |
Omnibus hominibus suis, Hugo de Gornaio salutem.  Noverint omnes ad
quos praesens scriptum pervenerit, quod ego Hugo de Gornaio  dedi et  concessi
in perpetuam eleemosynam Deo, et Sancte Maria, et Ricardo presbytero de Feri-

ille  qui  preedictam possidebit  eleemosynam, singulis  diebus ad altare  Sanctz

lanus, Hugo Rex,magister Rogerius, Johannes Camerarius,Golterius filius Seoldi,

No. 52.—LiecaT or  THE   HoLy   Cross  (page 130).
It is singular that in Harpley church was the light of the Holy Cross ; whether
founded by the Gournays does not appear.  Blomefield, vol. viii, p. 458.
No. 53.—HueH DE   GoUrNAY   V.  A  MEDIATOR  (page 130).
Pendant que Richard etoit encore en terre sainte, il s’ eleva de grands troubles
en Angleterre au sujet de Guillaume evéque d’ Ely chancelier du Royaume, au-
quel Richard en partant avoit confié la regence de I’ Angleterre.   Ce prelat tur-
bulent  avoit par son mauvais gouvernement irrité  contre lui  presque tous les
Grands du Royaume, et les choses allerent si loin  que Jean Comte de Mor-
tainge, frére du Roy,  et leur mére Eleonore  le chasserent comme un  perturba-
teur.    Richard en etant informé dans la Palestine envoia avant son départ quelque
seigneurs en Angleterre, dont les principaux etoient, Hugues de Gournay, Guil-
laume de Passy, et Dreux de Troubleville, auxquels il  donna tous les pouvoirs
necessaires pour pacifier ces troubles.
Ces deputés en revenant de  la terre sainte passerent 3  Rome,  ou ils trouve-
rent les ecclesiastiques que Gautier archevéque de Rouen y avoit envoié pour y
porter ses plaintes contre I'évéque d’ Ely, et confererent avec eux sur le sujet de
ces divisions.
Apparement que Hugues de Gournay qui  etoit chargé des ordres du Roy, ne
manqua pas de conferer aussi avec le Pape Celestin III., qui etoit fort porte pour
I'Evéque d’Ely;  et ce fut en cette occasion que le Pape,  qui voulut  marquer &
Hugues l'estime qu'il avoit pour lui, lui donna une bulle par laquelle il accordoit
des indulgences a ceux qui, au  jour de 1'exaltation de la Sainte Croix, visiteroient
avec piété dans 'eglise collegiale de Gournay, les reliques des Saints, et le mor-
ceau de la vraye  croix, que Hugues, apportoit de  la terre  sainte, et qu'il placa,
l'année suivante, dans sa collegiale. On conserve encore au chapitre de Gournay

## p. 753 ##

la bulle de ce Pape; voila tout ce que nous scavons du  veiage de Hugues de
Gournay, qui, quoique honoré de la confiance de Richard, ne laissa pas, 'année
suivante, d’'abandonner ses interéts pour se  jetter du coté de la France.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay.)
No. 54.—PEACE  BETWEEN  PHILIP   AND   RicHARD (page 131).
Les Nouveaux allies se donnerent mutuellement des otages pour la sureté de
leur parole, et confirmerent de part, et d’autre leur ligue par le serment de quelques
seigneurs, nous trouvons que Hugues de Gournay fut un de ceux qui se donnerait
pour caution de la parole de Richard, ce qui ne laisse aucun lieu douter de ce que
nous avons dit plus haut, que Hugues etoit rentré par la paix de Louviers dans
le parti d’Angleterre; on a conservé ce traité d’alliance avec la signature de Gour-
nay dans les archives de la tour de Londres, dou RimerI'a inseré dans sa grande
collection des traités ou chartes des Roys d’Angleterre.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay.)
No.  55.—TREATY  BETWEEN  PHILIP   AND   RicHARD (page 131).
“ Un nouveau traité fut donc conclu, les bases en furent plus larges, car il de-
voit etre definitif.  L’on en trouve encore I'original au tresor de Chartres, 1196.”
Capefigue’s Histoire de Philippe Auguste, Vol. II., page 66.
Richard cede a Philippe les mouvances des fiefs que Hugues de Gournay, dit
le Coucon, tient en Normandie,  & moins qu'il ne prefére rendre ’homage au roi
d’Angleterre, comme duc de Normandie.   Hugues de Gournay cede tous ses fiefs
d’Angleterre a Richard de Vernon, vassal du roi anglais, qui lui donne a son tour
Vernon, sous  'hommage au  roi de  France.     Richard  rend 4  Philippe,  Neuf-
marché, Gaillon, Nonancourt avec leurs chatellenies.    On mettra des bornes pour
separer d'une maniere distincte les possessions de France et d’Angleterrre.—Le
roi  de France cede 4 Richard, &c.—il  aura encore la propriété des villes et
chateaux d’Argues, Driancourt, les arriere-feifs des feudataires de Hugues de
Gournay qui lui sont demeurés fideles, Beauvais, et ses dependances.

## p. 754 ##

No. 56.—HueH DE   GoOuUmNAY V.  (page 131).
Hugh de Gornay was a witness to a Charter of Richard I. datedthe ninth year
of his reign (1197), from Lions, 14th May.
Bibliotheque Imperiale, page 829 of the MS.des Blancsmanteaux;
coté No. 36.
Hugh de Gournay, and his nephew (nepos) Robert de Bova, gave the chapel
of St. Beuve to the priory of St. Laurent en Lions, in 1200.    This chapel was
in the hamlet of Normanville in Montigny.   From the Archives of  St. Laurent
quoted in “ Histoire de haute Normandie,” Vol. L., p. 590.
Who Robert de Bova was, I  know not; perhaps of the younger branch of the
Gournays, who were Lords of Montigny.   Nepos often means cousin german.
No. 57.—CHARTER  OF  HucH  DE   GourNay  V.  (page 132).
By a charter without date,Hugh de Gournay confirmed a donation ofhis liege-
man Hugh de Avednis, to the Abbey of Mortemer, as follows :—
In nomine sancte et individue Trinitatis, ego Hugo de Gornaco,universis sancte
ecclesie fidelibus, tam presentibus quam futuris, in perpetuum.  Notum sit vobis
me concessisse Deo, et ecclesie Sancte Marie de Mortuomari, terram illam quam
apud Boscum Hugonis in elemosinam perpetuam predicte donavit ecclesie Hugo
de Avednis, homo  meus.   Quod  ut  in  perpetuum ratum  permaneat, presentis
carte  testimonio et  sigilli mei        *       *       e confirmo.   Testes: Magister
Walterus, Godefridus clericus, Hugo de Advesnis Senior, Wilhelmus filius Ste-
phani, qui XX  solidos inde accepit qui mihi dati sunt pro concessione.
Original sceau perdu ; aux Archives de I'Eure, fonds de Mortemer.
No. 58.—HuaeH DE   GOURNAY  V. (page 132).
Hugh de Gournay occurs also as a witness to a Charter of  Richard I. to  the
Abbey of Mortemer, dated at St. George de Boucherville, 16  May, 9th  year of
his reign (1198).
Chartul. de Mortuomari, p. 14.   MS. Latin, No. 2735.  Biblio:

No. 59.—ArcHBISHOP   HUBERT  (page 133.)
Hubert Walter Archbishop of  Canterbury from  1193  to  1207  was born at
West Dereham in Norfolk, and founded the Abbey there. He was prime minister
to Richard I. and John;  and raised the money to purchase the freedom of the
former from captivity, when in the hands of the Duke of Austria.
No. 60.—CHURCHES  AT   CAISTOR  (page 134).
Il paroit, il est vray, par une charte d’Alexandre de Castre, et de son fils Robert,
que ce fut Alexandre qui donna I'eglise de Saint André, au chapitre de Saint
Hildevert  avec toutes les  appurtenances, et  tous les  droits qu'il  pouvoit  avoir
dans les autres eglises de cette ville, apparement qu'il le fit a la pri¢re de Hugues
de Gournay dont il relevoit, ce que Hugues pour I'engager, le  dedommagea
d’ailleurs ; quoiqu’il en soit, il est certain que Hugues avoit droit sur le patronage
de ces eglises, et qu’il le donna a celle de Gournay.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay.)
No. 61.—MATHIEU   DE   GOURNAY  (pages 134 and  301).
On trouve mention d'un Mathieu de  Gournay dans une charte de 'an  1198
on environ, par laquelle Alexandre seigneur de Castre en Angleterre, et vassal de
Hugues de Gournay a donné au chapitre de Saint Hildevert de Gournay, le pa-
tronage de l'eglise de Saint  André de Castre.   Ce  Mathieu de  Gournay est au
nombre des temoins de cette donation.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gourna;y  in the list of Collaterals.)
Here Matthew de Gournay of the Norfolk line witnesses a deed of a vassal of
Hugh de Gournay.
No. 62.—DEED oF  SURETY oF  HucH  DE  GoURNAY V.  (page 134):
Ego Hugo de Gornaco notum facio universis ad quos presens scriptum per-
venerit, quod ego de mandato domini mei Johannis illustris regis Anglie,  juravi
super sanctas reliquias, quod si ipse Philippo,  illustri regi  Francie, non  teneret
pacem, sicut carti sud,  quam idem rex Francie habet,  testatur, infra diem com-
petentem postquam exinde summonitus fuero, continebo me super hiis erga dic-
tum regem Francie secundum tenorem carte super illam pacem facte a domino
rege  Anglie.     Quod  ut  firmum  sit et  stabile, sigillo  meo confirmo.     Actum

No. 63.—CHARTER  oF  HuGH DE   GouRrNAY V.  (page 135).
In 1202,  Hugh de Gournay  granted  the  following  charter  to the Abbey of
Fescamp, in which he mentions his wife Juliana, and his children.
Omnibus ad quos presens scriptum  pervenerit,  Hugo de Gornaco,  salutem.
Sciatis quod ego pro salute anime mee, Juliane uxoris mee, et puerorum meorum,
et heredum, dedi Deo et ecclesie Sancte Trinitatis de Fescanno, et in perpetuam
elemosinam concessi, et  presenti  scripto  confirmavi  dimidium  feodum militis
quod Rogerus Kesnel de Winemersvilla tenet, ita quod in prefato feodo nihil
retineo, nec heres meus, preter orationum  beneficia et anniversarium meum,  et
heredum meorum, et propter  hanc  elemosinam liberam  et quietam receperunt
me in fratrem et  participem  beneficiorum suorum me  constituerunt.    Quod ut
ratum sit et inconcussum, sigilli  mei  impressione corroboravi.   Testibus: Hu-
gone de Agia,  Thebaldo de Hordenc,  et multis  aliis.    Actum anno M° ducen-
tesimo secundo.
Chartu:l  Fiscanni, fo. LIII.  v* & plus  completement fo. LX.  v*
MS. du XIII® siécle, recemment acquis par la  Bibliotheque de
Rouen.
No. 64.—CHARTER OF  HucH DE   GOURNAY (page 135).
The following charter is from the Chartulary of the Monastery of La Trappe,

## p. 757 ##

in the bishoprick of  Seez, and county of La Perche, bordering on Normandy.
It is deposited in  the  Bibliotheque  Imperiale  (du  roi)  at  Paris.   Fonds des
Cartu:l No. 205 :—piece cotée E. II.
This convent of La Trappe was founded in 1140, and Robert Count de Dreux
was one of the benefactors.  He married Rolande de Coucy.
Hugh de Gournay’s charter is without date, as follows, and respects his terri-
tory of Ecouché.—Neustria Pia p. 789.
Universis ecclesie fidelibus ad quos presentes littere pervenerint, Hugo de
Gornaco, salutem.—Noverit universitas fidelium, quod ego Hugo de Gornaco
donavi in perpetuam elemosinam, monachis Sancte Marie de Trappa, pro salute
anime mee, et antecessorum meorum sexaginta solidos Andegavensium, in prefec-
tura mea de Ecocheio, ad festum Sancti Andriz, quicumque eam teneat, capiendos
ad  emendum allectium*  tria  milia  ad  opus  quadragesime;  et  nisi  nuncius
monachorum ea die, vel in crastino quo illuc venerit, dictam elemosinam habuerit,
nuncius ille cum  equo et famulo ad expensas prefecti  plenarie erit.   Et ut hoc
ratum in perpetuum permaneat, &c.
Whether the grantor of  this charter is Hugh de  Gournay IV.  or  V. I  cannot
determine, as there is no date, or witness given to fix the date;  but it was pro-
bably the latter.    The charter proves that the lordship of Escouché was possessed
by the Lords of Gournay, perhaps up to the loss of Normandy by King John.
No. 65.—ExXTRACTS  FROM   THE   LIBERATE  RoLLs  (page 137).
Rotuli de Liberate.  5 John (1203).
Rex, &c.—Gilberto filio  Petri, &c.—Sciatis quod reddidimus Osberto de
Roveray, per judicium curie nostre, Pilardinton  et Stoke sicut jus  et heriditatem
suam, quas Hugo de Gornaco tenuit de feodo Gilberti Waseville :  et immo vobis
mandamus  quod eas illi  habere faciatis.    Teste Willelmo  de Breosa  apud  Mo-
linell. XVI. die maii—per eundem.
Duffus Hardy, p. 34.
Terra data}   Rex, &c.    Gilberto filio Petri,  &c.—Mandamus vobis quod sine
dilatione habere faciatis dilecto et fideli nostro Hugoni  de Harsey, Pilardinton,
quam Hugo  de Gornaco tenuit, et insuper Kingeston, quam dedimus eidem
————
* Allec or hallec is a herring.

## p. 758 ##

Hugoni de Harsi in excambium de Pilardinton:   volumus enim quod utramque
habeat. Teste Willelmo de Albeny, apud Molinell.  XIX. die maii—per eundem.
This was no doubt Pillerton-Hersey in Warwickshire, which was one of Hugh
de Gournay’s forfeited lands ;  but  this was afterwards restored to him per Rot.
Claus. 7 R Johannis :—M. IL.
Rex Vicecomiti Warwick, &c.—Precepimus tibi  quod sine dilatione habere
facias Hugoni de Hersey  terram de Kingston, quam prius habuit in escambium
terre de Pilardinton, quam  reddimus  Hugoni de  Gornaco.   Ita  tamen quod si
predictus Hugo de Hersey, terram illam de Pilardinton versus prefatum Hugonem
de Gornaco, vel per amorem vel per placitum recuperare possit, predictam terram
de Kinton in manum nostram reddat.   Teste eodem.   7 Martii.
Dugdale MSS., Ashmolean MSS.
No. 66.—CasTLE oF  LA  Haute  RocHE (page 137).
It appears by M. Guillemet’s * Notice sur diverses localités du  departement
de I'Eure”, page 63, that previous to  the building of  Chateau Gaillard by Rich-
ard I., a fortress or  manoir called Haute  Roche, had been built in 965, by one
Robert de Tournan, on the height of  the rock.   This fortress of  Haute Roche
subsisted some time after the  Chateau Gaillard was built, and was given by
King John to Hugh de Gournay, who made a gift of  it to the Abbey of Bec,
which society built a chapel on the site, dedicated to St. Nicholas.
It seems likely that it was the Castle of La Haute Roche which Hugh de Gour-
nay traitorously delivered up.—The Charter of donation of this manoir has been
lately found at Evreux,  in a fragment of  the Chartulary  of Bec,  and has been
sent to me by M. Leopold Delisle.   The date 965 is erroneous.
Notum sit omnibus Christi fidelibus quod ego Hugo de Gornaio, pro salute
mea  et  omnium  antecessorum meorum  et   heredum,  dedi  Deo,  et  Sancte
Marie, et  Monachis Becci in  liberam quietam et  perpetuam elemosinam, do-
mum unam apud Rupem de Andelio sitam, quam dominus meus Johannes, rex
Anglize michi et heredibus meis dedit, videlicet domum illam quam dominus
Robertus de  Torneham, tempore Regis Ricardi, fecit sub castello in memorata
rupe  edificato.   Hanc  itaque domum predictis monachis dedi  et  concessi

## p. 759 ##

liberarn et  quietam de omnibus consuetudinibus et exactionibus, eo tantum-
modo  excepto quod ibi, quotiens michi necessarium fuerit sumptibus meis hos-
pitabor ibi.   Abbas autem et conventus ibi debent facere quoddam oratorium
pro   salute  mel  et  antecessorum meorum  et  heredum.     Actum  anno  gratie
millesimo ducentessimo.  Testibus Johanne de Gisorcio et multis aliis.
Cartul:  du Bec,  fol. 219 v°.
Roger de Lacy was only Constable, not Earl, of Chester.
No. 67.—EXTRACTS  FRoM   THE   LIBERATE  RoLLs  (page 138).
Liberate Rolls.   5 John (1203).
Terra data}  Rex, &c.    Gilberto filio  Petri,  &c.—Mandamus vobis quod
faciatis habere Radulfo de Tilleio saisinam manerii de Wandovre cum omnibus
pertinentibus  suis,  in  bladis  et  in   omnibus  aliis   rebus  integre,  sicut  Hugo
de Gornaco illud tenuit quando recessit a  servicio nostro.—Teste G. de Bosco
apud Roth. XXX  die Januarii—per eundem.
Duffus Hardy, p. 45.
This order seems not to have been effective, and was followed by a second, in
which Hugh de Gournay is called a traitor.
Terra data} Mandatum est Gilberto filio Petri quod faciat habere Radulfo de
Tilleio Wandovre cum pertinentibus, quam (Rex) ei dedit, adeo integre ut Hugo
de Gornaco, proditor ejus, eam tenuit, die quo a suo recessit servicio, sicut alias
ei mandatum fuit:  et  dominus Rex  non vult quod  Walterus Pipardus aliquid
habeat in predicto Manerio, quia ipsi alibi providebit pro parte sua.
Duffus Hardy, p. 74.
No. 68.—CHARTER oF  HucH  DE   GoOURNAY   V.  (page 139).
It appears that Hugh  de Gournay retained the fiefs in the neighbourhood of
Laon, which he had doubtless received from his mother, Millicent de Coucy.—
The  following  charter, in  favour of the  Abbey of Bucelli in  that district, is in
the Bibliotheque imperiale, at Paris :—
Ego, Hugo, dominus de  Gornai, notum facio presentibus et futuris, quod as-
sensu uxoris mee et liberorum  meorum, pro anima patris mei et matris mee, et

## p. 760 ##

pro anima mea et uxoris mee et liberorum meorum, dedi in elemosinam, ecclesie
beati Petri Bucelliensis* quinque modios vini albi, et viginti solidos Laudunensis
monete, qui  de censu a redditibus meis apud Noviant} singulis annis, in festo
sancti Remigii, a me et ab heredibus meis predicte ecclesie in perpetuum persol-
ventur.  Dominus autem Ingerrannus,} a quo predictos redditus in festo tene-
bam,  elemosinam dicte  ecclesie factam  approbavit.     Sciendum autem quod
anniversarium predictorum  in octavis Beati  Dionysii singulis annis in ecclesie
Bucelliensi communiter fiet, et vinum predictum et nummi, ipsa die in refectione
conventus, expendentur.   Quod ut ratum permaneat, presenti scripto annotari et
sigilli nostri et prefati domini Ingelranni appensione fecimus confirmari.  Actum
anno Domini M°. CC®. quinto, mense Decembri.
Cartu:l  ecclesizz Bucelliensis, fol. xxx. v°. & xxxi1, v>.—MS. du
xIv®. siecle, a la  Biblio: imp.  a Paris, fonds des Cartulaires
Ne. 201.
This charter proves that Hugh de Gournay retained some of his foreign pos-
sessions after the loss of Normandy.

74.—HuecH DE   GoOURNAY   V.  (p. 141).
Nous avons deja dit que Hugues de Gournay survient plus de 9 ou 10 ans a la
reduction de la  Normandie;  en effet on trouve dans le  chartrier d’Angleterre,
compilé par ordre de la reine Anne; une lettre ou manifeste du roy Jean dattée
de I'an  1212  dans laquelle ce prince,  qui  etoit  mécontent de Guillaume de
Brieuse, et qui l'avoit fait mourir de faim dans la prison de Windesor, marquoit
les sujets de mécontentement qu'il avoit eu contre lui;  ce que quelques temoins,
et  entre autres Hugues  de Gournay certifient  étre veritable, par  apposition de
leur sceau.
(MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay).

76.—CHARTER  oF   GIRARD DE   FORNIVALLE  (page 141).
The following charter appears to be of about the date 1214, and probably was
witnessed by Hugh de Gournay during his stay in Poitou.
Omnibus ad quos presens scriptum pervenerit, Girardus de Fornivalle salutem.
Sciatis me dedisse et concessisse et hoc presenti meo scripto confirmasse sancti-
monialibus de Fonte Everaldi C. solidos pro uno annitario (sic) faciendo pro
animabus patris et matris mee, et dominorum et antecessorum meorum;  et illos
in meo censu Belleville eisdem assigno, et sic quod quolibet anno in eodem censu
x libras de denariis primo redditis ad festum omnium sanctorum recipient.  Tes-
tibus hiis, domino Rege, Hugo de Gornaio, Gerardo   juveni jus filio, Girardo ejus
nepote, Simone de Campo Remi, Eustacio suo fratre, Alexandro de Melcle  et
pluribus aliis.
Chartu:l Fontis Ebraldi, t. i. p. 202. MS., lat. No.  5480 de la
Biblio:  imperiale, & Paris.

Quare— Whether nepos is here to be taken for nephew, or cousin-german.
No. 79.—HvucH DE   BovEs (page 142).
During King John’s wars with his barons, he sought the aid of Sir Hugh de
Boves, a French knight of  some renown.  The knight assembled a formidable
body of  men,  who, designing to expel the inhabitants of Norfolk  and Suffolk
from their possessions, brought their  wives and children with them ; but  the
whole perished on the coast of Suffolk, in a violent  storm; innumerable bodies
were washed ashore at  Yarmouth,—Roger of Wendover says,  a vast number of
infants in their cradles.
Manship’s Yarmouth, p. 329.

No. 94.—MANOR oF  MAPLEDURHAM  (page 187).
That Matilda, widow of  Hugh de Gournay held the Manor of  Mapledurham in
dower, appears by the following entry in the Fine Rolls (Vol. I., p. 328, as pub-
lishedby the Record Commissioners), wherein command was given that it should
be discussed before the Barons of the Exchequer, whether the Manor of Maple-
durham was answerable to the King for the  payment of £230, and half a mark,
and two casks of wine, which were owing to the King by Hugh de Gurnay.
Mandatum est Vicecomiti Oxoniz quod demandam ducentum et triginta libra-
rum, dimidii marcce, et  doliorum vini que per summonitionem Scaccarii Regis

exigit de manerio de Mapulderham, quod Matilda  quee fuit uxor  Hugonis de

spectum usque in XV. dies a die  Sancti Michaelis  ut  tunc discutiatur  coram
Barones de Scaccario utram debat inde respondere vel non.—Teste Rege apud
Windlesore XX VII. die Augusti.  (23 Hen. III. 1239).

No. 96.—THE MANOR oF   BERLING  IN  Sussex (page 187).
The Manor of Berling with East Dean, in the rape of Pevensey, in Sussex,
wasone of the most important parcels of the barony of  Gournay, inherited by
the Bardolfs from them.  On the Roll of Charters of the 30thHenry III. (1264),
at the time of the Court being in Gascony, is the following charter, mentioning
this manor :(—
Pro Willelmo Bardolf.  Rex archiepiscopis, &c.—Sciatis nos concessisse et hac
carta nostra confirmasse Willelmo Bardolf   juniori, quod ipse et heredes sui im-

## p. 769 ##

perpertuum habeant liberam warrennam in omnibus dominicis terris suis de Phel-
lingham in   Comitatu Lincolnie, et de Cantele et Castre, in Comitatu Norffolcie,
et de Berling et  Estdene in  Comitatu Sussexie, dum  tamen terre  ille non  sint
infra metas forestarum nostrarum, ita quod nullus warrennam illam intret ad fu-
gandum in  ea, vel ad aliquid  capiendum quod ad warrennam pertinet; sine
licencia et voluntate ipsius               et heredum nostrorum, super forisfacturam
nostram decem librarum.  Quare volumus, &c.   Hiis  testibus.   Bonifacio archie-
piscopo Cantuarie tocius Anglie primato.  Petro Herefordie Episcopo.  Gui-
done de Lusignan.   Galfrido de Lusignan et Willelmo  de Valencia fratribus*
nostris, Petro de Subaudia, Johanne Maunsell, preposito Beverlaci, Radulfo
filio  Nicholai, Johanne de Grey, Roberto Waleraund, Nicholao de Sancto Mauro,
Radulpho de Batrepus, Imberto Puzeys, Willelmo Gernon et aliis.   Datum per
manum nostrum apud Burdegaliam.
XV.  die Augusti.

No.  98.—MONUMENT   OF   WILLIAM  LoRD  BARDOLF  AND  JoaN   HIS    WIFE
(page 191).
These figures are engraved in Stothard’s “ Monumental Effigies,” under the
erroneous name of  Sir  Robert Gusshill  and  his lady—as appears by  the fol-
lowing communication respecting them, in  the Gentleman's Magazine for Nov,,
1832, by Mr. Alfred John Kemp,—brother-in-law of the late Mr. Stothard.
«« Owing to the accident which prevented its lamented author from personally
superintending  his elaborate work to its close, several of the beautiful original

## p. 771 ##

drawings, which he had prepared for insertion in the series of  Effigies,remained
without any superscription indicating the persons whom they represented. I
was enabled, however, duly  to appropriate them all, save one,  which, on the in-
formation  of  an antiquarian  friend  of  Mr.  Stothard,  was lettered—* supposed
to be Sir Robert Gusshill and his lady, in Heveringham Church, Notts.’
« When I was engaged inthe compilation of the descriptive letter-press for the
« Monumental Effigies,’ on  attentive consideration  of the  above  figures, I  felt
convinced that this supposition was erroneous, and I  pointed it out  as such, al-
though I  was unable during the progress of my descriptions through the press,
to give to the monument its right appropriation.
«1 can, however, now decisively assign these figures to the persons whom they
really represent.  They are the effigies of William Phelip,  lord  Bardolph, and
Joan his wifé, who was daughter of Thomas lord Bardolf.  This Thomas lord
Bardolf was associated with Henry Earl of Northumberland, Scroop Archbishop
of York, and Thomas Mowbray Earl Marshal, in that effort against Henry IV.
which succeeded the defeat of their party at Shrewsbury field.    This second at-
tempt was frustrated by the policy of Ralph Nevill, Earl of Westmorland, who,
with Prince John of Lancaster, headed the King’s forces.  The Archbishop and
the Earl Marshal were made prisoners, and beheaded at York.
“The Earl  of Northumberland and the lord Bardolf fled into Scotland, and
thence, on information that they would be betrayed into the King’s  hands, into

## p. 772 ##

Wales.   In 1408, two years after, they made a third experiment, and after long
journeying, say our Chroniclers, came to Thirsk in  Yorkshire,  and  proclaimed
that ¢whoso  would have libertie  should take up  their  armour and followe
them, whereupon much people resorted to  them;  but  Sir  Thomas Rockley,
Sheriff of Yorkshire, with other knights of that country, went against them, and at
Bramham Moor, near to Haselwood, fought with them a great battel (14  Feb.,
1408), and slew the Earl of  Northumberland, whose head was streight waies
cut off, put on a stake, carried openly  through  the city of London,  and set on
the bridge.   The Lord  Bardolf was sore wounded and taken alive, but  died
shortly after.’ —He was quartered as  a  traitor, according  to the  barbarous cus-
tom of the time;  portions  of  his  body  were  set respectively  on the gates  of
London, York, Lynn, and Shrewsbury, his head upon the gate of Lincoln.   On
the petition of Avicia his widow, they were afterwards taken down and buried.
(Dugd. Baron. Vol. I.,  p. 683).   This Lord Bardolf is  introduced in several
scenes of the first  act of Shakspeare’s History of Henry IV., and the narrative of
his overthrow is  faithfully summed up  in  a féw lines in the 4th  scene of the
4th act :—
‘ From enemies Heaven keep your Majesty,
And when they stand against you, may-they fall,
As those that I  am come to tell you of!
The Earl of Northumberland and the lord Bardolph,
With a great power of English and of Scots,
Are by the Sheriff of Yorkshire overthrown.’
He left two daughters, Anne, married to  Sir  William Clifford, and Joan, to Sir
William Phelip.   The husbands respectively petitioned the crown for the lord-
ships  which,  but  for the  attainder  of Thomas  Lord  Bardolf, would  have  de-
scended to his daughters, their wives.  The petition was granted.  Avicia, their
mother, had brought lord  Thomas Bardolf several lordships in the county of
Suffolk;  and at one of these, Dennington, William Phelip and his lady resided.
(Gough's Sepulchral Monuments, Vol. II.,  part IIL, p. 362).   He  founded a
chantry in the church  for the benefit  of himself and  his  wife, for  the souls of
Henry IV., Henry V., and of all  the faithful defunct.  He  was chamberlain to
King Henry VI, and a Knight of the Garter; a circumstance which is marked
by the badge of that noble order attached round the greave of the left leg of his

## p. 773 ##

effigy.   The figures have not been engraved in Gough's work, (Gough ut supra)
but they are described with tolerable minuteness. The circumstance of the Gar-
ter, this description, and a view of the tomb, published in Kirby's Suffolk views,
(See Catalogue of  the Prints and Drawings in the King’s Library, British Mu-
seum, under Dennington,) have enabled me with certainty to ascribe these anony-
mous figures in ¢  Stothard’s Monumental Effigies,’ to William Phelip, lord Bar-
dolf and his lady,—a communication which may be acceptable to all those of
your readers who possess the work, and to the studious in  illustrations of Eng-
lish history in general.
« A more beautiful specimen of the military and female costume of the 15th
century, than is afforded by this monument, can hardly, I  think, be found.
“ Yours, &ec.,
“ ALFRED  JOHN  KEMP.”
This  seal of  Thomas Lord  Bardolf  attached to  a deed belonging to Revd.
C. BR.Manning, of 14th Richard II. (1390).

## p. 774 ##

Note   99.—LESs   DESCENDANS DE   LA   FAMILLE   DE   GOURNAY EN   LiGNE
COLLATERALLE (page 277).
Outre les seigneurs de Gournay dont nous avons fait I'histoire dans le chapitre
precedent, on trouve dans les actes ou monumens publics, et dans quelques his-
toriens de Normandie et d’Angleterre, plusieurs autres personnes issues de la
meme famille, et dont la posterité soit long temps perpetuée en Angleterre  de-
puis que Hugues dernier seigneur de Gournay fut obligé de s’y retirer.
Du Tillet met judicieusement la maison de Gournay aussi-bienque celles de
Mortemer, de Neubourg, de Moubray, et plusieurs autres qui  s'etoient provig-
nées en Angleterre, au nombre de ces familles qui etoient Normandes d'origine;
et remarque au sujet de leurs descendans, qui  dans le  14°. siecle ont  fait  la
guerre en France, que leur  vaillance a été attribuée aux Anglois, parce que leur
souches etoient inconnues, et qu'etant éloignées de leur  origine elles s’etoient
naturalisées en Angleterre, ainsi on ne peut aucunement douter que les Gournays
d’Angleterre ne descendent veritablement des premiers seigneurs de Gournay,

## p. 775 ##

mais la negligence des ecrivains, et la disette des actes publics, qui nous derobent
la connoissance de ces seigneurs, nous empeéchent aussi de decouvrir la suitte
complette et exacte de leur posterité.   C’est pourquoy nous sommes obligés de
remarquer seulement ce qui se trouve de leurs puinés, et des descendans de ceux-
cy, sans entreprendre de donner une génealogie formée de toute leur famille
afin cependant de garder quelque ordre nous observerons la  suitte des temps
auxquels il en est fait mention.’
Note   100.—THE   OLD    HOUSE   OF    THE   NORFOLK    COUNTRY    GENTLEMAN
(page 280).
The Rev.                               described to me the old house of his  family
at                , which I  have no doubt was a specimen of the  old  Norfolk  Hall.
The entrance was by a porch, over which was a small tier of rooms going to the
top of the building.   The porch opened at once into the Hall, which was a long
room where the family lived entirely, using no other;  on the right  hand side of
the Hall was the kitchen, which opened into it ;and on the left the parlour, the
butteries, &c., and an opening into the stair-case. There were no  bells in the
house, and  when  a servant was wanted some one rapped at  the kitchen door.
And there was an enormous wood  fire in  the hall,  on  which  sometimes half a
tree was burning.
Mr.               's grandfather lived to the age of 90, with a second wife, and a
sister who died at 93;  they dined at two o'clock, and always played two games
of  whist before, and two after dinner.   They used but little plate, and kept an
old horse with a pad for Mrs.               , she riding pillion with the old groom.
In Mr.               's recollection the parlour was never made use of but on one
occasion, and  that  was when the  Countess of  Buckinghamshire came from
Blickling to  dine with  the old  squire.  In  this parlour there was a closet in
which the best china was kept.                 |
This old Mr.                 was a man of some literary acquirement, and was
amongst the most scientific planters of trees of this period, as those at
will testify.

## p. 776 ##

Note 101.—Waxuam HaLL  (page 281).
I  was at Waxham Hall, the old seat of the Woodhouses of Waxham, in Sep-
tember, 1854.   There are considerable remains of  the ancient mansion, but it is
now a large farm house ;  and the tenant described to me the  manner in  which
old Sir Berney Brograve lived there.  He farmed his own land, and had about
a hundred workmen lodging in his house;  these all dined in the great hall  to-
gether, and Sir Berney every now and then * knocked down” a bullock for them
to live upon.   He was the last gentleman who kept a  fool.   This family of
the Woodhouses of  Waxham was altogether distinct from that of  the same
name at  Kimberley.   These particulars as to  the habits of  Sir B. Brograve
shew what the mode of  life ofNorfolk country gentlemen was formerly.
Note 102.—THE PHEASANT (page 283).
The pheasant possibly existed in England at an earlier period than that of  the
Crusades. It is mentioned amongst other game in a grant of Edward the Con-
fessor to Randulph Pepperking of the forest and hundred of Chilmer in  York-
shire, a copy of which exists at Canon Hall, the seat of Mr.  Spencer Stanhope.
It is in rhyme, and amongst the game in the forest is mentioned
“ Partridge, Feasant hen and Feasant cock.”
But I  doubt the authenticity of this rhyming grant, the language of  which is
hardly older than the time of Edward III.
See Morant’s Essex, in Malden, where this same rhyming grant also occurs.
Lye in his Saxon dictionary has “ worhana and worhen—phasianus gallus—
moor cock and moor hen "—but these are grouse or moor fowl apparently.
Note 103.—DR. Macro (page 285).
Dr. Macro was of  Caius College, Cambridge,—rector of Hockwold cum
Wilton, and minister of St. Nicholas, Yarmouth.
No. 104.—WALTER   DE   GOURNAY (page 288).
Walter  de Gournay  is proved  to be  certainly  a son of  Gerard  thus:—The

## p. 777 ##

passage from ‘  Les Olim,” given at page 293, and  the  facts stated at page 292
prove the Gurneys of Swathings to  be of the blood of  the Lords  of  Gournay,
and  that  they descend from  Gerard de Gournay;  Walter de Gournay was the
first of this line.
Hugh de Gournay IV, eldest son of Gerard, was of full  age in 1112; he was
therefore born about 1090 ;  and the younger sons of Gerard de Gournay were
therefore born between 1090 and 1104—the date of  Gerard's death.  Of  these
younger sons Walter de Gournay must have been one, as Matthew de Gournay
his grandson first occurs as witness to a deed (app. XLIX. p. 308)  of the date,
as Mr. Norris thinks, of about  1160.    So  that  there could  not  by possibility
have been more generations between him and Gerard than those authenticated
thus :—
Walter de Gournay
born between 1090 and 1104.
Living in the time of Stephen.
|
William de Gournay
born about 1120.
Living in  1167.
|
Matthew de Gournay
born about 1140.
First occurs about 1160.
Dead about  1207.
From this it is certain that  Walter  was son, and  not  grandson of Gerard  de
Gournay.     He might probably have been  named by his  father  Gerard,  after
his near relation Walter Giffard, Earl of Buckingham (see page 47) ; or perhaps
after Walter de la Ferté (see page 25).
M.  De la Mairie conjecgures that   the vill  of Bois Gautier, close by Montigny,
was named after Walter de Gournay.
Pays de Bray, vol. L., p. 92.
Note 105.—WILLIAM DE   GOURNAY (page 293).
William de Gournay was witness to a charter  given  to  the  priory  of  Notre

## p. 778 ##

Dame du Pre, at Rouen, by Henry II.,,  King  of  England  and Duke of Nor-
mandy.
De la Mairie, Histoire de Gournay, Vol. I., p. 183.
This charter is given in  the Monasticon,  Vol.   VI.,  p.   1100,  but   with   only
two of the witnesses.
This is in all probability the William  de Gournay 1st of  our Record—but
cotemporaneous with him was apparently another William de Gurney, who was
one of  three ministers  of Louis  le jeune, and ¢     preepositus Parisiensis.”—The
following rhyme was made regarding the three ministers of Louis.
“ Gautier* vendange et Buchardt grape
Et Willelmus de Gurney hape]
Lewis prent que que leur escape.”
See Walter Mapes de Nugis curiarum;
Published by the Camden Society in 1850, p. 213a.
Notice of this William de Gournay, minister of Louis le jeune, occurs in the
following abstract.—
“ Anno 1152.   Quelque tems apres Geofroy (Abbé de S. Germain) eut  une
difference contre Elienne de Macy, lequel s’etoit saisi d'un homme de corps de
I’ abbaye nommé Ingelran d’Antoni, parcequ’il faisoit un fossé proche d'un grand
chemin sans sa permission. L’abbé Geofroy regardant cette entreprise comme
une injure faite 4 sa personne, et a son abbaye, s’ en plaignit au Roy.
La cause fut plaidée devant Guillaume de Gournay, Renaud de Beaumont, et
Bauldouin de Flandres, qui y présidoient en I’ absence du Roy.—Les parties ne
pouvant pas s’ accomoder, il  fallut  la  décider par le duel.—Les champions se
battirent courageusement pendant long-tems;  celui de Saint Germain arracha
I’ eil  a son adversaire, et se  jetta  sur lui avec tant de vigueur qu'il 'obligea de
se déclarer vaincu ;  ce qui décida I’ affaire en faveur de I’ abbaye.”
Histoire de I’ Abbaye Royale de S. Germain des Prez.
Par Dom. Jaques Bouillart. Paris, 1724. fol. p. 89.
Note 106.—TENURE   BY   PARAGE  (page 299).
The “droit  de parage” was abolished throughout his  dominions by  Philip
* Gautier, camerarius.    +  Bucardus.         1Hape, a hook.

## p. 779 ##

Augustus;  the  eldest son  being the  only  one  allowed by  him  to  hold imme-
diately of the Crown.
See Sir John Stevens’s Lectures on the
History of France, Vol I., p. 315.
Note 107.—CHAPEL  AT  NORMANVILLE  (page 299).
An ancient chapel at Normanville was given, in 1200, to  the priory  of  St.
Laurent en Lions by Hugh de Gournay and his nephew Robert de Bova.
See ‘ Haute Normandie,” vol. L., p. 590.
The word “nepos”, here translated nephew, oftenmeans cousin german. Robert
de Bova may have been of the Coucy family  (page 127),  Boves having been
one of their principal fiefs.   M. De la Mairie  supposes that he may have been
one of the family of De Montigny.
The following charter in  favour of Nicholas de Montigni,  of  that  family,—
the donor's name not being given,—is taken from L'Histoire de la Maison de
Harcourt, vol. IV., p. 2190.
Carta Nicolai de Montegniaco.
Notum, &c., quod in augnmentum feodi, nos  dedimus dilecto  et  fideli nostro
Nicolao de Montegniaco,  juxta terram de Maciaco, sicut carta nostra quam su-
per hoc habet,  testatur, eidem Nicolao et heredibus ejus de uxore sua despon-
sata, damus et concedimus in  perpetuum, terram Petri de Nicole apud Leon-
villam, et apud Mesnillos, et terram Vvalleri de Philly apud Manetot, et terram
Willelmi  Crassi apud  Rokervillam  et  decem acras Bosci,  &ec., actum apud
Vallem—Rodolii, anno domini M. C. XC.
Note 108.—DEED  oF  HAMELIN  DE   WARREN  (page 301).
According to a copy of this deedin Dodsworth’s collections,BodleianLibrary,
No. 90, the witnesses are:
Testibus Petro de Hikebot, Michaele de Bois,  Roberto de Mortemer,  Rogero
de Parceli, Roberto Burgernon, Guillelme Burgernon, Simone Anglo.
No date.
From Sir Robert Hutton’s Book of Seals.
Of these, Robert de Burgenon seems the same as Robert le Bourguignon,
to  whom the manor of  Swathings was given by  Hugh  de  Gournay IV.
(page 122).

## p. 780 ##

Note 109.—MATTHEW  DE   GOURNAY  (page 302).
I have said that Matthew de Gournay probably did not long survive the year
1206, but this appears to be a mistake;  he was living  2nd Henry III  (1217),
as is shewn by the following extract :
Fines 2. Regis Henrici 111.
Norff.—Mattheus de Gurney dat Domino Regi XX.  pro habendo brevi de
attingendo, &c., &c., in comitatu Norfolcie de tenemento in Swathing in comitatu
Norfolcie.
Note 110.—SEALS OF   THE   GOURNAYS  (page 318).
In  the pedigree from the Spelman MSS., the seal of  William Gurnays III.  is
said to be “in  an ovalle,”  but in the copy  of the same pedigree in Henry  Gur-
nays’ book, it is called his ‘  seale manuelle,” which is doubtless correct.    Also,
the word ¢inamilded,”  in  the next  page, applied to  the seal of  William de
Gournay IV.,  is given as “manull” in  Gurnays’ book, by  which is  probably
meant ¢    manual."—The seal manual was a portable seal, not of large dimensions.
Note 111.—WiLLiAM DE  GoOURNAY II.  (page 323).
The name of William de Gurney occurs as witness to  William de Clifford to
the abbey of Dore, in Herefordshire.
Dodsworth’s MS. collections, Bodleian Library,
No. 42, p. 134.  Placita 4 Henr. III. (1220).
Hereford.—Irrotulata carta Willelmo de Clifford,—&ec., &c.   Deo et ecclesie
Sancte Marie de Dora, &c., &e.
Testibus Willelmo de Breus et  Willelmo  filio
suo, Willelmo de Gurnay, &c.   Fol. 138.         |
This occurrence, and his  being a witness to  the gift  of  Walter de Clifford to
the same abbey* (mentioned in the text), is remarkable, and might have arisen
from the marriage of Matilda, the widow of  Hugh de Gournay IV., with Roger
de Clifford of Bridge Solers, in Herefordshire.—See page 187.
* Dugdale Mon., vol. V., p. 555.

## p. 781 ##

‘Note112.—JoHN  DE  BoLEMER VERSUS  ALICE DE  BALESHAM (pages 328—332).
John de Bolemer, a partizan of  the King’s,  seizes the stock (averia) and
chattels on the manor of  South Wootton, before the proclamation of  peace;
which manor belonged to John de Gurney, in arms against the King.    Alice de
Balesham brings an action against John de Bolemer, declaring the stock, &c.,
I  have thought it worth the while to print at full  length this interesting plea,
inasmuch as the copy of it from the Abbreviatio Placitorum in Appendix LVIII.
does not give all particulars. Amongst other matters Alicia de Balesham pleads
that the stock seized by  John de Bolemer was hers, she being the  widow  of
Stephen de Balesham, John de Gurney’s tenant, to whom he had granted a writ-
ten lease of  the Manor  of South  Wootton  to farm,  for  twelve years, from  the
feast of St. Michael, 43rd. Henry III,  (Anno 1258).   This written lease (scrip-
tum) was produced in Court, and proves  the custom of  granting leases to te-
nants, in Norfolk, as early as the reign of Henry III.
Amongst the Records in  the custody of the Right Hon. the Master of  the
Rolls, pursuant to the Statute, 1 & 2 Vic., cap. 94, to wit Placita coram Rege, 49
Hen. III.    [No. 124].   (Anno. 1264).
(M.  8.)
Placita de                                       Sancti Michaelis coram Rege apud Can-
tuariam, Anno. XLIX",, incipiente quinquagesimo.
(M  13.)
Adhuc de crastino Sancti Martini et de Octa                             apud Westmo-
nasterium.—
Norff.—Johannes  de  Bolemer  attachiatus  fuit  ad  respondendum Alicie de
Balesham de placito quare post pacem domini Regis nuper in  regn
publice clamatam, ipsam bonis et catallis suis apud Wutton ad valenciam quad-
raginta et quinque librarum depredatus est, ad grave dampnum ipsius Alicie, et
contra pacem, &c.—Et unde queritur quod die dominica proxima ante festum
Sancti Michaelis hoc anno cepit averia ipsius Alicie in  villa de Wutton.   Scili-
cet tres equos precii triginta solidis, quatuor boves precii xlviij solidis, quatuor-
decim vaccas precii quinque  libris, et  xij  solidis, tres bovettos precii xx)  solidis,
octies viginti et undecim oves precii xxj marcis et quinque solidis.   Et averia illa
abduxit, et  die lune  proxima  post  festum sancti Michaelis anno predicto, cepit

## p. 782 ##

bladum suum ad valenciam xix libris et  xviij solidis, et bladum illud abduxit in
earectis [et inde fecit voluntatem suam] unde dicit  quod deterioratus* est, et
dampnum habet ad valenciam centum librarum, et inde producit sectam.
Et Johannes venit et defendit vim et injuriam et quicquid est est} contra pacem
quando, &c.—et  bene defendit  quod  non  cepit  averia predicte  Alicie, nec illa
abduxit, nec aliquid de blado suo, sed revera dicit quod ipse venit ad manerium
cujusdam Johannis de Gurney in  Suth Wutton, et quia idem Johannes fuit in
conflictu de Lewes  contra  dominum  Regem et alibi post  eundem conflictum,
venit ipse ad predictum  manerium post bellum de Evesham  [ante pacem clam-
atam   .   .   .   .   |  etseisivit maneriumillud in manum suam, et catalla ibidem
inventa tanquam super inimicum  domini Regis, ea cepit.   Et  dicit quod post
bellum de Evesham, per provisionem curie domini Regis, reddidit domino Regi
seisinam ejusdem manerii, salvis sibi catallis in  eodem manerio inventis.  Et
petit judicium si de aliquibus catallis captis super inimicum domini Regis tem-
pore guerre, debeat ei respondere.  Et   Alicia per  attornatum suum  dicit quod
predictus Johannes dimisit Stephano de Balesham, quondam viro suo, manerium
de Suth Wutton cum pertinentibus ad firmam tenendum, a festo Sancti Michaelis
Anno  xliij  usque ad  finem duodecim annorum plenorum completorum per
quoddam scriptum quod profert, et  quod hoc testatur.  Et  dicit  quod idem
Stephanus ante  mortem suam  assignavit et legavit  eidem Alicie  predictum ma-
nerium tenendum usque ad finem predicti termini.   Ita  quod ipsa post mor-
tem predicti Stephani intravit predictum manerium per exsecutores ejusdem Ste-
phani et illud tenuit per tres annos ampliusan   .   .   .   .   bellum de Lewes
et [postea] predicta bona sua in eodem manerio in pace per unum annum et am-
plius, quousque predictus Johannes predicta averia et  blada sua post  pacem in
regno proclamatam [predictis die et anno] cepit  et abduxit  sicut predictum est.
Immo petit judicium de cognicione sua.
Et Johannes dicit quod die quo ipse seisivit predictum manerium de Suth-
Watton et cepit catalla in eodem manerio inventa, videlicet predictos equos,boves,
vaccas, et septies viginti  oves, et duo carectata bladi  tantum, fuerunt catalla illa
predicti Johannis  de Gurney et non predicte Alicie, et quod illa  cepit post bel-
lum de Evesham et  ante pacem proclamatam.  Ponit  se super patriam.   Et
Alicia  per  attornatum suum dicit  quod predictis die et  anno fuit  ipsa in
* i.e.  Attornatus.        t  Sic.

## p. 783 ##

seisina de predicto manerio per assignacionem exsecutorum Stephani, quondam
viri sui,  et  omnia predicta averia et  bladum, de quibus queritur,  sua pro-
pria fuerunt, et non Johannis de Gurney, et quod ita sit, petit quod inquiratur
per  patriam et Johannes similiter.   Ideo preceptum est vicecomiti quod  coram
eo, &c., in pleno comitatu venire faciat duodecem tam milites, &c., per quos, &c.,
et  qui  nunc,  &c.,  et   per   eorum,  &c.,  diligenter  inquirat  si  predicta  Alicia
fuit in seisina de predicto manerio, et de predictis catallis et blado, videlicet de
tribus equis, quatuor bovibus, quatuordecim vaccis, octies viginti [et xj.] ovibus
de precio predicto, et de predicto blado per assignacionem exsecutorum predicti
Stephani, faciendo x1 solidos quolibet anno die aniversarii sui infra predictum
terminum ; quousque predictus Johannes predictis diebus et anno, averia et blada
predicta cepit [et post pacem] sicut predicta Alicia dicit, vel si predictus Johan-
nes de Gurney tenuit predictum manerium, et predicta catalla que ipse cognos-
cit se cepisse in  eodem manerio, fuerunt ejusdem Johannis, quando ipse seisivit
predictum manerium tanquam super inimicum domini Regis, et ante pacem pro-
clamatam, sicut  predictus Johannes dicit.   Et  inquisitio,  &ec., scire facit in.
