&c., quia tam, &c.   Kt concessumesthu . . . ..  inde.  Et Rogerus de Bole-
mer manutenet                           predictum Johannem ad terminum predictum.
South Wootton was apparently not the only manor forfeited by John de Gur-
ney in consequence of his being in arms against the King—this appears by  the.
following extract :—
Placita coram Rege in Octavis sancte Trinitatis anno regni regis Henrici tercii
Rotulus 4.
Norff.—Preceptum vicecomiti quod ad terras Johannis de Gurnay que dedit
Willelmo Charles (quere ?) occasione transgressionis Regi facte per ipsum; tem-
pore turbacionis, &c.—Eidem Willelmo habere faciat seisinam, et imprisonat
resistentes. fol. 173.
Dodsworth’s MS. Collections. Bodleian Library
No. 142.
Note 113.—RoLL or  Arms (page 329).
This roll of arms, printed by Hearne, is apparently of the date of the reign of

## p. 784 ##

Henry IIL, or of the beginning of that of Edward I.    Lewellyn ap Griffith, men-
tioned in it, was killed in battle in 1277.    According to Dugdale,  in his baron-
age, the  following  persons mentioned in  this  roll  were living at  the periods
annexed:—
Geoffrey de Neville, Governor of Scarborough Castle—54 Henry III.
William de Ros—19 Edward I.
John de Monmouth—13 to 41 Henry III.
Hugh Bigod—temp. Henry III.
Aleyn de la Zouche—23 to 54 Henry III.
Geoffrey de Tregoz—40 Henry III.
William Longspé (not Earl of Salisbury)—temp. Henry III.
William Bardolf (who married Julia de Gournay)—temp. Edward I.
Ralf de Camois—died 5 Edward I.
Philip de Marmion—17 Henry III. to 20 Edward I.
-  Also John de Gurney—1245 to 1277.
Sir Harris Nicholas proves the roll of arms  published  by him to be earlier, i.e.,
between the years 1240 and 1245.—See his preface to that publication.
- I believe these rolls of arms are the earliest records of hereditary arms in Eng-
land.   At page 339, I have given a list of ancient Norfolk families who bore the
engrailed cross of various colours.  In Norwich cathedral, on the north side, be-
hind the quire, is a coped tomb,  or coffin-lid,  with an engrailed  cross upon it.
I  think this  monument is as early as the thirteenth century.   Whether the en-
grailed cross is an armorial bearingis  very doubtfu;l perhaps not;  and in such
case it  may be  very likely the tomb of one of  the Priors of Norwich ;  but if  it
is  an armorial bearing, the tomb is probably that of a Gournay, an Ufford,or an
Ingoldesthorpe, those families  having borne an  engrailed  cross for  their arms.
I  give the engraving of it in the following page.

## p. 785 ##

sable 3
Cross en-
grailed or.
It is remarkable that individuals of each of these families accompanied Prince
Edward, afterwards Edward I.,  in the last crusade, in 1270,—viz.,Sir John De

## p. 786 ##

Gournay, Sir Robert de Ufford, and Sir John de Ingoldesthorpe; and their
arms may have originated from that circumstance. The Uffords and Ingoldes-
thorpes were both families of eminence:  the former became Earls of Suffolk.
The Ingoldesthorpes had a considerable estate in Norfolk.   Thomas de Ingoldes-
thorpe, known as “ Redde Thomas,” lived about the time of  Richard I.   His
son was Sheriff of Norfolk and Suffolk in  1236.   Two branches of  this family
ended in heiresses. Ela, daughter and heir of Sir Thomas de Ingoldesthorpe,
married Sir Hugh  Jernegan, Knight,  about the year 1250;  and Isabel, the
daughter and heir  of  Sir  Edmund  de Ingoldesthorpe, married John Nevill,
Marquis Montacute, in the reign of Henry VI.   I have before stated that from
the wording of  the fine given at  p. 325, between William de Gurney II.  and
Thomas de Ingoldesthorpe, it is probable the former married Katharine, the
daughter or sister of the latter.
Note 114.—JoHN DE   GOURNAY  I.  A   CRUSADER   (page 329).
I  have mentioned that  letters of  protection  were given to  John de  Gurnay
amongst  others  who accompanied  Prince  Edward  to the  Holy  Land.   They
were as follows :—
Rex omnibus, &c., salutem.  Cum dilectus et fidelis noster  Johannes de Gur-
nay crucesignatus nobiscum, et cum Edwardo primogenito nostro profecturus sit
ad partes transmarinas in subsidium Terre Sancta, Suscepimus in protectionem
et  defensionem nostram eundem Johannem, homines,  terras, res, redditus  et
omnes possessiones suas.  Kt  ideo vobis mandamus, quod ipsum Johannem,
homines, terras, res, redditus et omnes possessiones suas manuteneatis, protegatis
et  defendatis, non inferentes eis vel inferri permittentes injuriam, molestiam,
dampnum vel gravamen. Kt si quid eis foris factum fuerit, id eis, sine dilatione,
faciatis emendari.    In cujus  rei testimonium  has litteras nostras  eidem  Johanni
fieri fecimus patentes, a festo Pasche proximo praterito per quadriennium proximo
sequentes duraturas. Volumus eciam quod idem Johannes a tempore quo iter
preedictum arripuerit, per tantum tempus, quod extunc super fuerit, ad perficien-
dum quadriennium pradictum  sit quietus  de  omnibus placitis et queerelis, ex-
ceptis placitis de dote unde nichil habet, assisa nove  disseisina@ et ultima
prasentationis; praesentibus minime  valituris,  si contingat ipsum iter preedic-

## p. 787 ##

tum non  arripere,  nec   postquam  citra  predictum  quadriennium  redierit de
partibus supradictis.  Teste rege, &ec., (1270).
The form of  this document, which I have filled up with  John de Gournay’s
name, is  given  in  a  work  called  “ Excerpta  Historica,” —published by  Mr.  °
Samuel Bentley, in 1831.  He  gives the list of  those to  whom similar letters
were given.  Probably Prince Edward undertook the Crusade partly in order
to free the kingdom from those who had been most active in the  civil wars,—of
whom John de Gurnay was one.
John de Gurnay is mentioned in the following records :—
In the Rotuli Hundredorum;, which were made 2nd Ed. 1. (1274), Vol. L p.
444, under Mitford Hundred :—
“ Dicunt quod Johannes de Gurnay  applicavit  sibi  warennam, sed nescimus
quo waranto nec quo tempore.”
And again at page 515: —
“ Dicunt quod Johannes de Gurnay clamat habere warennam in Hardingham,
nesciunt quo waranto.”
In the same Record (p. 499) this claim,  on the part  of William  de Gurnay
II1., is given:—
“ Dicunt quod Willelmus  de Gurnay clamat hebere waraniam in Harding-
ham, et nesciunt quo waranto.”
Also, at page 461 :—
« Willelmus  de  Caletorp  tenet  1 feodum militis  in   villa de Harpele cum  me-
dietate dicti feodi, quod Johannes de Gurney tenet de dicto Willelmo in  eadem
villa de Comite Warennz, qui de Rege in capite.”
And in Dodsworth’s Collections.  Bodleian Library, No. 93.   Rough Notes
from Records—p. 30°.   4 Ed. I., dorso :—
Norff.—Ricardus de Braundon
Johanni de Gurnay de tenemento in Runhall.
Note 115.—JoBN DE   GouURNAY  II. (page 342).
In the British Museum, amongst the  Additional MSS. 5522, fol.  171, is the
Norfolk feodary book;  being a book of tenures of such lands as were holden in
capite, and by knight's service in Norfolk, supposed to be made for collection of
an aid to Henry III. on marrying his sister Isabel to  the Emperor Albert  2nd.
A.D., 1235;.  but it is decidedly of a later date.
This MS. volume belonged to Blomefield, and in it is the following entry :—
“ Hundred de Mytford.
Johannes Gurney tenet manerium de Swathyng in  Hardyngham de Hugone
de Bardolf et idem de Rege.
John de Gournay occurs also in the Escheat Rolls as feoffee in the manor of
Winburgh and others.
Escaet. 7 Ed. 11. (1314).
Norff.—Jurati dicunt quod non  est ad dampnum si Rex concedat Thome
Bardolf quod ipse de maneriis de Quineborghe in  comitatu Norfolcize feoffare
possit  Johannem de Gurnay, &c.—et inter alia manerium de Stoke Bardolfe in
comitatu Nottingham, Okebroke in  comitatu Derby qua  tenentur de domino
Rege in capite per Baroniam, &c.
Dodsworth’s  MS.  Collections, Bodleian
Library, No. 57.
Note 116.—EDMUND  AND   JOHN   DE   GOURNAY (page 357).
Dans une charte de Richard II. donnée en faveur du prieuré de Walsingham
il    est fait mention d'un  Edmond de Gournay qui  avoit  donné  quelques biens a
ce prieuré et dans une autre charte du Roy Henry IV. son successeur on trouve
aussi le nom d'un  Jean  de  Gournay au  nombre  des bienfaiteurs de  ce meme
prieuré de Walsingham. La famille de Gournay subsistoit encore en Angleterre
dans le 16° siecle, car on  voit dans le  chartrier d’ Angleterre sous le regne de
Henry VIII. en 1520  une liste ou état  de  ceux  qui  avoient été commis par ce
Roy pour examiner ceux qui devoient accompagner le Roy Francois 17 a I'entre-
vue que ces deux monarques devoient avoir ensemble, et au nombre de ces com-
missaires on  trouve le  surnom d’ un  de Gournay, dont  le nom  n’est point ex-
prime.
MS. Hist:  des Seigneurs de Gournay—amongst
the collateral descendants.
See Monast. Angl. Vol. VL, p. 74, where these charters are given :—The first.

## p. 789 ##

Rex, &c.—Licentiam dedimus pro nobis et heredibus nostris quantum in no-
bis est, Stephano  de Hales chevalier,  Olivero  de Calthorp chevalier,  Radulpho
de Shelton chevalier, Willielmo de Walsham clerico, Edmundo Gurney, Willel-
mo Wynter (and others) ;  quod ipsi manerium de magna Riburgh, et manerium
de parva Riburgh, vocatum Woodhalle, cum pertinentiis in  comitatu Norff.,
dare  possint  et  assignare  prefatis  priori   et   conventui,  et  quod  idem  Ste-
phanus, Oliverus, Radulphus, &c.—concedere possint,  quod advocatio ecclesize
ejusdem manerii de magna Riburgh,  &c.—remaneat eisdem priori  et  conven-
tui, &c., &ec.
Note 117.—MAaNORS OF   SAXTHORPE  AND   FELTWELL  (page 358).
John de Mereworthe was lord of  the manor of Saxthorpe, which had been
held by John Gurnay II., as it afterwards was by John Gurnay V.
See Blomefield’s Norfolk, Vol. VI., p. 498.
This fact may explain the annuity of £20 a-year, granted him by Edmund de
Gurney, probably in consequence of some agreement between them.
The following extract from the Close Rolls relates to  one of Edmund Gur-
nay’s trusts :—
“ Norfl.—Johannes de Plays chivaler dedit Willelmo de Beauchamp, Johanni
Marmyon, Johanni  de Burgh, et Stephano de Hales,  chivalers, Edmundo Gur-
nay, &c.,  manerium suum  de  Feltwell  in  comitatu  Norfolcie,  &c.   Data  18
Januarii;  et manerium suum  de  Mundeford cum  advocatione Ecclesie ibidem,
eodem comitatu, &c.   Data 18 Januarii Anno 5 R. II. (1382).”
Dodsworth’s MS.  Collections, Bodleian Library,
No. 67.
Note 118.—THE WiLL    oF  EDMUND   GURNAY  (page 363).
Although the Register Harsyke, at  the bishop's office at Norwich, in  which
was the will of Edmund Gurnay, is almost illegible from decay, a copy of many
of the wills that were in it, exists in the British Museum,—in Harl. MSS. 10—

## p. 790 ##

fol.  144, pencil 148; being a MS. copy of these wills made in the  year 1637,
under the care of Sir  Simonds Dewes.  This codex contains many wills then in
the Bishop's office, at Norwich.   It  states that  after Register Harsyke, which
ends in  1404, there is  no Register until Register Hyrnings, which begins in
1419.
Amongst the wills copied, is that of Edmund Gurnay, which I give in extenso ;
it is the earliest will of any of the family which I  have discovered, being proved
in 1387.
Registrum Harsyke in archivo Registrarii Norwicensis.
Hec est ultima voluntas Edmundi Gurnay, facta apud West Barsham die Jovis
in festo ascensionis Domini, anno domini, Millesimo trecentesimo octagesimo sep-
timo, videlicet quod Katerina que fuit  uxor predicti Edmundi, Johannes filius
eorundem Edmundi et Katerine, Osbertus de Mundeford et Thomas Kempe ex-
ecutores testamenti ejusdem Edmundi, per bonum avisamentum ordinent et stabil-
ient diem sepulture sue, et quod omnes, tum gentiles quam alii boni homines
patrie ibidem circumstantes, ad illum diem apud West Barsham convenient, et
honorifice de bonis  dicti Edmundi pascantur, secundum ordinationem  executo-
rum  predictorum.  Et quod predicti executores contra dictum diem, vesturam
nigram pro eis, et aliis filiis et propinquis suis,* et albam vesturampro XIII pau-
peribus, ceram pro XIII torches, et duo tapres ad ardendum circa corpus dicti
Edmundi die  sepulture sue, quos quidem XIII torches predicti XIII pauperes
albo ornati circa corpus suum die predicto stando tenebunt, et quod predicti ex-
ecutores sui solvant tam predicto summo altari ecclesie de West Barsham, quam
ad emendationem ecclesie predicte, prout per eundem Idmundum in testamento
suo dispositum et ordinatum est, ac eciam quod distribuant die sepulture sue, ad
orandum pro anima sua, cuilibet pauperi homini ibidem  existenti vel  petere vo-
lenti, unum denarium.  Et quod Katerina, que fuit uxor dicti Edmundi, habeat
plenam dotem suam omnium terrarum et  tenementorum suorum, sibi per legem
contingentium, et omnia utensilia domus dicti Edmundi, et partem suam omnium
aliorum  bonorum et  catallorum  dicti Edmundi, sine contradictione vel impedi-
mento heredum vel  executorum snorum.    Et   predictus Edmundus  vult et con-
*         cedit predictis executoribus suis omnia bona et catalla sua, ac eciam omnia debita
sua in quorumcunque manibus fuerint  inventa, ad istam  voluntatem suam im-
* The word suppeditent seems wanting here.

## p. 791 ##

plendam, ac ad faciendum et distribuendum tam domibus religiosis quam fratri-
bus quatuor ordinum et aliis egenis et pauperibus prout sibi, pro anima sua, an-
tecessorum suorum  et illorum  quibus tenetur, melius  viderint  expedire—et ad
justum finem faciendum pro dicto Edmundo cum omnibus hominibus, eis con-
querentibus et rationabiliter probantibus quod aliquid eis debuit, injuste detinuit
vel per extorcionem ceperit.  Et quod heredes predicti Edmundi per assensum
et consilium executorum suorum predictorum, et secundum benedictionem suam
reddant et  satisfaciant, si aliquis bono et  rationabili titulo  eis ostendere poterit
quod idem Edmundus eos disseisierit de aliqua terra vel de aliquo tenemento,
vel de aliqua injuria eis facta per dictam Edmundum.
(Comp. Dec. 22, Anno 1638).
Probatum fuit istud testamentum apud Norwicum uni codicilld eidem annexa,
primo die Augusti Anno domini Millesimo CCC° LXXXVII™.   Et commissa
est administratio omnium bonorum dicto testamento nominatis, &ec.
(Comp. Dec. 22. Anno 1638).
The words Comp. Dec. 22.  Anno 1638, are in  the hand writing  of  Sir
Simonds Dewes.
The above is the “ ultima voluntas”, without the ¢    testamentum” I  suppose ;—
the latter instrument being referred to;  to which probably the witnesses, as given
by Blomefield, placed their names.—Vol. VIL. p. 43.
This will,  not being  in the first person, must  be considered not a  complete
copy, but a good summary of contents. I  think it likely the flag stone, at West
Barsham, engraved at page 408, was in memory of this  Edmund Gurnay; and
not of William Gurnay V.,—who was probably buried at Irstead.
Note  119.—RoBERT DE   WaUNCY   (page 364).
Robert de Wauncy was witness to two deeds of Richard Cceur de Lion to  the
Abbey  of   Stratford  Langthorne,  in  Essex,  dated at  Chateau-Gaillard,  24th
October, 10th year of his reign (anno 1198).   MS. copies of the original deeds
are in the hands of the abbey landholders.
In   Mr.   Stapleton’s Norman Exchequer roll,  page CXLVII.,  he  says:
« Amongst the disbursements by  the King’s  writ is one of  £168. 12s. for the

## p. 792 ##

raiment and expense of the daughter of the Comte of Britanny, and of the daugh-
ter of the Emperor of Cyprus, the unfortunate Isaac,* and of their  household,
and of Robert de Wancie and Walter de Ely,  who had them in their custody
and under their guidance, both whilst they were at Rouen and upon the journey
from Rouen to Chinon.—Temp. Rich. 1.”
Mr. William Wansey, of Arborfield, near Reading, has sent me what particu-
lars he possessed respecting the   Wauncys.  His own branch of the family have
been settled at Warminster, in Wiltshire, since the reign of Henry VIII.   They
bear gules three gauntlets, or gloves, argt.
NoTe  120.—ExTrAcT FROM   THE   PATENT  RoLLs (page 377).
Pat:  Henry IV., p. 2, m. 29, (anno 1406).
Rex  omnibus ad quos, &c., salutem.    Licet,  &c., de gratia tamen nostra spe-
ciali, et pro centum  marcis,  quas dilecti  nobis in Christo prior et conventus de
Walsingham nobis solverunt in hanaperio nostro, concessimus et licentiam dedi-
mus, pro nobis et haredibus  nostris,  quantum in nobis est, Johanni Gournay,
Johanni Drew persona ecclesize de Harplee, Ricardo Creyk et Johanni Lynge
seniori, quod ipsi quater viginti acras terrae cum  pertinentiis in Brunham, qua
de nobis non  tenentur,  dare possint, et  assignare preefatis priori  et  conventui.
Habendum et  tenendum sibi  et successoribus suis ad anniversaria Edmundi
de Reynham militis, et Cristiana uxoris ejus in ecclesia ipsorum prioris et con-
ventus de Walsingham, singulis annis, diebus obituum eorundem Edmundi et
Cristianze,  juxta  ordinationem  preedictorum,  Johannis,  Johannis,  Ricardi  et
Johannis, in hac parte faciendum imperpetuum, &c., &e.
Dugd. Monas:t  vol. VL, p. 74.
The Reynhams had a manor in Burnham Westgate.
See Blomefield, vol. VIL, p. 36.
John De  Gurnai was escheator for Norfolk, 2nd Henry  4th, the year after his
shrievalty, as appears in the account of the collection of the aid for marrying the
King's daughter Blanch, to the Duke  of Bavaria that same year;  according to
a copy of this aid as levied in Norfolk, in Hunstanton charter room.
* I  suppose the Emperor Isaac L’Ange is intended.—See L’art de verifier les dates, 2nd series, vol. 4, p. 313.

## p. 793 ##

Nore  121.—JoBN DE   GOURNEY  (page 378).
We have stated that the will of John de Gourney V. has not been discovered.
This is to be accounted for by  the fact that the Registers in the office of  the
Bishop of Norwich, from the year 1404 to 1419,  have long been lost, as stated
in note 118.
John de Gournay left a son and heir, as appears by the following documents :
Amongst the  Records  late  of  the  Queen's  Remembrancer,  preserved in the
branch Public Record Office,  Carlton  Ride, in  the custody of  the Right  Hon.
the Master  of  the  Rolls, pursuant to  the  statute 1 & 2 Vic., cap. 94:   to  wit,
among the Escheator’s Inquisitions it is thus contained :
Inquisitio capta apud Holt  markett in comitatu Norfolciee . . . . proxima
post festum anunciationis beate Marie Virginis    .  ..........           anno regni
regis Henrici quarti a conquestu decimo, (1408,)  coram Johanne [le Straunge)
.  .Escheatore domini Regis in dicto comitatu, virtute cujusdam brevis domini
Regis eidem Escheatori inde directi, et inquisitio consu       per sacramentum
Johannis Birston, Nichola Skomfyt, Thoma Estker, Johannis . . .......
Johannis  Gresham de  Holte,  Johannis  Holtman,  Roberti  Freman,  Johannis
Cee                                       Johannis . . ..........         quidicunt  quod Johannes Gurnay in
dicto  brevi  domini  Regis nominatus, non tenuit  aliquas terras  seu tene-
menta in dominico suo ut de feodo, nec de servicio . . . . de dicto Rege in capite,
nec de aliquo alio domino in  dicto  comitatu  .  .  .  .  obiit.   Et  idem  Johannes
Gournay obiit die Martis proxima ante festum Sancti Nichole .  .  .  .  .  ultimum
elapsum.   Et dicunt quod  Edmundus  filius  dicti  Johannis Gurnay  est heres
ejus de sanguine propinquio;r  et quod idem Edmundus fuit etatis decem anno-
urm in festo apostolorum Philippi et Jacobi ultimo elapso.                                |
This Edmund, son and heir of  John de Gournay, died sine prole, and pro-
bably under age, as is stated in a pedigree of Gurney, in Dodsworth’s collections,
Bodleian Library, vol. 108.    Sir  H.  Spelman is,  therefore, correct  in  saying
that  John de Gournay V. was succeeded by his nephew Thomas Gurnay.
A similar inquisition  was taken at   Attlebrigge, co. Norfk.,  with  the same
findings ;  as also at Rudham, with the same findings, 10th Henry IV.
By  this it appears that  John Gournay died on  5th December, 1408—but
Blomefield  states that   John Gournay,  of West  Barsham, Esq.,  died  Lord of

## p. 794 ##

Woodhall Manor, in Baconsthorpe, in 1407 :  this is a year earlier, and there-
fore erroneous.
Hist. Norfk., vol. VL,  p. 512.
John de Gournay’s being stated to possess no lands or tenements held of  the
Crown, or any other superior lord,  arises from all his lands being in the hands
of feoffees.  This was generally the case, to avoid dues payable upon the death
of the holders of fiefs.
Note  122.—MERCHANT'S  Mark  (page 381).
Mr.  W.  C. Ewing, in  the Norfolk  and Norwich  Archaeological Society's
Papers (vol. II1.,  p. 182),  gives the mark  of  John  de Heylesdon, with  the
date 1333.
Note 123.—FINE BETWEEN NicHOLAS BokiNG & THOMAS  GURNAY  (page 385).
The probability is, this Fine was with Thomas Gurney II. and Margaret Jer-
negan his wife;  and that  Thomas Gurney I. was dead before 1444, the date of
the fine.
And indeed there are several reasons for supposing that Catharine Kervile and
not Margaret Kervile was the wife of Thomas Gurney I. :—
1.  Tilston, the writer of the ancient pedigrees I  possess of the date of Charles
I1., calls the wife of Thomas Gurney, Catharine, daughter of Robert Kervile, of
Watlington.
2.  Catharine, daughter of Robert Kervile, appears in the pedigree of  Kervile
of Watlington, exactly of the time of Thomas Gurney I.

## p. 795 ##

3. Robert Kervile, of  Watlington, ob. 1434, and mentions his  daughter
Catharine in his will, but does not mention any daughter named Margaret.
4. No Margaret Kervile appears in either of the pedigrees of Kervile of Wat-
lington, or Kervile of Wiggenhall St. Mary’s, of the time of Thomas Gurney I.
Therefore my having named the wife of Thomas Gurnay I. Catharine, in the
pedigree at page  286, seems correct.   But,  nevertheless, as the  Kervilles  of
Watlington bore the arms of their  family with a difference, the fact that in  the
ancient  glass, now  at  Keswick, Gurney  impales  Kerville without  a difference
would lead to  the idea that she was of  the elder branch of  the family; and
moreover the Jernegans had no  fiefs in  Castleacre where the inheritance of
Margaret lay according to the fine, whereas the Kervilles had.
M.  Leopold Delisle, in his researches in Norman muniments, has not  met
with  anything which relates to the family of de Capravilla, de Chevreville, or
as it is generally written in England, de Kerville, or Carville.  I conclude there-

## p. 796 ##

fore that this  family was more distinguished in  England than in Normandy.
They held their lands in Wiggenhall as under-tenants to the Barons of Wirme-
gay ;  and were therefore probably retainers of  Hermerus de Ferrariis, or his
descendants the families of de Wirmegay, Warren of Wirmegay and the Bar-
dolphs,  and were enfeoffed of their manors by their  superior lords, certainly as
early as the time of Richard I.,  and probably earlier.
The pedigree of the Kervilles of Wiggenhall St. Mary’s as given at page 389,
is in some respects incorrect, and I  therefore reprint it with the alterations which
were necessary.  Lhey are chiefly taken from the visitations of 1563 and 1612,
and from the Islington Court Rolls.

## p. 708 ##

By the Court Rolls of  Islington, in Freebridge Marshland Hundred, it appears
that in 22nd James I. (1624), Mary Kerville, an infant, aged 4 vears, was ad-
mitted, as the daughter and heir of Sir Henry Kervyle, Knt., deceased, to certain
lands, (parcel of her late father’s estate in Wiggenhall,) held of the Manor of Is-
lington, by Copy of Court Roll.   Sir Henry Kervile must have, therefore, died
intestate.
And  in Ist  Charles I.  (1625),  Hamon  Bozoun,  of Whissonsett, Esq., was
admitted to these lands, as cousin and next heir  of  Mary  Kerville,  deceased,
daughter and only heir of the  said Sir Henry Kerville, Knt.,  deceased.
From this latter entry it is evident that of the children of Humphrey Kerville
and Ann Cobbe (living in 1625), Elizabeth, the wife of Robert Bozoun, alone
had issue living in 1625.
Nevertheless, on the 15th of April, 1659,  the above-named  Hamon Bozoun,
in pursuance of certain covenants contained in an indenture bearing date the
28th day of November, 12th Charles I.,  and made between himself, of the one
part, and Sir Henry Bedingfield,  of Oxburgh, Kt.,  William Sheldon, of Beoly,
Worcestershire, Esqre., Richard White, of Hutton, Essex, Esqre., William Cobb,
Esqre., son and heir of Geffery Cobb, of Sandringham, Norfolk, Esqre., deceased,
Gregory Gawsell, of Watlington, Norfolk, Esqre., Thomas Boteler, of Thetford,
Norfolk, Esqre.,  and Susan Michell,  of  Watlington aforesaid,  widow,  of  the
other part, surrendered all  the  copyhold  lands  in  Islington to which  he  had
been admitted tenant  as the  heir  of  the said Mary  Kerville  and Sir  Henry
Kervylle,  Kt., deceased, to  Susan Gawsell, spinster, niece and heir, and also
devisee of the above-mentioned Gregory Gawsell, who was then deceased, and
who was grandson of  Thomas Gawsell and Catharine Kervyle mentioned in
the foregoing pedigree.
This was either part of  a family settlement of theKervilleproperty, or a sale ofit.
The Bozouns of Whissonsett were thus the representatives of the Kervilles of
Wiggenhall St. Mary’s ;  when they became extinct the Crowes of East Bilney
were their  heirs—from whom descend the Munnings  family,—now I  believe
existing.
The church of Wiggenhall St. Mary’s contains the arms of  the Kervilles in
many parts, and of other families, in the glass and elsewhere. The monument
of Sir Henry Kerville is of marble and alabaster.

## p. 799 ##

The inscription on it is as follows :—
Hic  deponitur corpus Henrici Kervilli, equitis aurati, filii  et haeredis Hen-
rici Kervilli, armig. de Winefreda conjuge sud Antonii Thorold militis filid pro-
creati; uxorem duxit Mariam, Francisci Plowden, armig. gnatam, e qui prolem
binam, sed in  cunabulis extinctam suscepit, Gervasium scilicet et Mariam;
sororem habuit unicam, Annam, Rob° Thorold, armig.  nuptam,  sine exitu  de-
functam ;  26 Junii,  1624 obiit, et in  illo antiqui  sui  stemmatis Kervillorum
nomen.   Quam  reliquit conjux  viti, eum  sequuta est consors morte,  Martis
6  eodem anno.

## p. 800 ##

On the monument are gules a chevron or between 3 leopards’ faces arg’. Ker-
ville, impaling Plowden—azure a fess dancette in chief two fleurs de lis or.
Kerville impaling Fincham barry of six sable and argent a bend ermine.
Plowden in a lozenge shield.
Kerville impaling Lovell, of  Barton—arg'. a chevron azure between 3 squir-
rels sejant gules.
Kerville impaling Boteler or Butler, of Babingley, Norfolk—sable 3 covered
cups argent.
None of the pedigrees give these two last marriages with aLovell and a Boteler.
On the top of the monument is the crest of the Kervilles, a goat sable.  Their
bearing this  crest is  a  singular circumstance, the goat having reference to
.  their Norman origin—de  Capravilla.    The motto,    Virtus ubique una.”
At Wiggenhall St. Germans was lately found a gold ring seal of  one of the
Kervilles.   The seal part turns upon a swivel, having two sides.  The one the
arms and crest of Kerville, with a mullet  for difference; and on the reverse the
inscription, “ Knowe thyselfe” and a fetlock.

## p. 801 ##

It appears to be of  the sixteenth century; and, from the mullet  being the
difference of  a third son, probably  belonged to Edmund Kerville, third  son of
Humphrey Kerville, Esq., and Ann, daughter of Jeffrey Cobb, Esq.
On a small marble stone, at Wiggenhall St. Mary’s, is  a heart, in the centre,
of brass; and round it  four labels or  pieces of brass, each inscribed.  Two of
these are now gone; but I  have had an engraving of  this made, with  the in-
scription restored, as it existed in the time of Blomefield.
Orate p. anima dni  Roberti—Kervile militis de Wigenhale—Filii Edmundi
- Kervile de-Wigenhale, cujus cor hic humatur.
The style of this monument is of  the date of Ed. 3d.   Probably Sir Robert
Kerville might have died, or been killed abroad during  the wars of that period,
and his heart conveyed to  his native parish for interment—perhaps by his own
direction.
This Sir Robert Kerville  is said to have been the ancestor of the Kervilles of
Watlington, a junior branch of  those of  St. Mary's; but in a  rental of West
Walton manor, of the date 27th of Elizabeth (1584) late belonging to  the

## p. 802 ##

Priory ofLewes itis stated that Henry Kerville,  Esq.,  of Wiggenhall St. Mary's,
held 26 acres of land in Tylney, late Sir Robert Kerville's, knt. ;  and from the
circumstance of Henry Kerville  inheriting his  land, it  is  most probable that
Sir Robert Kerville was not the ancestor of the Kervilles of Watlington.
Robert Kerville of Watlington, who died in 1434, was the first of his family
who held the manor there.  In his will (hereafter given) he leaves his manor of
Watlington to his sonRichard—he stating that this manor had been in the family
of  De Watlington, from whom he probably acquired it by marriage or purchase;
nevertheless Walter de Kerville held lands in that parish, temp. Edward II., and
Robert de Kerville in 4th Edward III.   The pedigree of the Kervilles of Wat-
lington seems to  be as follows :—

Shortly after the Herald's Visitation of Norfolk in 1563, the Kervile family of
Watlington appear to have conveyed to  the  Gawsels their  estate there, and to
have removed themselves into the neighbouring town  of South Lynn, where,
after the death of Mr. Henry Kervile in  1605, the race gradually decayed, or
became extinct; at least none of  their representatives appeared at the Herald's
next Visitation of the county in 1612.
The arms borne by the Kervilles of Watlington are gules, 3 leopard’s faces ar-
gent, jessant fleurs de lis  sable quartering Drewe sable in a bordure argent a
griffin segreant of the first, with a crescent for the difference of a younger branch
—the crest is a cone, (by some called an eel-pot,) gules between two lion’s  jambs
argent.
These are the arms and crest which were proved and allowed, with the differ-
ence of a younger branch to Thomas Kerville  of Watlington,  at the Visitation
of  1563, but  as they do not  contain the quarterings of Haultoft  and Tilney
allowed at the  same  time to Henry Kervyle,  Esq.,  of Wiggenhall,  it  may be
inferred that  the  Watlington Kervilles branched off  from the original stock at
some period anterior to the marriages which brought in those coats.
The will of Robert Kerville  of Watlington, was made 13th Henry VI., and
proved July, 1435.  It  is as follows, taken from the office of  the Bishop of
Norwich :—Register Surflete.

acre et dimidium terre vocate monys,jacentes in Wathlyngton, et quinque acre vo-
cate Roks,  jacentes in Ilsyngton vendantur per executores et feoffatores meos pro
solucionibus predictis. Item volo quod residuum omnium bonorum meorumnon
legatorum disponatur per executores meos in  divinis celebrandis, pauperibus
relevandis, et aliis operibus caritatis, pro anima mea, et animabus quibus teneor
secundum dispositionem dictorum executorum,prout viderint melius deo placere
et anime mee prodesse, ut velint rationem reddere coram summo judice.  In cujus
rei testimonium huic ultime voluntati mee sigillum meum apposui.   Acta sunt hee
die loco et anno superius annotatis, et  anno regni Regis Henrici sexti post con-
questum Anglie terciodecimo Probatum fuit prius testamentum una cum codicillo
eidem annexo apudNorwicum,coram nobis officialiDominiNorwicensisEpiscopi,
xij die mensis Julii anno domini M° cccc® xxx’.   Et commissa est administratio
bonorum executoribus infra nominatis in formajuris juratis.
The bequest of ploughs and carts to his family by Robert Kervyle would argue
a  very primitive state of  society.  The manor having been in the family of de
Wathlington, as stated in  the will, seems to imply  that this Robert Kervyle had
either bought or inherited it from them.

## p. 807 ##

In the Charter room at Hunstanton Hall is an account book of Edmund Ker-
ville and Humfrey Bedingfield, executors of Thomas Kervylle of Wiggenhall St.
Mary's ;—these accounts contain some curious matter, and I  have thought it
worth the while to print extracts from them.

## p. 812 ##

There remains an ancient barn and other outbuildings at Wiggenhall St.Mary's,
—also the gate house of  the mansion of  the Kervilles, of which we give an
engraving.
I  have stated that the Kervilles held their fiefs under the Lords of Wormegay
as early as the reign of Richard I. ;  but by  the Liber Niger Scaccarii published
by Hearne (vol. I,  p. 288, Ed. 1774,)  it  appears that  they held  these fiefs as
early as the time of Henry IL, and probably in the time of Henry I., or earlier.
The passage is as follows :—
Carta Willelmi de Wormegai.
Henrico Regi Anglorum, Karissimo domino suo, Willelmus de Wormegai
salutem et  fidele servitium.  Sciatis, quod omnes milites, quos de vobis in

## p. 813 ##

capite teneo, feodati  fuerunt, vel antecessores eorum, anno et die quo Henricus
Rex, avus vester, fuit vivus  et  mortuus, per Ricardum filium Wace, quem pater
meus feodavit de dominio suo postea, unde facit quartam partem servitii uniug
militis,   Et isti sunt milites quos de vobis in capite teneo,
Robertus filius Willelmi, qui tenet f, II, militum,
Robertus de Chiefrevill, II. milit.
The Liber Niger  is  an account of those who held their lands under the king,
and granted relief to Henry II., on his  daughter marrying the Duke of Bavaria,
in 1167, (see page 288 of this Becord,).  Next to Doomsday and thePipe Roll of
Hen. I., the Black Book of theExchequer is the most ancient account of knight's
fees existing.—(See Sims's Manual, page 39 and note.)
Besides the families of Kerville seated at Wiggenhall St. Mary’s and Watling-
ton, there were other branches of the same stock in other parts of Norfolk.  That
at Beachamwell was very early enfeoffed of  a manor there by the Bigods, and
became extinct in  the reign of Henry V.   Notices of  two individuals of this
line appear in the Rotuli Hundredorum, Vol. I., p. 459, temp. Edward I.
4  (3ilbertus de Welle et Bartholomeus Sloysheze et Willelmus Quyrhosheved
ceperunt Willelmum de Chervyle injuste, et ipsum detinuerunt quousque extor-
serunt ab eo unam marcam,”
“Jdem Gilbertus venit et venire  fecit  homines ignotos ad domum Rogeri  de
Chervyle in Bycham, et vastavit bladum, et alia bona sua ad valorem xx",   Et
postea volujt  comburere domos suos quousque finem  fecit  cum  eo per  com-
bustionem xii marcarum.”
“Idem Gilbertus de Welle arravit super unam acram terre cum caruca sua de
vilenagio domini Rogeri de Chervyle, militis magne aetatis, quo dictus dominus
Rogerus ibi venit et defendebat ex parte domini  Regis, quod illam terram non
arrasset predictus Gilbertus,  propter hoc  noluit cessare sed percussit predictum
dominum Rogerum  militem  super pectus suum ita quod ad terram reversatus
cecidit, et de dicto ictu langwebat usque ad mortem, et dictam acram terre  cum
uno mesuagio ab eo extorsit.”
This attack on Sir Roger Kerville is a curious illustration of  the lawless state
of society in the rural districts in the reign of Edward IL.

## p. 814 ##

Nore  125.—FamiLy  oF  BAXTER (page 387).
The  family of  Baxter of Forncet and of  Tasburgh was very ancient and of
consideration, as appears by their pedigree in the Norris MSS.   Thomas Bax-
ter, Esq., of that family, built Rainthorpe Hall in Florden parish, about the year
1600.  They have for arms, arg. on a pale sable three bezants, all within a bor-
dure of the second.
See Blomfield in  the above named parishes.
NoTE 126.—SEeAL oF   THOMAS  GouURNAY II (page 391).
20th Henry VI.    A deed of  feofment with letters of attorney from Sir Thomas
8th June.       Kerdeston, Sir William Oldhalle, Knt., Thomas Gurnay, Esq.,
(1445).          and  others,  to    John Wode  of  Berston  and  others,  of  the
Manor  called Waldgraves in  East Barsham.   The seal of
Thomas Gurnay on the fifth label is a poor impression in red
wax  of  what  must  originally have been a  beautiful signet.
The legend is scarcely discernible.
The original deed is in the charter room of Hunstanton Hall.
NOTE 127.—JERNEGAN  OF   SOMERLEYTON (page 396).

## p. 815 ##

The tradition of the Jernegan or Jerningham family being originally  Danish,
does not  appear to rest upon any documentary evidence; but  is  nevertheless
very likely true, as these traditions are generally founded on fact.   There is no
mention of the name in Doomsday Book, but in the Black Book of the Exche-
quer,  the  date of which is 1167,  (Edit. Hearne, Vol. 1., 301), it is stated that
Hugh de Gernegan held one knight's fee of the honor of Eye.—This Hugh was
the son of Jernegan, who is mentioned in the Castleacre Register, and whose wi-
dow Sibilla paid 100 pounds into  the Exchequer in  1183.—(Pipe Roll, 30
Hugh Fitz-Jernegan was of Horham-Jernegan in Suffolk,  and from him  de-
scended the  Jernegans   of  Stonham-Jernegan  and Somerleyton  in   the  same
County, of which latter the Jernegans or Jerninghams of Cossey in Norfolk are
a branch, and the  only one  existing  at the  present day.—(Playfair's  English
Baronetage, Vol. 6, p. 171).
The above is the monument of Sir Thomas Jernegan and Joan his wife, in So-
merleyton church, Suffolk,   Weever, quoting from Camden, says the inscription
was :—
« Jesus Christ, both God and man
Save thy servant Jernegan.”
SCPPLEMESY
TO  THE  RECORD
The arms are those of Jernegan impaling Appleyard of  Dunston in Nosfolk ;
but  the arms of  Appleyard were azure a  chevron or, between 3 owls, argent,
while
here they are a fess gobony betwee3 nowls, which I presuis man eerror
of the carver, (see page 413).  Margaret
the daughter of  this Sir Thomas
Jer-
ningham was wife of Thomas Gumey IL—See Suckling’s Hist.  of  Suffolk,
part V. page 55.   This marriage is proved by John  Jernegan’s letter to Margn-
ret Paston given at page 391;  also by Elizabeth
White's legacy to  her niece
Margaret wife of Thomas Gurnay, at page 395.*   Sir H. Spelman is therefore
wrong in stating,  at page 318, that Margaret Jernegan married Joka Gournay V.
Nore 128.—THouas GURNAY (page 309).
Walter Aslack and Margery
his wife presented ThomasGumatoy the living
of  Rushmere in Suffolk, in 1435.   Who this Thomas Gurnay was does not
appear.
Nore 120.—WiLLiAM GURNAY IV.—EscuEAToR (pege 399).
Inquisitio capta apud Acle markett in comitatu Norfolciee, 13° Octobris Anno
6° Edwardi IV.  (1466), coram Willelmo Gumey armigero, escaetori in dicto
comitatu.  Juratores dicunt quod Johannes Paston, &e., &e.
In 13th Henry VII.  (1497) William Gurney was a feoffee in the settlement
of some of the estates of Lord Scales, who being Queen Elizabeth Woodville’s
brother, it seems likely William Gurney was a Yorkist.  His being Escheator for
Norfolk under Edward IV. confirms the probability of this.
This legacy            furred gown with a pair of pendant sleeves with the lining of green buckram.
(From the onions wil?

## p. 817 ##

NoTE 130.—CARMELITES OR WHITE FRIARS (page 403).
The Priory of Carmelites or White Friars was one of the largest in the city of
Norwich.   It  was in St. James's parish, near the river, on the right-hand side of
the street called Cowgate, which led  from White Friars’ Bridge to St. James's
Church.  It was founded by Philip de Cowgate, of Norwich, merchant,in 1256,
and many distinguished people were buried in  it.   In  a public-house in Cow-
gate, near St. James’s Church, is a crypt with groined arches, which was part of
the conventual buildings, and is now used as a cellar.
Note 131.—CALTHORPES OF  POCKTHORPE  (page 405).
The manor house of the Calthorpes in Pockthorpe was the same as that after-
wards inhabited by the Blennerhassets, and called Hassets' Hall.—See Blome-
field, vol. iv., p. 428.
Dame Jane Calthorpe,  of  Norwich,  widow,  and  Thomas,  her son and heir,
surrendered the lease they had of it from the Dean and Chapter in 1550, when
William Blennerhasset had a lease of it.    This  Dame Jane Calthorpe was
daughter of John Blennerhasset, of Frense, Esq.,  and widow of Sir Philip Cal-
thorpe.—Norris MSS.
I  think it likely William  Gurney had resided in this same manor house in
Pockthorpe, called the Lathes, before the Calthorpes, or  with  them, having
married a Calthorpe. -  I  find the sons-in-law of  families frequently designated
as of the place of  residence of  their fathers-in-law, with  whom’ they often re-
sided, particularly during the first  years of marriage.
Note 132. —INQUISITIO POST MORTEM WILLELMI  GURNEY SENIORIS (page405).
By virtue of a brief, dated at Westminster the 10th of May,  in the 13th year
of Henry VIII.  (1532),  and directed to the Escheator of Norfolk, inquisition
was taken at Norwich on the 4th of November following, to inquire what lands
were held by William Gurnay,  senior, at the time of his death, whether in his
own hands or  in  those of  feoffees;  and  the  Jurors  find  that the manors of
Swathynges, Rokelondtoftes, and Hyngham, with their appurtenances, and also
lands and tenements, with their appurtenances, in the towns of Hardyngham,

## p. 818 ##

Reymerston, Brandon, Runhale, Corston, Rysyng, Thuxton, and Hynghamberg,
with the advowson of the Church of St.  James in Thuxton, were held for him by
feoffees, who were William Calthorpe, Knt.,,  Henry  Gray, and Robert Drewry,
Esq., and that this  trust was created by  deed  bearing date  the 19th of Septem-
ber, 2nd Richard III. (1485), and that to fulfil certain agreements made between
‘Henry Heydon, Esq., and the said William Gurnay, senior, Esq., as shewn by
indentures bearing date the 28th of May,  1st Richard III.  (1484), these feoffees
demised the trust to others, for the use of William Gurnay, Esq., and Amy, his
wife,  and  afterwards  of  William Gurnay,  junior,  his  son and heir,  and Anne
Heydon, daughter of Henry Heydon, Esq.,  whom, by God's permission, he
was about to marry, and their lawful issue.—That William Gurnay,  junior, and
-   Anne, his wife, had issue Anthony Gurnay, and that the said William dying in
the life time of his father, his rights descended, after the death of his said father,
to  Anthony Gurnay, his son and heir.—That William Gurnay, senior, died seised
also of the manors of West Barsham, North Barsham, Houghton,* and Denver,
with their appurtenances, and of lands, tenements, rents, and services, with their
appurtenances, in  the towns of  West Barsham, North  Barsham, Houghton,
Denver, West  Derham,  Fyncham,  Fordham,  Downham,  and Larlyngeforthe,
which, by deed dated 6th of April,  the 21st Henry VII.  (1505),  he gave in
trust to Edward  Howard, Knt., Philip  Calthorpe, Knt.,  Robert  Clere, Knt.,
Robert Drewry, Knt., Nicholas Appleyard, Esq., William Calthorpe, of Pock-
thorpe, near Norwich, and Thomas Gurnay, Esq.,} who, by deed dated 28th of
January, the 23rd Henry VII. (1507), transferred the same manors to Anthony
Gurnay, Esq.,  and Margaret,  his wife,  one of the  daughters and coheirs of Sir
*       Robert Lovell, Knt.,  and other trustees.—That William  Gurnay, senior, died
~              seised also of  the manor of Harplee, with the advowson of the Church of St.
Lawrence there, with their appurtenances, and of lands, rents, and services in
Harplee aforesaid  and in  Houghton;  as  also  of  a  manor  called  Barkers  or
Bakers, with its appurtenances in Ursted (Irsted) ;  these also descended to the
said Anthony Gurnay.—And that the said manors of Swathynges, Hyngham,
Rokelondes, with their appurtenances, are held of William Arundell, Knt., and
* Houghton in the Dale.
t  This must have been Thomas Gurnay, second son of William Gurney, senr., and one of his executors,
but I  do not understand his being designatedEsquire.

## p. 819 ##

Lord Maltravers, as of their manor of Canteley,* by the service of one knight's
fee, and are valued at eighteen pounds per annum.—And that the said manors of
West  Barsham,  North  Barsham, Houghton,  and  Denver,  with  their  appur-
tenances, are held of Thomas  Earl of Arundell,  as of his  manor of Castle acre,
by one knight's fee and a half,  and are valued at twenty  pounds per annum.—
And that the said manor of  Harplee, with its appurtenances, is  held also of
Thomas Earl of Arundell, as of his manor of Castle acre, at half a knight's fee,
and is valued at ten pounds  per annum.—And  that the  said manor of Barkers
or  Bakers is held  of John,  Abbot  of  St. Benet’s Holme,  as  of  his  manor  of
Netysherd, in soccage, at a rent of  cii. shillings per annum.—And the Jurors
farther say that the said William Gurnay, senior, on the day of his death held
no manors, lands, or tenements of our lord the King in capite, in demesne, or in
service, nor any other manors, lands, or tenements whatsoever in the county of
Norfolk, except the manors aforesaid, with  their appurtenances, in form above
specified. —And that he died on the 16th day of February, A.D. 1507, the 23rd
of Henry VII.;  and that the said Anthony Gurnay is of blood and next heir,
and of the age of thirty years and more at the date of this inquisition.
Abridged and translated from the original document in
the Public Records, in the custody of the Master of
the Rolls, and preserved in  the Rolls  Chapel, to
wit: Escheats XIII,  Henry VIII., part 1, No. 103.
Also by virtue of another brief, dated at the same place and time as the fore-
going, and directed to the Escheator of  Suffolk, inquisition was taken at Bury
St. Edmund's on the 6th day of November following, for the same purposes.—
And the Jurors find that William  Gurnay, senior, died seised of  the manor of
Depden, in the said county, with all its appurtenances, together with the advow-
son of the Church of  St. Mary  the Virgin there;  and that the same trust was
created, and for the same use and intent.—And that the manor of Depden, with
its  appurtenances, was held  of  Thomas Earl of Arundell, as of  his manor of
Castle acre, at one knight's fee, and was valued at twenty pounds per annum.
See the original document in  the Public Records, in
the custody of  the Master of  the Rolls, and pre-
served in the Rolls Chapel, to wit: Escheats XIII.,
Henry  VIII,   part 1, No. 122.
* It is clear by this  that  these manors were held by the West Barsham Gurneys under the lords of
Cantley and Caistor, which had formerly been the elder line of their own family.

## p. 820 ##

Note  133.—WiLLIAM GURNEY  V.  (page 409).
William Gurney and Ann Heydon married soon after the 28th of May,  1st
Richard 3rd (1484),  as appears by  the inquisition taken after the death of his
father, William Gurney IV.   I  have stated that his  widow, Ann Heydon, re-
married after his death to  Sir  Lionel  Dymocke,  Knt.,  of Maring-on-the-Hill,
Lincolnshire.  She survived her second husband also, and lived at Boston, in
Lincolnshire, as a widow.  In her will she mentions her son Anthony, and also
another son, Thomas Gurney (called Gurro in the document), and her daughter
Sybsey.*  It is as follows :—
Extracted from the Registry of the Prerogative Court of
Canterbury.
In the Name of God Amen—The xxix day of  Marche in the yere of o* Lord
m' v* & xx    I Dame Anne Dymoke of  Boston Widowe hole of mynde and in
good remembrance makithe this my  testament concludyng therin my last Will
in this maner & forme, follouyng.  Firste I bequeythe my Soule to Almyghty God
& his Moder Mary, the Moder of Mara & all the Seints of the holy Company of
hevyn.   And my body  to be buryed in the Churche of  Seint Myghell in the
friers prechers in  Boston, &  my Mortuary as the lawe &  custom requyreth.
Alsoe I bequith to our Ladies werke of Lincollne iij*  iii}% and to of Ladye of
warke of Irsted in Northfolke xiij*  iiij%.  Alsoe I  gyve & bequythe to the highe
Aulter in the Quere of Seynt Myghell of the friers prechers in Boston ij peas of
Veluet, and an olde Gowne of  blacke Veluet, & iij Aulter Clothes, that is to saye
oon aulter clothe to the foresaid aulter of  Seynt Myghell, and an other to the
Aulter of  Seynt Domynyke, and an other to the Ault. of cure Ladye of Pitie.
Alsoe I  will have a preest to praye & syng for the Soule of  me,my benefactours &
all Xpen Soules, by the space & terme of iij yeres fullie to be complete & ended
at Scala celi in Boston, & he to have for Stipends & wages yerely v* vj* viij¢ for
his  annuall Seruice till the said yeres be expired and past.   Alsoe I bequeyth
and gyve to Petir Prate Prio" of  the frieres ij peyre of Shets, & to every yong
frere in the saide house Sherte clothe.   To the Subprio® ij peyre shets, to Friere
Robert Croft too peces of Canvas.   Alsoe I  gyve & bequeythe to Antony Gurno
* Amy, daughter of William Gurney and Ann Heydon, married George Sybsey, of Boston, gentleman.

my sonne a Gold Ring  w*a  turcas, a  fetherbedd, a tabill, a payre of tressils of

Probatum fuit suprascriptum testamentum coram domino apudLamehithe viij®
die mensisMaij AnnoDominiMillesimo quingentesimo vicesimo primo,juramento
Antonij  Gurno Executoris in hujusmodi testamento nominati, ac approbatum et
insumatum. Et commissa fuit administracio omnium et singulorum bonorum et
debitorum dicte defuncte predicto executori, de bene et fideliter administrando
eadem, ac  de pleno  et fideli Inventario, &ec., conficiendo, et nobis  citra  festum
Sancti Thome Martiris proximum futurum exhibendo. Necnon de plano computo
reddendo, &c., ad Sancta Dei evangeliajurato. Reservata potestate similem com-
missionem faciendi Johanni  Robynson,  armigero,  et  domino  Johanni  Kempe
presbitero executoribus etiam in hujusmodi testimento nominatis, si venerint infra
annum, &c.   Quarto die Mensis Julij  Anno Domini Millesimo quingentesimo
xxviij° commissa fuit  consimilis  administracio, &c.,  domino  Johanni Kempe
presbitero executori, &c., in persona Ricardi Feld NotariiPublici procurante, &c.
Note  133.—HEYDONS AND  BOLEYNS (page 412).
The daughter of Sir Geoffery Boleyn, who married Sir Henry Heydon, was
named Anne (not Elizabeth, as stated by some).  She survived her husband,
and her will, which is one of much interest, I  give at full length.  It is singular
she names her husband Sir   JohnHeydon.   This must be a mistake of the scribe,
I presume. By niece and nephew, as used in the will, are meant granddaughter
and grandson;  the Latin nepos having frequently the same meaning. She men-
tions her daughter Anne Dymocke, whose will has been given before.  West
Wyckham, in Kent,  was one of  the residences of  the Heydon  family,  which
accounts for her bequests in favour of  that place and others near.  (Blomefield,
vol. 6, p. 506.)   She directs that she should be buried in St. Luke’s chapel, in
Norwich Cathedral.  Whether by this she intended the Heydon chapel built by
her husband's father does not appear,
The will is as follows ;—

## p. 823 ##

In the Name of God, Amen—I Dame Anne Haydon, Wedowe, beying in my
hole mynde & off good memory, the xvij® day of December, In the yere of o*
Lord  God m' v° ix.  And in the first yere of King Henry the viij® make this my
testament and last Wyll in  maner and fo'me folowing, that is to sey, First gra-
cyous Thu. I  as a synfull creature by reason of  my demerits not worthy to  be
accepted into the holy Company of Heven to contynue in that holy place, Lord
wioute thy grete and large m'cy and  grace, The which  through thy passion to
eu'y cristen man meekely and lowly asking graciously grauntist.  Wherefore
I nowe beying in  my  full  &  hole mynde and in p'fitte love & Charitie & in
stedfast faith aske and crye Thu. our Lady Seint Mary the blissed Modre, all the
holy  Company of Heven, and all  the   Worlde mey;   Trustyng verely that
through thy passion and w* the sucour and relieff of that gracious Lady thy Mo-
der and Mayde to synners to hir calling for  helpe of  hir  grete pitie gretly and
very comfortabill.   Wherefore blissed Ihu. I  comytte my sole to the and to thy
blissed moder  our  Lady  Seynt  Mary, and to all  Seynts of Heven through the
meane and helpe of  Seynt John Baptist, Seynt Anne, Seynt Mary Magdalen
and Seynt Cristofer myne Avowers.* And my Synfull body to be buried in the
Chapell of Seynt Luks in the Cathedrall Churche of the Holy Trynitye of Nor-
wiche If I die in Norwich or in Norff. It. I geue & bequeth to the repa’cion of the
said Cathedrall Churche x" of lawfull  money w* this condicion, that the pryo’
and Monks of  the saide Churche suffre me to  have my sepulture in the said
Chapell of Seynt Luke, and alsoe that they kepe oonys in the yere a solempne
Dirge and Masse of Requiem in the saide Churche duringe the t'me of three
yeres next after my decesse. It° I bequeth to the prio® of the saide place vj* viije.
Monke  there that is no preest xij’, they to saye Dirige and Masse and to pray
for my soule att my burying daye.  It* I  will that myn Executo’s make noon
comon dole for me at my burying : but that they geue in the stedde therof x mrs.
to poore people keping houshoulds in Norwich most specyally to  theym that
bee dwelling w'in the parishe that I  dwell yn.    It*   I geue to the blake freers
xl   To the Whitefreers xx’.  To the Grey Freers xx*, and to the Austen Freers
in Norwich xx’, they to pray for my  soule.   It’ to eu'y suster of Normans xij
It’ to the Nonnes of Karrowe xx* to  praye for me.   It* to the repa’cion of the
®  Avower,—Patron.

Proved at  Lambeth 16th of May,  1510, by William Paston and Thomas
Edman, clerk.
The family of Boleyn is  very ancient in Norfolk.   The spelling of it varied,
being sometimes written Bulleyn and Bullen.   It is said to be of French origin,
and may be conjectured to take its name from the town of Boulogne, whence it
may have originated.    The  Boleyns  were early  settled at  Salle,  in  Norfolk.
Simon de Boleyne purchased lands there, by fine, in the reign of Henry IIL,
and was probably father of John de Boleyne, living at Salle in  1283.   Sir
Geoffrey Boleyne, a descendant, was  in high favour with Sir John Falstaff.   He

## p. 829 ##

was a citizen and mercer of London,  and Lord Mayor of the city in 1457.    He
realized a great fortune, and purchased the manor of Blickling,  which he made
his residence.    By  his honourable course, he raised his  family to eminence, and
placed them amongst the great magnates of  the realm.  His  second wife was
the  coheiress of Lord  Hoo  and Hastings;  and amongst other children had
Ann,  wife of  Sir  Henry Heydon, Knight, of Baconsthorpe, whose daughter
Ann was wife of William Gurney, Esq., V.   Sir Geoffrey Boleyn’s son married
the heiress of Thomas  Butler, Earl of Ormonde;  and Sir  Thomas Boleyn,  their
son and heir,  married  Lady Elizabeth Howard,  daughter of the  Duke of Nor-
folk, and was father of  the unfortunate Ann Boleyn, second Queen of Henry
VIII.   Sir Thomas was created Earl of Wiltshire and Viscount Rochford.  His
son,  Lord  Rochford,  was executed at the same time with his sister.
It is a matter  of  doubt  whether Ann  Boleyn  was born at Blickling,  or  at
Hever Castle, in Kent, or Rochford Hall,  in Essex;  but the probability is that
Blickling was her birth-place.   Both Blomefield and Sir Henry Spelman are of
that opinion.   The words of  the latter  (in  his  Icenia, p.  150,) are decisive:
“ Blickling,  Bolannorum  aliquando  sedes, e quibus orti  sunt Thomas  Bolen,
comes Wiltsheriee, et Anna Bolen uxor Regis Henrici VIII., optimee principis
Diva Elizabethae mater, natalitium hic sortita.” Sir Henry Spelman’s authority
in  this  question may be  considered conclusive, as he lived in the reigns of
Elizabeth and James I.,  and was connected with the Boleyns through the Le-
stranges, one of whom he married.  Mr.  Sharon Turner considers the birth of
Ann Boleyn at Blickling as quite certain (Modern History of England, vol. 2, p.
181), and there is a general tradition in the place itself to the same effect.   The
house of the Boleyns was pulled down by the Hobarts, who purchased the manor,
and built the present mansion in the early part of the seventeenth century.
The arms of Boleyn were argent a chevron gules between three bulls’ heads
couped sable.
