I  give the will of Sir Geoffrey Boleyn, the Lord Mayor, almost entire.   The
bequests it contains are worthy of one of the great merchant princes of England,
and prove him to have been a man of generous benevolence, as well as of success-
ful talent.
Extracted from the Registry of the Prerogative Court
of Canterbury.
In the name of God amen, the xiiij® day of  the moneth of Juyn, the yere of
oureLord God m' cece Ixiij, and the thridde yere of the Reigne of Kinge Edward
the Fourth  aft" the Conquest of Englond.   I  Geffray Boleyn,  Citezein and

## p. 832 ##

Mercer, and Alderman of Citee of London, beinge hole of mynde and my gode
memory make and  ordeigne this  my present testament in  maner and  fourme
folowyng, that is  to wite, First I  bequeth and comende my soule to Almighty
God my Maker, And to the blissed Virgyn oure Lady  Sainct Mary his Moder,
and to all Sainctes, And my body to be buried in the Chapell of Sainct John, in
the Chirche of Sainct Laurence, in the Jurie  of  London, on the South partie of
the Chauncell of  the same Chirche if it happen me to decese in London or els
where within the Realme of England, Sauing all ways yat if I  decese within the
Shire of Northfolk, I  wol that then my  body be buried in  the Church of Blik-
ling, in the Chapell of Sainct Thoms, which I in tyme passed did do make in the
North Side of the Chauncell there.   And  aft’ that my body so buried I   wolle
that alle my dettes whiche I  owe and am bounde in of right be truly and fully
paied.  Itm. I bequeth to  the Vicar of the said Chirche of Sainct Laurence for
my tithinges  and  offringes forgoten &  withholden and to pray for my soule x.
mares st'ling.   Itm.  I  bequeth  towards the  making  of  a  newe Rodeloft  to be
maade in  the said Chirch of  Sainct Laurence an c'. st'ling, to be paid assone
as the  same Rodelofte shalbe fully  made and ended, to  the entent that the
parisshens there bere the ouerplus of  the coste of the same Rodelofte, that ye
Chaauntries in the same Chirche nowe be kept and served after th’ententes and
willes of the foundours of the same.  Also I  will that xx". sterlings of my goodes
be dispended vpon the werk of the bodie of the Chirche of Blikeling aforsaid, or
vpon ornamentez for the same Chirch or on bothe, as shalbe thought moost ne-
cessarie by the discrecon of my Brother Maist" Thoms Boleyn, and of myn exe-
cuturs.  Itm.  I  bequeth cc marcs sterlings therwith for  to  fynde couenable,
honest and vertuous preest of goode gounaunce and conu’sacion to sing and saie
Diuine seruices, and pray for my soule and the soule of Dionise somtyme my
Wif, and for the soules of  Geffrav and Alice my fader and modir, and of Adam
Book, and for the soules of  all them that I am bounden vnto,and for all cristen
souls in thilke Chapell in which my body shall happen by this my Will to be
buried by xx yere nexte suyng after my decese.  Prouided  alway yat  the same
priest be a Doctour or Bachiler, or a Scoler of Diuinitee, or els att lest a Maister
of Arte, and be not promoted ne beneficeed in other wise.  Prouided also that if
the samePriest bee disposed to lerne and to exerce scoles att any of the Vniu'si-
tees or  to  labour in preching the worde of  God, and thereoppon effectualy

## p. 833 ##

exerce 8coles in any  of  the said Vniu'sitees or labour in preching the worde of
God by  licence of myn  executours or  of  thexecutours of  myn executours, I
wol  that then duringe the tyme of  such his  exercyng scoles or  labouring in
preching, the same priest be not bounde by  this my testament to sey or to pray
for my soule and the soules aforsaid in the fourme abouesaid in the said Chapell
in whiche my body shall so happen to  be buried,  but thereof during the said
tyme be discharged, so all weys  that  duringe the same tyme the said priest sey
and doo diuine s"uices for my soule & the soules abouesaid in suche of the said
Vniu"sitees and places as hee shall exerce scoles and labo" in prechyng.   And
also yat the same prieste yerely duryng the said  xx  yere att every of  the festes
of Estre, Natiuitie of Sainct John Baptiste, Sainct  Mychell the Archaungell and
Christs masse,  come, sey and  doo  diuine  seruice  for  my  soule and the soules
aforsaid in thilke  of  the said places in which my bodye shalbe buried, and also
say there att every of the same festes a sermon hauyng my soule and  the soules
aforsaid therin sp‘ially recomended. And that myn executours yerely in eu'y of
the said foure festes yeve and deliu’e of  my goodes to  the same priest ouer his
salarie for his costes to bee dispended in his comyng thid" and retournyng from
thens, if he be Docto® of Diuinitie vj*. viij®&. And if he be a Bacheler of Diuini-
tie v*.   And if he be a Scoler of Diuinitie  or a Maister of Arte iij® iiij%   Itm. I
wol that my fun’ell expenses as wele at the tyme of my burying as at my moneth
mynde* bee maade and doon so as my body shalbe buried with blak candelstikkes
and with xiij. torches to be born  by xiij. pour and nedy housholders that be no
comen torchberes nor vsed to begge.   And that  eu'y of them sey on the yeve at
diriges v. pater nosters, v. aues, a crede, and owre lady  saulter.  And on the
morow at Masse v. pater nosters, fyve auees, a crede, and our lady saulter.  And
y* euery of them haue for his labo’ a peir of treent bedes, a gowne of Russett or
of blak lyned, and a hode of blak and xij. of money.   And I  wole not that any
herce or  gylt  Candelstikes be ordeyned or sett aboute me nor any grete feste
made, but a dyner to  my Wife and to my brother Maist® Thomas and to myn
‘executours and suche other frendes and neighborughis as my  said Wife, my
Brother and executours wolle calle vnto them.   And also I  wol that if my body
be buried at London as is aboue reherced, that then the prisoners of Newgate,
Ludgate,  Flete,  Kingisbenche,  Marshalcie,  and  of  Clerkis Conuicte at West-
mynster haue the  day of my  moneth mynde  a  refresshing of mete and drinke,
* Month-mind,—~monthly remembrance of the dead.               T¥ Treen,—wooden.

## p. 834 ##

yat is to say, eu'y iii}. p"sones of the same prisoners if it be on a flessh day haue
a messe of  beef and of  moton  boyled,  a  penyworth of brede and a potell Ale.
And if  it  be on  a  flish  day that then eu'y iiij  of  the said prisoners haue a
messe of ij maner fishes and a peny worth of brede and a potell Ale.   And after
the Exequies and seruices of  myn enterment made and ended, I bequeth all the
said xiij torches to be distributed and disposed to poure Chirches after the dis-
crecion of myn executours where theym shall seme moost needefull. And if my
bodie be buried at London as is aboue said, I  wole that then eu'y pouer creature
beyng there at myn entering and moneth mynde haue of my goodes to pray for
my soule a peny.    And if my body be buried at Bliklyng, that then I  wole that
there be deled there of  my goodes the  day of my moneth mynde to eu'y pouer
creature comyng thider in  pure money ij°.   Itm. I  bequeth to Anne my Wife,
in the name of  her dower or  resonable part  to hir belongyng of all my goodes
and catals mouable m'. m'. marcs sterling.  And that one halfe of all my siluer
Vessell and plate  vncoyned, and also all  th'apparaill, ornamentez and joielx
longyng to  her owne pp’e body.    And all my stuffe of Vstilments and necessa-
ries of houshold longyng to hall parlo® botrie and pantrie chambres and kechyn
that is no place sauing, all  weys, I  wole that  the apparaill, ornamentes, Joielx
and necessaries to  myn  own ppre body belongyng be  sold and the money
thereof comyng be disposed be myn executours in  clothing, bedding, lynnen
and wollen and other necessaries to  poure and nedy people aftre the discrecon
of myn executours within a yere next folouyng aft’ my decese. Itm. I bequeth
to  Thomas my Sonne ccc marcs sterlinges.  And  to William my Sonne coc
marcs sterlings.   And to Isabell my Doughter  if  she be  not  maried  afore my
decese a m'. marcs sterlinges. And to Anne my doughter if  the same Anne be
not maried afore my decese a m'. marcs sterlings.   And  to  Alice my doughter
if she be not maried afore my decese a m'. marcs sterlings.  And forthermore I
bequethe and wole that  the halvendele* of my aforeseid siluer vessell and plate
vncoyned left vndisposed be devided among my said sonnes and doughters as
egally as is maybe bythe discrecions of the said Anne my Wife,my BrotherMaist".
Thoms, and of myn executours vnderwritten.    *   *   *    Also I bequethe and wole
that John Morland my s"vunt haue the custodie and keping of the money Joialx
and plate aboue by me to the forsaid Thomas my sonne bequethen vnto tyme as
* Halfendele,—half.

## p. 835 ##

my  said  sonne Thomas come to  his age of  xxv. yere  or  be maried.    Also I
wole and bequeth that Hugh Joye my s'vunt haue the custodie and kepynge of
the money, Joyalx and plate by me to the forsaid William my sone aboue be-
quethen  vnto tyme  the  same William  my sonne come to his age of xxv. yere
or be maried.   Also I  bequeth and wole that Geffray Randolf, Mercer, have the
custodie and keping of the money, Joialx and plate by me to the foresaid Isabell
my daughter aboue bequethen vnto tyme the same Isabell come to hir age of
xxv. yere or  be maried.   Also I  bequeath and wole that  Geffray Wallisshe,
myne apprentice, haue the custodie and keping of  the money,  Joialx and plate
by me to the  forsaid  Anne my doughter  aboue bequethen vnto tyme  the said
Anne my doughter come  to  her age of  xxv.  yere or be maried.    Also  I   wole
that William Welles, myn  apprentice, haue the custodie and keeping of  the
money, Joialx and plate  by me to  the said  Alice my doughter  bequethen vnto
tyme the said  Alice  my  doughter  come to hir age of  xxv.  yere or be maried.
Itm. I  ordeyne and wole by this my present testament that euery of  the forsaid
John Morlond, Hugh  Joye,  Geffrey  Randolf,  Geffrey  Walissh  and  William
Welles fynde and make sufficient suertee bounden accordinge to the custume in
this Citee of London for  Orphans for  such money, Joialx and plate  as aboue by
me is ordeigned and assigned to his custodie and  keeping for to delu’e the same
money, Joialx  and plate  to  suche as it  shall  app'tene by  force of  this
my  testament as  soone as the  cas shall require, accordinge to  my  Will
thereof aboue expressed, with outen any  encrece therof or  therfor to be axed,
had or born  but  of  the  free will and  conscience of hym  to whom the said cus-
.  todie as is aforesaid is  assigned.” And  I  wole not, but I  strateley forbede and
defende that none of  the saied John Morlond, Hugh  Joye, Geffray Randolf,
Geffray Walissh & William Welles within the tyme of  the said custodie in any
wise leve  or  occupie any of  the  said Joialx  and plate  to  his  custodie aboue
assigned, but vtterly absteine hym therefrom by all the said tyme.   And if any
of them within the said tyme occupie  or  leve anie  of  the same Joialx or plate
ayenst the fourme  aforsaid, Than  I   wole  that  the  power of eu'y of them  soe
occupying or leuying any of  the  saide Joialx and plate  cese aswell of the cus-
todie of the money to  his  keping  assigned as  of  the joyalx  and plate to his
keeping also assigned. And that from thensforth myne executours vndrewritten
have the custodie and kepinge of the same money, Joialx & plate ’til the Childe

## p. 836 ##

or Children to  whome the same money, Joialx and plate by this my Will shall
app'tene be of the age of xxv. yeres or be  maried.    And I  wole and ordeyne by
this my testament that none of my  forsaid Children be maried w'yn  his age of
xxv. yere with outen the wille and  assente of  Anne my Wif, her moder, and of
my Brother Maister  Thomas  and of myne  executours or of  the more  parte of
them, So that the same Anne my  Wif while  she standith soole and my said
Brother be of the same more part.   And if any of  my said Children be maried
ayenst the  fourme aforsaid, or be gouerned in  other wise then  by the will and
assent of her said Moder while she standith soole, and of my said Brother and of
myn  Executours or  of  the more part  of  them in fourme aforsaid, I  wole and
ordeigne that then the bequests by me aboue maade to suche of my said Children
as happen to be maried or  governed contrarie to my will  afore reherced be
vtterly voide and of  none effecte.  And  that  then forthwith all  the money,
Joialx  and plate to hym or them of  my said Children that  so happen to be
maried or governed contrarie to my Will  aforesaid remayne and turne to myn
executours vnderwritten, and be by them  disposed for my soule and the soules
aforsaid in werkes and dedes of  mercie, pite and charitie after the good discrecions
of my said executours, any bequeste, matter, cause or wrytinge aboue maade to
the contrary notwithstanding. Item  I  wol  that my housholde and meneyall
meyne*  therin be kepte hoole togiders during  thre moneths nexte suying aftre
my decese, to th’entent that in meane tyme good pinsion and bestoweing may be
had and made of and for my said meyne and servantes. Itm. I  bequeath to the
Freres of eu'y house of  the iiij.  ordre of freres mendyciauntes in the Citee and
suburbes of London, that is to witte,  Carmes, Prechours,  Menours, and Augus-
tyns, for to pray  for my  soule, xx.    Itm.  I  bequeth to  the Crouched Freres
beside the toure of London to pray  for my soule vj’. viij%. Item I bequethe to
be disposed to  the moost  necessarie vse of  the pouer  prisoners of Ludgate in
London by the beste discrecons of myn executours in v. yere nexte suying after
my decese x" sterlings, that is to say, eu'y yere xI°.   Itm. I  bequeth to bee dis-
tributed and disposed in lyke wise amongpoure prisoners in Newgate of London
in v. yere nexte suying after my  decese xv". sterlings, that is, to eu’y  yere  iij®
sterlinges. Item I bequeath to be disposed in semblable wise among the pouer
prisoners of the Flete in v. yere next suying after my decese c. sterlings, that is
* Meyne—company.
to say, eu'y yere xx*.   Itm. I bequeth to be disposed in semblable wise among
the  pouer prisoners in  the  Conuicte  prison of Westmynstre  in  v.  yere next
suying after my decese c*. sterling, that is to say, eu’y yere xx*.  Itm. I bequeth
to be disposed in  likewise among the pouer prisoners in the Kyngisbenche in
Southwerk in v. yere nexte suying  after my decese xv". st'lings, that is to say,
euw'y yere iij!.   Itm. I bequeth to be disposed in like wise among the pouer pri-
soners of the Marchalsie in Suthwerk in v. yere next  suying after my decese x".
sterlinges, that is to say,  euery  yere  xI°.   Itm.  I   bequeth to poure  syke  and
feble people in the hospitall called Sainct Mariespitall w”oute Bishopsgate of
London by the goode discrecons of myne executours in v. yere next suying after
my decese c*. sterlinges, that is to say, eu'y yere xx.    Itm.  I  bequeathe to be
disposed in like wise to the pouer sike and feeble people in the hospitall of oure
Lady of Bethelem without Bisshoppesgate aforsaid in thc said v. yere nexte after
my  decese c®. sterlinges, that is to say,  every yere  xx’.    Itm. I  bequeth to be
disposed in  like wise to the sike pouer and feeble people in  the hospitall of
Sainct Thomas in Suthwerk in  v.  yere  next  suying  after  my  decese by   the
discrecon of  myne  executours c®. sterlinges, that  is  to say,  euery  yere  xx".
Itm. I  bequeth to be  disposed in lykewise to the like pouer and feble people in
the hospitall of oure Lady called  Elsingspitell of  London in v. yere nexte suy-
ing after my decese 1° sterlinges, that is to say, eu'y yere x°   Itm. I  bequeath
to be disposed to  the pouer  sike  and  feble people in the Hospitall  of  Sainct
Bartholomew  in  Westsmythfeld in  the  Subarbes of London in  like wise in v.
yere nexte suying after my decese x". sterlinges, that is to say, euery yere xl".
Itm. I bequethe to be disposed to the lepers of the loke* beyond Sainct Georges
in Southwerk in v. yere next suying after my decesse by the discresson of myne
Executours c*. sterlinges, that is to saye, eu'y yere xx’.   Itm. I  bequeth to the
lepers at Kyngeslond beside Hakeney to be disposed among theym by myn
executours in v. yere nexte suying  after my  decesse a c®. st'lges,  that is to say,
euery yere xx°.   Item I  bequeth to be disposed in like wise to the pouer Almes-
women of Almeshous within the hospitall of Sainct Katerine beside the towre of
London in v. yeres next suying after my  decease 1°. sterlinges, that is to say,
euery yere x.   Item I bequeth and wole that cc". sterlinges of  my goodes be
disposed by the discrecons of myn  executours to  the pouer  housholders in the
*  Loke,—a path or lane.

## p. 838 ##

townes and parishes  of  Blikling,  Holkham,  Stukey, Mulkeberton, Kemsyng*
and Sele and other parisshes and places where I  or any  other persone or per-
sones to myne vse haue or att  the  tyme of my  decese shall  haue any  lyuelode
and in other parisshes and places to myne said lyuelode adioyning, that is to
wite, in money, clothing  and  other  necessaries to  hem  behouefull in as hasty
tyme as it shall mowe goodlye be done after my  decese to th'entente that  they
pray sp‘ially for my soule and the soules aforsaid. Itm. I bequeth and wele that
ouer my biquestes aboue said there be disposed and distributed of my  goodes
for my soule and the soules aforsaid eu’y  yere  during v. yere next  suying after
my decese cc. sterlinges to pouer people  sp‘ially to pouer housholders havyng
Wyves  and  Children and not  bavyng  whereof  to  sustene themself.    And  in
releuyng  of  pouer people broght  to  pouerte by fire,  water or other misfortune
of  the Worlde, and in  amendyng and reparing of  High Waies, passags and
briggs where nede is.   And also in  releuyng of pouer prisoners kepte in prison
out of Citee of London in whatsomeu™place or contre they be w®in the Realme
of Englond.   And I  wole that myne executours ordeigne a Man or Men feithful
to theire conceite, and to dispose the forsaid somes. of money by me aboue be-
quethid to and for the prisons and hospitalls aboue expressed and named, and to
the forsaid lepers.  And also of the money comyng of  the sale of  th’apparrell,
ornamentez and necessaries and Joialx to my propre body belonging.   And also
the sayd cc. st'linges yerely duryng the said terme of v. yere after the maner and
fourme aboue expressed. And  that my  saied executours delyuere the  saied
sommes of  money to  the saied Man or Men by them so ordeyned to doo and
obserue therof my said bequestys, at suche sep’ate tymes as shalbe thought ex-
pedyent withouten any contradiccon or ageynseyng.  And that within conuenient
tyme after eu"y receyte maade or had in this  behalfe the same man or men re-
ceivyng or ordeyned to dispose swere and  declare to myn executours howe, and
in what maner, and where the money by them or any of  them received, is or
shalbe disposed.   And I  wole  that  the same man  or  men so to be chosen and
ordeyned doyng truly their labo" and devoir in the premisses,haue of my goodes
eu'y yere during the said terme of v. yere  almaner of costes to be dispended for
and aboute the same distribucion. And also a competent rewarde for his or their
labour by the goode discreccion of myn  executours. Item I bequeath to John
Randolf, Mercer, late my Ser'unt, tenne poundes sterlinges. Itm. I bequeth to
* Quere,—Kempston.

## p. 839 ##

John Lewes, late my sunt, to be deliu’ed to hym at suche tymes as by th’advise
and discrecion of myne  executours it  shall  seme or be thought  necessarie, c".
sterlinges. Itm. I bequeth to Edmonde Joce, late myn apprentice, c*. sterlinges.
Itm. I  biqueth to Hugh Joye,  myn  apprentice, x.  marcs stg.   Itm.  I  biqueth
to  William Welles, myn apprentice, x".  sterlings.  Itm. I bequeth to  Richard
Sutton, myn apprentice, X.  marcs sterlinges.   Itm. I bequeth to Geffray Walissh,
myn apprentice, xx". sterlings.  Prouided alweys that my saied s'untes and ap-
prentices be goode, trewe and helpyng to  myne  executours in all thynges  con-
cernyng this my testament. Item I biqueth and wole that  Anneys Likill, my
s'unte, have x. marcs sterlinges of my goodes to bee delyuered to her aft. th’ad-
vyse and discrecion of Anne my Wif, and of myn executours. Itm. I bequethe
and wole that eu’y of  myn other s’untes aboue not named dwellinge or beyng
w® me at the tyme of my decese bee rewarded of my goodes after his  degree by
the goode aduyse and discreccion of my sayd Wife and Executours.  Itm. I be-
quethe to Geffray Randolf, late apprentice of RauffMarche, x". sterlinges. Itm.
I bequethe and wole that Maister Thomas Randolf, Clerk, haue of my  goodes
eu'y yere during Xx. yere nexte  suying after my decese, if he  so long abide and
continue at Scoles, proceding at Diuinitie, att Cambrigge or at Oxenford, or els
laboure in  prechyng, x.  marcs sterlings forto syng and pray  for my  soule.
Prouided that if  the saied Maister Thomas Randolf in  the meane tyme be ad-
uanced or promoted to any benefice of  Cure or Prebend exceeding the yerely
value of x. mares sterlinges, that then fro  tyme of suche p'mocion or advance-
ment forthewarde my saide biqueste to  hym  maade be of none effect, but from
thensforth the  paiement to  hym to be maade of  the said x. marcs yerely  cese.
Itm. I biqueth and wole that Maist™ Geffray Bishop, Viker of Fulbo'ne, haue of
my goodes eu’y yere duryng his lif, to be paide at iii}. Vsuell termes of the yere
by even porcions, x. marcs sterlings, if my  goods soe long endure.  And as long
as the same Maister  Geffray  be beneficed I   will  that  hee dispose the  said  x.
marcs yerely for my soule after his owne discrecon.  And that by as longe tyme
as he shall not stande beneficied he haue and take the said x. marcs yerely to his
ppre vse, to syng and pray  for my  soule.    Itm.  I  bequeth  and wole that Dame
Johane hoo my  Cousin, nonne of  the house of  Berkyng, haue of my  goodes
yerely during hir life after my decese, if my goodes soe long endure, xxv;)*. viij®
sterlinges to her to bee paied yerely  att the iiij. vsuell termes aforesaid by even

## p. 840 ##

porcons forto pray  for my  soule.  Itm. I  woll  not  that any Dettour of myne
that is openley knowen insufficient  of goodes for to pay to myn executours the
dette that he owith mee be in any  wise imprisonett,  cupleded or   vexed therfor.
Itm. if it can be proved that 1 haue had of anye persone or persones any goodes
by any meens otherwise than verray right and goode conscience requiren, I  wole
that due restitucion and amendes be maade therfor of my goodes as equite, righte
and goode conscience requiren.  The residue of  all my  goodes and catals and
moueables whersoeuer they be aft my dettes paide, my  fun'ell expenss maade
and doon, and this my present testament p‘formed and fulfilled, I  biqueth and
remitte hoolly to the dispocision of myne executours, to th’entente that they the
same Resydue truly, feithfully  and hoolly  dispose for my soule and the soules
aforsaid in as hasty tyme as it shall nowe goodely be doon, in fyndyng of pouer
and vertuous scolers and children to scoles, and in  marriage of pouer maydens
beyng destitute of goodes and frendship, and in releuyng of pouer people, and in
other werkes and dedes of mercy, pitee and charitee after their goode discrecons,
as they wold that I  shuld doo for their soules in case semblable if they  were in
my wey and I  were in theirs.  And  of  this my  testament I make and ordeyne
myn  Executours, that is  to  say, Anne my  Wif,  Rauf Verney, Aldermann,
Richard Nedeham, Thomas Burgoyn, gentilman, and Hugh Fenne, gentilman.
And of this my testament I  make & ordeyne my Brother Maister Thomas Boleyn
Ouerseer. And I bequeth to euy of the forsaidRauf, Richard, Thomas Burgoyn
and Hugh, taking on hym the chardge of execucon of this my testament, for his
labo™  xxx". sterlinges. And I bequeth to the forsaid Maister Thomas my Brother
for his conseill, ouersight and labo™ in  this behalue x".  stlinges.  In Wittnesse
wherof to this my testament I haue sett my  seall yoven att London the day and
yere aboue said.
Proved at Lambeth 2nd of July,  1463, by Ann,  widow of the testator, and
Richard Nedam, the executors.
In a collection of original  Norfolk  letters in  my  possession are some of  the
Heydons’, all  of  them apparently addressed to  Sir  Bassingbourne Gawdy,
Knight, of West Harling, some of them written whilst he was Sheriff of Norfolk,
which office he filled three times—in 1578, 1593 and 1602.   This  collection of
of Norfolk letters contains nearly three thousand in number.  They were in the

## p. 841 ##

possession of Oliver Le Neve, Esq., of Witchingham, who married Ann, daughter
and heir of Sir John Gawdy, and of Peter Le Neve, Esq., Norroy. - They are
nearly all written by members of Norfolk  families, and addressed to Norfolk
people, a large portion of them to the Gawdys and Le Neves, and many contain
matter of considerable interest.
Those written by the Heydons are as follows :—
No. 1.
After my right  hartie comendacions, understandinge that it hathe pleased the
Quenes majestes to appoynte you highe sherief of this Countie ofNorff. I am ther-
fore bolde to crave your frendshippe (as heretofore I  have likewise done of other
sheriefes) to let me have the  nomynacion  of  the baylywick of Holte Hundred,
which most accustimably I  have had in yeres bypast.  And I shall not fayle, god
willynge, to appoynte suche an one to the same as shall not onelye enter bandes
for the due execucon of that offyce and all thinges therto belonginge accordinglye
as to hys dutie apperteyneth, but also be readie to showe you what pleasure he
ys able to the best of his power.    And uppon thaccomplishment herof I  will re-
mayne your debtor for anye lyke  pleasure wherin I maye stand you insteade.
And thus I byd you hartelye farewell. Ffrom Baconsthorpe this xxj*. of Novem-
ber 1578.
I pray you commend me hartely
to your
Your  well assured frend
XTOoFER   HEYDON.
To the right worshipfull and his
verye lovinge frend Bassingborn
Gawdye esquire high sherief of
the countie of  Norff.
No. 2.
Uppon motion made to me in the behalf of my cosyn John Smythe who now
remayneth in your coustodie, I have thought good to request you that considringe
the contagiousnes of the sicknes now in  Norwich you wonlde be contented with
m". Sheriefs leave, to suffer him to come into the countrie and remayne there for

## p. 842 ##

a tyme with a keper, whose charge and your frendshippe therin he will consyder,
and I  will not be unthankfull to you for your favor and good will towards hym
in that behalf. And thus I byd you farewell.  Ffrom Saxlingham* this xxx*®. of
Your loving frend
XToFER HEYDON.
No. 3.
After my right hartie comendacons, wheras you la‘elye directed your lettre to
me, ST William Buttes,  and sundrie others, requiringe us that all expenses set
aparte, we should make our personall appearance before the Justyces of Assize,
and attend upon them at the next Assizes to be holden within this countie, which
lettre was written in other fourme than hathe bene heretofore accustomed, unlesse
it wer by some speciall comission, or lettres  from  the  Queenes majesty, or the
counsell.  And for  that about the same tyme, I  may be occasyoned by reasen
of my farre distance from that place and otherwayse to be absent, unlesse it wer
by some direction for servyce of  the Prynce, which I  would in no wyse slack,
but in all respectes perfourme, to the best of  my  power.  I  shall hartely praye
you therfore, that by this bearer, you will adwise of  thoccasyon of your wright-
inge in that sorte.   And  thus right  hartelye I  comitte you to Almightie god.
Ffrom Saxlingham this xij®. of Julye 1579.
I  pray  you  hartely I  may  be
comended to  m™. Gawdy your
good beddfelow.
Your most assured
XTOFER  HEYDON.
To  the  right   worshippful  Bars-
ingborne Gawdy esquire High
Shereif of the countye of  Norff.
With  speed.
My good frend your Uacle andI, upon consideracon of the wante of convenient
*  Saxhngham was a manor and residence of the Heydons.

## p. 843 ##

provision in Lytcham to entertaine in anie good sorte the Assemblie that should
have bene there, as also for sparringe some travaille, have thought flakenham
more indifferent* and meeter to receave the companie, and thereupon do resolue
(god willinge) the Juries have knowledge of this our alteracon.  And I have sent
this bearer herewith  to  informe you thereof prayinge you not to faill to accom-
panie us there upon Tusday next by ix of the clocke in the fornoone; untill which
tyme with my  verie hartie  comendacons I  bid you  farewell.   Baconsthorp  the
xxij* of Sept. 1581.
My wyf comendeth her hartelie
unto you and ys  glad she shall
se you here shortlie.
Your verie loving and assured
frend in all I may,
WiLLM.   HEYDON.
To my  worshipfull  and  assured
good frend Bassingborne Gawdie
Esquire give These
At Harlinge.
Haste.
No. 5.
Good cosine, beinge incited by the imoderate bragges of  farmer} I  [have]
taken uppon me to stande in electione for  one of  the places in the parliament,
which of my self I  did never  dreame on vntyl  I  was provoked contrary to  my
expectation, my sute is good cosine for your furtherance uppon munday at 7 of
the clok at norwich by the voyces of your  tennantes and frendes, and if I mighte
enioy the companie of my cosines or  of one of them ther, I  doe thinke it shold
doe me so much credit as I  wold bynd my self to ryde a hundred myle to doe
* In the 16th century, the word indifference was used in the sense ofpassable, as it seems to be here.
>           Thomas Fermor, Peg. of East Barsham.—Sir Christopher Heydon was not  successful in his attempt
ot  be coming Knight of the Shire for Norfolk in 1586,  Mr.   Fermor   and Mr. Gresham, of Intwood, having
; but two years later, in 1588, he was chosen member for Norfolk,  to which the next letter
(No. 6) seems to refer.

## p. 844 ##

them any plesure.  And thus in hast with my comendations to  youre self and
my cosine your bedfellow I  byd you farwell, xxiiij of September 1586.
Your assured
CHrisTOPHER  HEYDON.
To the Worshippfull my very lov-
"inge  cosine Basingburne Gaudy
Esq" geve thes.
Understandinge that ther is a parliament  expected, though my self meane not
to stand  for  the  voyce of  the  country,  to be one of the Knightes,  yet to meet
with* the secret labour  of some that  more of a factious  humor then affeccon to
the service of the Comonwealth, have underhand practised to transferre the repu-
tacon of the place to a stranger from the gentrye of Norffolke, I have thought
good to praye you not to goe against vour owne countreymen, but rather that at
my request you and your frendes would hould your voyce in suspence, and re-
serve the libertye therof untill the elleccon; 1  doubt not  but wee shall fynd of
our owne countreymen more sufficient  then any stranger that shall unworthylye
seeke his owne  glorye,  to  our  generall disgrace, and  withall I  hope  fewe will
shewe themselves so weake as to preferre any pryvat  mans humor  whatever be-
fore his owne particular inbredd love, to  the good of his native countrey ;  assur-
inge you that such will be propounded unto you by deliberat advise and generall
approbacon at the eleccon as can not be misliked in your choise, and so beinge
sorye that I  and others in a comon care of the honor-of our countrey are enforced
by the vayne ambition of some to enter into this apposite course, I  repose upon
your good affeccon and consideraccon together as to a case in this moment ap-
perteyneth, and so I  comend me in hast.   Maninngton 2° January.
Your assured frend,
Caristo. HEYDON.
The following letter probably has reference to fear ofdisturbance consequent on
the execution of Mary Queen of Scots, which had occurred on the 7th of February
®*  To meet with is to be even with,—to counteract.

## p. 845 ##

previously;  and also proves the preparations of defence which were made on
account of the  threats of invasion by the King of Spain, and which afterwards
resulted in the celebrated Spanish Armada, and its great failure :—
After  my  verie hartie comendacons, Receyvinge yesternight verie late, the
copies of a Lettre with instructions from the Lords of her majesties prvye coun-
sell to  the L. Lyeutenant of this countie, addressed; and also his Lordships
lettre directed to anie of us the deputie Lyeutenantes of this  shiere for suche
services as are therin expressed and  comannded to be put in  present execution,
I  am therfore urged in the absence of the rest of myne assocyates, to wright this
my Lettre unto you;  And therby to advertise you, yt is required comanndment
should be geven that  all  the persons appoynted as captens of  footemen and
horsmen should call their inferior offycers of the fotebandes unto them, and charge
them without anie delay to warne all  persons under their charge from man to
man to be readie with their arms and weapons by a daye certenlye to be lymyted
80 it be before the xx" daye of this instant moneth of marche.  And the persons
eyther deade or gone out of the countrie, or that be lame or sycke, must be sup-
plyed by the seid daye, to be in all poyntes sufficientlye provyded and furnished
with  armour and otherwise, which direction I preaye and in her majestes name
require you to see in ewry respect effectuallye perfourmed accordinglye before the
xvj** daye of this seid moneth.    And if you lyek to conferre with me at norwich
on Tewesdaye next  concernynge this  service I  wilbe readie to  impart unto you
suche matter as I  have that may be in any wyse to your best satisfaccon. And
thus I hartelye comitte you  to  god.   firom Baconsthorpe this v*. of marche
1586.
Your verie lovinge frend
WiLL.      HEYDON.
I have receaued a comission under the Exchequer seale and a lettre from her
majestes Atturney generall directed to me, mr.  Sargeant Gawdie, yourself, and
Mr. Buxton to inquire and sease to her highnes use all the goodes and two partes
of the land of certaine recusantes therein contained. And for that I feare the pro-

## p. 846 ##

ceedinge therein wille be longer delayed in staying upon mr. Sargeantes returne
from  his  circuitt  (whose asistance I  do  especiallie desire) I  have therefore
thought good to pray vou to meete me at Attlebrigge upon munday next by x of
the clocke in the  forenoone that we may  conferre of some course for  the Exe-
cucon thereof.  And  so haueing in meane whyle sent herewith a copie of the
lettre that you may consider and the better inform your self of the contentes of
the comission with my hartiest comendacons I bid you farewell.  ffrom Bacons-
thorp the xv®. of Auguste 1587.
Your assured frend in all I may
WiLrM. HEYDON.
To the worshipfull my Approued
good ffrendBasingborne Gawdie
Esquire give these with  Speed
Speed Speed.
Whereas by the space of fforty yeares and upwardes as I  am informed ther
haue bene kept a Inne at the signe of the whighte horse* in Kenynghall wherein
William Mulley lately dwelt, and nowe he is gone from thence, I  am in the be-
half of this bearer William Cheflley alias Blackwill  to  request your favor in his
behalfe to signe and seale this  licence which  I  haue put my hand and seale to.
And in performeing of this my requeste you shall  fynd  me redy to requite you
with the like.    And thus I  comitt you to God this ffirst of October.
Your assured loving frend
CarisT. HEYDON.
To   the   right   Worshipfull   his
verey Lovinge ffrend S*.Bassing-
burne Gawdy Knight.
This following order was obviously in  consequence of  the Spanish Armada
being then expected. The English troops were encamped at Tilbury fort in Essex.
They were about forty thousand strong, commanded by  Lord Hunsdon, the
* The White Horse Inn still exists at Kenninghall.

## p. 847 ##

Queen's cousin (son of Mary Boleyn).   It was here that Queen Elizabeth re-
viewed her army in  person, and by her courage and high  spirit inspired it
with  the  utmost zeal in  the  national defence.   Altogether 101,040 men
were called out, regimented and armed in England and Wales.—(Turner's Mo-
dern History of England, vol. iv., p. 495.)
No. 10.
Forsomuche as Bassingborne Gawdy the yonger Esquier is appointed by us
Captaine of  two hundred men levyed in the countie of Norff. to  be  sentt unto
Tilborie in Essex by the right hon. lorde of her majestes privie councells direc-
con, these are therefor for his  better performannce of  the said  service,  to  will
and demannde you  upon  the receipte hereof  to  furnishe hym and his men at-
tendinge hym,  or the bringer hereof appointed by hym, with  suche able poste
horses as they shalbe occasioned to  use, at ordinarie pryses.    And hereof ffaill
you not as you tender the furtherance of her highnes service and will annswer
the contrarie at your perill.   Given under the hands of us the depuitie leiutennte
of the said county the thrid  of Auguste 1588.
Wire.  HEYDON.
To all maiors Baylyves Cunsta-
blis  and other her majestes offi-
cers and loveinge subiectes to
whome yt may appertaine and to
knowe of them.
I  hartelie thanke you  for your kinde and frendlie message you sent mee by
.my man who was with you from mee for the Bayliewyke of Holte Hundred and
wilbee redie  to  requite your  good will  towardes mee with anie pleasure I  may
.performe you.   I  am further to desire you to bee pleased at my requeste to en-
tertaine into your seruice the bearer hereof Robt. Cowell beeinge a yonge man
of  good  parsonage and discended of. honeste parentage, and verie desireous to
. gerve  you, or your undersheriffe  yf  your   self  bee furnished, not dowbtinge but
hee will so carrye himself as shall bee to  your  well lykeinge and deserveinge

## p. 848 ##

your fauor.    And so leavinge hym unto you  with my verie hartie comendacons
I  comytte you to God.   At Saxlingham the iiij**. December 1593.
Your verie lovinge cosen and frend
WiLrLM. HEYDON.
To the right worshipfull my verie
good cosen and frend Bassing-
borne Gawdy Esquier highe She-
riffe of the countie of Norff.
By the following letter it  appears Sir Christopher Heydon was in great diffi-
culties as to his affairs in 1602.   This might arise from the extraordinary hospi-
tality of  Sir Christopher Heydon his grandfather, and from the projects entered
into by his father Sir William Heydon, with certain citizens of  London, by which
he contracted a  large debt. The former was a man of high character, much es-
teemed for  his  justice, charity and remarkable hospitality.  He is said to have
entertained 30 head or master shepherds of his own flocks at a Christmas dinner
at Baconsthorp, a proof of his great estate, and of the uninclosed state of Norfolk
at that time. This Sir Christopher Heydon, “the great housekeeper,” died in 1579.
See Blomefield, vol. vi., p. 507.
I  am sory my unhappie estate giveth me occasion to becum a suter vnto you
in a matter of  this nature, but  I have no  other way to preuent executions that
are nowe lykly  to  cum out  agaynd Mr. Thetford as my suerty but by your
frendly forbaring to execute the same till  the next  terme, not  doubtinge in ye
meane tyme but to satisfie ye parties them selves.  If I  could have sould land I
wold not have suffred matters to growe to  this extremitye, and before the tyme
prefixed I  assure you uppon my honest faythe to prouide sum way for ther con-
tentment.   And in the meane tyme, I  will not  desire your  undersherifes losse,
but will most thankfully consider him for his fes, which though it wilbe a charge
yet by this fauor I  shall  preuent much infamie, grefe, and other inconueniences
yt  will happen if my suertyes shold be molested and empeached by my debt.  I
most ernestly pray you yt  you will instantly giue your ordre by lettre or other-
wise to your ondersheriffe for ye satisfyinge of this my request, giuing him con-

## p. 849 ##

tentment to ye vttermost of his  due, wherby as I  shall acknowledge my self
infinitly pleasured so I pray you persuade yourself, you shall ever fynd me ac-
cordingly redy to  deserue your  kyndnes  at  this  present.   So comending me
hartely to your self, with my service to my Lady in hast I rest
Your very assured frend
CHrisTR. HEYDON.
To  ye  Right  worshippfull S-.
Bass. Gaudy Knight Highe She-
rif of Norff.
There being no  date of  the year to  the following letter, it  is  difficult  to
determine which Sir John Heydon is here intended—but it was probably Sir
John Heydon, son of Sir William Heydon and Ann Woodhouse (see pedigree,
p. 412), who was cotemporary with Sir Bassingbourne Gawdy.
With rememberaunce of  my dewtie this be to ascertayne you that after your
coming from Norwich  yesterdaye the chalenge between S'. Robert Mansfylde
and S°. John Heydon took effecte.  The first payd for it but bled onlye, but S*.
John Heydon before this is thought to be deade.  This nuse we had from one
that cam from Norw" yesterdaye, whoe upon the waye mette w* passengers that
cam to the ende of  the fraye & reporteth thus.    They went about  twelve of  the
clocke from Norw® to  fight  about half a myle distant from  Herford  Bridge w*
out frende of  eyther syde.  In  the fight passengers cam bye and before their
neere approch they did see S'. John Heydon falle: he is hurte in many places
but the deadlye wounde is by a thrustin at his throte and out at the toppe of his
heade.  S* Robert went to  Norwich  w™ the armes of the other who leye upon
the grownde till  the foresaid passengers cam to him, but could neyther stande
nor speake, and they conveyed him to  a house next to the place, but further he
reporte not.    Thus much  I   thought  to  certefie you,  yf it be newse it shall be
wellcome, yf not  1  desyre you  to  continewe  your  good  constructon  of  mee.

## p. 850 ##

I  am and will be redeye to doe any sfuice for the discharge of my dewtie, or that
might contente your worship.
Ro. DAWBENEY.
Redgrave Octob. 3.
To the right worshippl. S* Bas-
singborne Gawdee
at Harling
*Sir Christopher Heydon, who died in 1623, was one of the last great cham-
pions of  the occult sciences, and had for his correspondent, William  Bredon,
a profound divine, Vicar of  Thornton in  Buckinghamshire, and ‘  absolutely
the most polite person for nativities in that age.”  Bredon was Sir Christopher's
chaplain, and is said to have assisted the knight  in the composition of his  « De-
fence of Judicial Astrology,”
—* a work,” as Wood observes, «of  no  common
reading, and carried on with no mean acquirements.” (Athena Oxonienses, Vol.1,
p. 475.) Of Bredon it is reported that he was so inordinately given over to tobacco,
that when no more of the fragrant weed was left to him,  he did  not  scruple to
mutilate his bell ropes and smoke them.

## p. 851 ##

There is a small portrait engraved of one John Heydon, with his arms,—the
cross engrailed, &c.    He was born in 1629, and is called Eques, a title to which
Granger says he had no right.  (Biographical Hist. of England, vol. 4, p. 108.)
Granger's account of him is very curious.   He appears to have been in low cir-
cumstances, and living by his wits,  or rather,  I   suppose, by  the want of wit in
those who consulted him.    Lord Clarendon, in the ¢     Continuation of his Life,”
says : “ There was a poor fellow, who had a poor lodging about Tower-hill, and
professed skill in horoscopes, to whom the Duke (of Buckingham) often resorted
in disguise, &c.”   This poor fellow, as appears from Carte’s “ Life of the Duke
of Ormond,” was Heydon.
But  who  was this John Heydon?   He  could not have been the son of
Sir Christopher, nor is it hardly possible that he could have been his brother;
yet the arms prove  him  to  have been of  the  family.   He  was a voluminous
‘author, and Granger gives the curious titles of several of his books.
Note  134.—GoURNAY FAMILY—ANTHONY GURNEY. (page 414).
La famille de Gournay subsistoit encore en Angleterre dans le seizieme siecle,
‘car  on voit dans le chartrier d’Angleterre, sous le regne de Henry viii. en 1520,
-une liste ou etat de ceux qui avoient été commis par ce roy pour examiner ceux
qui  devoient accompagner le  roy Francois premier a l'entrevue que ces deux
‘'monarques devoient avoir ensemble, et au nombre de ces commissaires on trouve
‘le surnom d'un de Gournay dont le nom n'est point exprimé.                       |
M.S. Histoire  des Seigneurs de Gournay :—
amongst the collateral descendants.
This was possibly Anthony Gurney, whose connexion with the Court through
- Sir Thomas Lovell, his wife’s uncle, might lead to his being a ‘  Commissaire” on
this  occasion.
Anthony Gurney was born about the year 1491, as appears by the account
of his burial at Great Ellingham in Henry Gurnay’s Register Book.
“ Anthony Gurnay, Esquire, lord of the towne, buried in the North Chappell
there & in the midst thereof overthwart y® dore entring into  the  church tow”
y* South.  Ja. 1555, about his age of 63.”

## p. 852 ##

This would be Jan.,  1556,  new style, and he must have been of age in 1511,
when he presented to the Rectory of Harpley.   It appears moreover by an inquisi-
tion taken at Norwich, 13th Henry VIII. (1522), that Anthony Gurney was then
more than 31 years old,  and that he was therefore eighteen years old in 1507,
when his grandfather William Gurney IV. died.   So that he was in full posses-
sion of  his  estates on coming of age.  I  do not find in what year he married,
but his son Francis was born in 1521; and Margaret Lovell his wife was 26 years
old on 30th October, 18 Henry VIII (1526), therefore born in the year 1500.—
—(Inquisition  of  that date.—Dodsworth’s Collections, No.  154.—Bodleian
Library.)
He obtained a considerable estate with his wife, Margaret Lovell, particularly
after the death of her relation, Henry Spelman, Esq., of Great Ellingham, in
1524.   He  lived  at  Irstead  and  West  Barsham  chief part  of  his  life,  or  at
Bury  Hall,  in  Great Ellingham.   He  did  not begin to  build  the house at
Great Ellingham until 1553, as appears by Henry Gurnay’s Register.  He was
then married to Elizabeth Tyrrell,  his second wife,  who,  by some means, had
Bury Hall manor, in Great Ellingham,  for her life,—as I  conjecture, from her
having been the widow of Henry Spelman, Esq.  It is stated by Henry Gurnay
that the reason why his  grandfather, Anthony Gurney, built the hall at Great
Ellingham was because he had only his  wife's life  interest in Bury Hall.   No
house had been maintained for the space of 120 years on the site of  the Great
Hall ;  but the place was overgrown with bushes and trees. But his wife Elizabeth
died before he had  finished the building,  and himself within  four years after
his first beginning to build ; so that the undertaking was completed by his grand-
son and heir, Henry Gurnay I.
“ These buildings were chifly of ould timber of the remaines of Skoulton Hall,
pulled down by the said Ant’. after he p'ceived that  S'. Rich’.  Sothwell uppon
an  unadvised price  sett  theron  would  recouer it by an  assumpsit.   The  best
timber to be had first give and sould as the sparrs of the haule to the re-edifying
of Hengham Chancell.”
Considering the large estate possessed by Anthony Gurney, and that from
early life,  I  am surprised that the mansion he erected  was not of a more costly
description.   It  still exists,—in some measure, no doubt, altered by subsequent

## p. 853 ##

proprietors, but could never have been more than a common manor house, sur-
rounded by a moat.
The fact also of his never having been High  Sheriff of Norfolk and Suffolk,
or  knighted, is  remarkable, especially considering his  relationship to  Ann
Boleyn, and still  more as Sir Thomas Lovell,  his wife's uncle, was in  high
favour with the family of Tudor.
He certainly inherited Gournay’s place in Norwich, a large and valuable man-
sion, also the manors of Harpley, West Barsham, Hingham Gurney’s, Swathings
in Hardingham, a manor in Baconsthorpe, another in Denver, also Depeden in
Suffolk,andIrstead inNorfolk,and with it the appertenances of  Barton,Nettysherd,
Dilham, Tunstead, Smallborough and Honing; the manor of Neatsherd was appa-
rently held by lease from the abbot of St. Benet's Holme for forty years, from
25th. Henry VIII. (1534).   In addition to this his wife brought him the princi-
pal  manor in  Great Ellingham and Mortimer’s manor in Scoulton;  also the
patronage of the lesser rectory at Attleborough; all these inherited by her from the
Mortimers.   The whole of these would in  modern times have produced a very
considerable rental, and must have placedAnthony Gurney, in his period, amongst
the principal landed proprietors of Norfolk;  but there are many indications of
"his baving been far from a man of prudent habits ;—the building a new mansion
at Great Ellingham, having already two (at West Barsham and Irstead) if  not

## p. 854 ##

more, bears the appearance of this,  and that he was in some measure in difficul-
ties is proved by the sales of his property.  He sold Gournay’s place in Norwich
to Thomas  Gawdy of  Shottisham, Gentleman, in 1st. Edward VI.  (1548), and
alienated Swathings in Hardingham, Denver, Baconsthorpe, Depeden in Suffolk
and Scoulton, which last he sold to Sir Robert Southwell, in 1540.  (Blomefield,
Vol. II,  346).   Gurney’s manor in Harpley appears to have been settled on the
issue of his second marriage, and  so passed away through Elizabeth, the only
child of that marriage, to the families of Stubbs and Yelverton.
Thus the inheritance of the Gurneys in Norfolk became materially diminished,
and Henry Gurnay, Anthony's grandson and heir, marrying early, and having a
very large family of thirteen children, was necessarily in very straitened circum-
stances, as he himself states.
Anthony Gurney died on 13th. January, 1555, O.S., and was buried at Great
Ellingham, as stated above.
Note 135.—ANTHONY  GURNEY   AND   ELIZABETH   HIS   WIFE.  (page 417.)

From this it seems probable that Sir John Tirrell, Knight,of Gipping, was either
father-in-law or brother-in-law of Anthony Gurney through his second wife. . ’

## p. 855 ##

Note 136.—PEDIGREES  BY   Cook.  (page 417.)—Note b.
This is  not  correctly stated :  the  volume in  question contains pedigrees of
about twenty Norfolk families; outside is written by Cook Clarenceaux, but he
died in 1592, and the pedigrees are later;  that of the Gurneys was made in 1622,
as appears in the notice of  Sir  Thomas Gurney,   this year Sheriff of Essex ;”
but a later hand states Sir Thomas died and was buried in 1631.   On  reference
to the Herald's College, Sir  Charles G. Young states this book of  pedigrees to
be in  the hand writing  of  John Tilston, a  collector, who lived in the reign of
Charles II.

Note 138.—INQUISITION  ON   THE   DEATH   OF   ANTHONY  GURNEY. (page 423.)
Inquisition was taken at Walsingham, on  the 27th of March, in the 2° and
3" years of Philip andMary, (1556) by virtue of a Brief directed to the Eschea-
tor of Norfolk, to inquire what lands were held by Anthony Gurney at the time
of his death.  And  the Jurors find  that he did  not  hold  any manors, lands,
tenements or hereditaments in  the County of  the King and Queen in  Capite or
otherwise.  That he was seised of  the manor of Irsted with its appertenances,
and that by deed bearing date the 28th. of May, the 35® Henry VIIL., (1544),
he demised it to Francis Gurney, his son and heir, and Ellen, one of the daugh-
ters of Robert Holdyche, Esq., whom Francis was about to marry, with all its
appertenances in  Irsted,  Barton, Netisherde, Dilham, Tunsted, Smalboroughe,

## p. 856 ##

Honyng,  and Beston, to be held by  the said Francis and Ellen, and their lawful
issue.  That Francis and Ellen had issue Henry Gurney their eldest son, and
other children, and that Francis Gurney died seised of the manor of Irsted, and
Ellen his wife survived him.   That the manor of Irsted was worth £12 per an-
num, and  was held  of  the  Bishop of  Norwich, but  by  what service the Jurors
knew not.    And farther, that Anthony Gurney died  seised also of the manor of
Ellingham, in  Great Ellingham, and of West Barsham,  and  of  a  manor called
Gurney’s manor in Hyngham.   And that he died on the 14th. day of January,*
last past, seised of the aforesaid manors.   And that Henry Gurney was his next
heir, being son and heir of Francis, who was the son of the said Anthony.   And
that Henry Gurney was seven years old when the Inquisition was taken.  And
the Jurors say farther, that the manor of Elyngham was worth £14 per annum,
and was held of  the heirs of  Robert lord  Bardolff in soccage. And that the
manor of West Barsham was worth £33, and was held of Henry lord Maltravers
and Anne his wife, as of their manor of Castleacre, by the service of one knight's
fee.  And that  the manor of  Gurneys was worth £7 per annum, and was held
of the heirs of Henry  Clyppesby, but  by what service the Jurors know not.
And that Ellen Gurney was yet living at Ranworthe.
Abridged from the original in  the Public Re-
cords in  the custody of  the Master of the

part 2, No. 46.
Upon the coming of  age of Henry Gurney, grandson and heir of  Anthony
Gurney, on the 21st January, the 12th. Elizabeth, (1569), a commission of en-
quiry was issued from the crown, and an Inquisition held  at  Holt, on the 10th.
of April following, to find what estates were held by Anthony Gurney at the time
of his death.  And the Jurors say that he was seised of the manor of Great El-
lingham, otherwise called Ellingham Hall manor in Great Ellingham, Rokeland,
Depham, Morley,  Little Ellingham, Hingham, and Attleborough, and of the
advowson of a part of  the church  at Attleborough ;  also of 60 acres of meadow
and pasture land in  Great Ellingham,  Depham, Attleborough, Hingham and
Rokeland;  and also of the manors of West Barsham, Southall, and Gantes Halle
with  their appertenances in  West Barsham, North  Barsham, East Barsham,
¢    i.e., 1565, old style, the new year beginning then 25th March, two days before this inquisition was taken.

## p. 857 ##

Houghton .  . .  .  . ,  and Skoulthorpe.  Moreover of  the manor of  Gurnays
in Hingham . . . . ..    Little Ellingham and Depham with its appertenances.
That he was also seised of  a tenement called Croffts with  its appertenances in
Hingham;  and of four acres of meadow, eight of pasture, and ten of arable land
in Hingham.   And the Jurors further say that the manor of  Great Ellingham
with its appertenances was held of Philip and Mary late King and Queen, as of
their honour of Wyringegaye (Wirmegay) by half a knight's fee, and was worth
£20 per  annum.    And  that  the  aforesaid manor of  West Barsham, Southalle,
and Gantes Halle with their appertenances were held of the Earl of Arundell as
of his manor of Castleacre, by  what service the Jurors know not, and was worth
£20 per annum.   And that  the aforesaid manor of  Gurneys with its apperte-
nances was held of Henry lord Morley, as of  his manor of Hingham, by what
service the Jurors know not, and that it  was worth 50s. per annum.  And that
the tenement callod Croffts in Hingham, and the four acres of meadow, eight of
pasture, and  ten of  arable land were held  of  lord  Morley, as of his manor of
Hingham, by what service the Jurors know not, and was worth £4 per annum.
And  the Jurors farther say that  Anthony Gurney died on  the 13th. day of
January, 1555, and that Henry Gurney was his heir, being the son of Francis
Gurney, deceased, who was son of the said Anthony.   And that the said Henry
when the Inquisition was taken was of the age of 21 years.  And that Anthony
Gurney held no other lands or tenements of the Queen in capite or otherwise.

Note 139.—ALICE GURNEY. (page 425.)
It  seems doubtful whether Alice Gurney, who married Dengayne, was not aunt
to Anthony Gurney, and not  sister.  The old book of Norfolk pedigrees, de-
scribed at page 855, gives Alice  as daughter of William Gurney IV. ;  and this
is somewhat confirmed by two original deeds now in my possession.
By the first of these, dated 24th. May, 15th. Edward IV., (1475) John Den-
gayne, son and heir of Alice Dengayne, lately widow of Robert Fulman, Esq.,

## p. 858 ##

conveys to William Gurney, Esq. and William Dengayne, Gentleman, the ma-
nors of Parker, Beketon, and Garners, &ec., in Brunstead, and land in Brunstead,
Stalham, Ingham and Estryston in Happyng hundred.
By the second, dated 14th February, 9th. Henry VII., (1493), William Den-
gayne, Esq., conveys the same manor and lands to Thomas Lovell, Knight of
the body to the king, Henry Heydon, Knight, William Gurney, Esq., and John
Brunstead.
Note 140.—AMY GURNEY. (page 426.)
Amy Gurney, according to Harl. MS., 1552, p. 157°, married George Sybsey
of Boston, whose son John Sybsey married Elizabeth Heneage of Stainton, and
resided at West Barsham, being described of that place in the Herald's visitation.
He probably rented the house and demean lands there.
According to Harl. MS. 4755, fo. 83% her name was Anne, as follows :—
‘  George Sybsaye of Boston, Gent., married Ann daughter to Gurney, sister
to Anthony Gurney, and had John his sonne and heire, Elenor, Alice married to
Will. Bleverhasset of Norwich, Gent.”
“ John Sybsaye of West Barsham, in Norff., Gent., sonne and heire to George,
cossen and heire to William Thorpe of Thorpe, in Lincolnsh., Gent., &ec.
Sybsay bore arg. on a bend azure, 5 crosses, pate¢ or.
Sibsey is a parish near Boston, from which this family doubtless originated.
It appears by Pishey Thompson's history of Boston, (Ed. 1856,) p. 229, that
there still exists a street in Boston called Sybsey-lane, where the Sybseys for-
merly possessed a mansion-house, garden and great orchard called Sibsey Place.
This family flourished as merchants at Boston from an early period down to the

## p. 859 ##

eighteenth century.  In 1338, George Sibsey was chamberlain of  the Corpus
Christi guild;  in 1461,  William de Sibsey was a member, and alderman of the
guild in  1492;   George Sibsey, of Boston, Gentleman, was member of the guild
in 1533; and Amya  Sibsey is mentioned in a subsidy roll  for the year 1547.
This last must be Amy the daughter of William Gurney V.
Note 141.—WiLL  oF  ELIZABETH  BRANDON. (page 427.)
See this will, given in Nicholas’s Testamenta Vetusta, vol. IL., p. 432; and at
‘page 497, is  the  will  of Sir  Thomas Brandon, Knight, who mentions his  “  sister
Catherine Gorney.”
It is not likely  that the Gurney who married Brandon was Henry Gurney
brother  of  Anthony,  as his  date is  too  late;  and  moreover he is called John
Gournay of  Levington, in  Suffolk, Esq., in  Add. MSS. 19, 120, Brit. Mus. ;
and was probably the Sir  John Gournay, Knight, mentioned at  p. 426, whose
daughter married John Trye, according to Lysons.   In Add. MSS. 19, 120, fo.
51, is a pedigree of  the heirs of  Charles Brandon, and his relations amongst
whom his estates were chiefly divided, on the death of his son Henry Duke of
Suffolk.   In  this  pedigree amongst  others  occurs Catherine Brandon, aunt of
Charles Duke of Suffolk, and the issue of her marriage with   .  .  .  .  .  Gurney :
thus—
CHRISTINA, 1 fil. et coher nupt. Dar-   ELIZABETH,      2  fil.  et  coh.   ELIZABETH,     (2nd of the name ?)
nell, et  eum  supervixit  et  vidua  est   nupt.  Walter  Ayscough  et    3 fil. et coh.  nupt. John  Trye et
This pedigree is  taken from an Inquisition on the death of the last Brandon,
Duke of Suffolk, now in the Rolls’ Chapel.
Quazre whether  this John  Gurney of  Levington could  be  a  son of Thomas
Gurney II., and Margaret Jernegan, mentioned in his father’s will, dated 1471 ?
See p. 397.   He had an annuity out of the manor of Depden.  Levington came
to John Gurney probably from the Brandons.

## p. 860 ##

Note 142.—MORTIMER OF  ATTLEBOROUGH.  (page 428.)
By Harleian MSS. 1560,*  fo. 308%, also 1449,  fo. 100,  and the  Lansdown
MSS. No. 205, fol. 45%, it appears that Robert de Mortemer, who was the ances-
tor  of the Mortimers of  Attleborough, was son of William de Mortemer,Lord of
Bec-crespin, near Havre, in Normandy.   The fact of the Mortimers having been
Lords of Bec-crespin is confirmed in Duplessis’s Upper Normandy, vol. 2,  p.
326, by  which it appears that this place  was sometimes called Bec-Mortemer.+
It  seems probable from  this   that  the Mortimers  of  Attleborough  were quite a
distinct race from the Mortimers afterwards Earls of March, who were Lords of
Mortemer-Sur-Aulne. I  do not possess any  local history of that part of Upper
Normandy in which Bec-Mortemer is situated, except that of Toussaint Duplessis,
who merely mentions the fact of the name,  which, however, is a strong proof of
the Harleian manuscript being correct.
The late Mr. Stapleton, the learned editor of the Norman Exchequer Roll, in
a notice of the Mortemers of Attleborough, states that  it    is evident this manor
of Attleborough was held in 1165 by William d’Aubigny, the king’s butler, an-
cestor of the Earls  of   Arundel,  by   a  vassal  who  had the local surname, viz.,
Alured de Atleburc, who held two knight's fiefs before the 26th Henry II. (1180.)
These fiefs were vested in Robert de Mortemer, as we learn from this entry in the
Pipe Rolls  and elsewhere: “Norff.  &  Suff.—Alexander filius  Willelmi  de
Rougham debet xI* ut placitis in curia Regis versus comitem de Arundel  et Ro-
bertum de Mortemer.”  Mr. Stapleton possessed a copy of the charter of Hugh
de Mortemer, son of Ralph, and grandson of Roger, the founder of the abbey of
St. Victor-en-Caux, in  1074, confirming the endowment to  which Hugh  and
William, his sons, and William,  his  brother,  and Robert and Ralph,  his sons,
are witnesses,    This  charter is  probably as early as the reign of Stephen; and
Mr. Stapleton identified this Robert (son of William)  with the Robert de Mor-
temer who acquired the  two  fiefs  at  Attleborough,  either by marriage or  new
feofment ; most likely the latter.   Parkin says that William de Mortemer held the
manor of Raveningham under the Earls  Warren before the reign of Henry II.
®   The first of these manuscripts, viz., No. 1560, is described in the Indexes of the Harleian collection as
‘  an heraldic book, in folio, written and tricked by  Mr. Richard Mundy,”  and containing     pedigrees,&c.,
of Suffolk families.  The second manuscript, No.  1449, is described as * an heraldic book,   written and
tricked by Jacob Chaloner, the peinter stainer,” chiefly concerning Suffolk families.  The Lansdown MS.,
No. 205, is * a volume of heraldical and historical collections formerly belonging to Elias Ashmole.”
t  It  was also called St. Martin-du bec.

## p. 861 ##

We may suppose his son was Robert de Mortemer, the aforesaid lord of Attle-
borough.   All this confirms the Harleian and LansdownMSS. as to Robert be-
ing son of William (lord of  Bec-crespin, who also held Raveningham, in Norfolk),
and goes far in identifying the Norfolk Mortemers with those of Wales.
Mr. Stapleton considered that the pedigree of these Welch Mortemers, given
at page 429, from Dugdale, was incorrect,  inasmuch as a generation  must have
been omitted, from the dates; it being improbable that Roger de Mortemer, who
died in 1215, could be grandson of Raoul de Mortemer, who came to England
with the Conqueror. He thinks  there were two Hugh de Mortemiers between
them.
There are,  at what is now  called  Attleborough Hall,  the  remains of the old
fortified mansion of the Mortimers.   It was defended by a deep square moat,
still existing, the side of which internally was strengthened by a buttressed wall,
much of which still remains.  Apparently, one, if not two, drawbridges formed
the entrance into the moated square, in  which  has now been built a compara-~
tively modern house by the more recent proprietors, the Bickleys or Ashes, who
possessed it by purchase.  There are ancient buildings, probably stables and
barn, outside the moat;   also  stew  ponds for  fish,  and some old timber  trees,
giving the appearance of a former park.

Note 143.—FaMiLY oF  Frrzrarr. (page 439—also 706.)
I have said that it is not clear how the family of Fitzralf ended in co-heiresses,
one of  whom was Maude wife of  Sir Robert Conyers.  Some light is thrown
upon this by a memorandum regarding the marriage of  John Fitzralf and Alice
‘Walesburgh, (hereafter to be printed in  this Supplement), contained in Henry
Gurnay’s Register Book*;  by this it appears that John Fitzralf, the son of John
®   No. 175, Tauner MSS., Bodleian Library.

## p. 863 ##

Fitzralf and Margaret Mortimer was twice married—first to the daughter of the
Lady  Bouchier, by  whom he had  no  issue,  and  secondly to Julian Broun, by
whom he had children.  Robert Fitzralf, the brother of this John, ¢    noised about
that he  should  become heir  to  his  brother and not the children,” upon which
John Fitzralf, by advice of council, examined the evidences of the Fitzralf and
Mortimer estates, of which none were entailed but  the manor of Finchingfield
in Essex, which  by some means he alienated.  By  the settlement given at page
707, the manors of Scoulton, Great Ellingham and Tottington (erroneously
written Pottingto)n, were settled on John Fitzralfe, junior, and Alice Walesbo-
rough his wife.   It appears a difference had arisen about this  settlement, and
eventually it was agreed by the Earl of  Suffolk (whose Countess was cousin of
Alice Walesborough), that  he was to  pay John Fitzralf the father, 400  marks
for a settlement in  tail  male; and in default of male issue from the marriage,
Maude, Joanna and Alice, the daughters of John Fitzralf and Julian Broun, were
to inherit.    This eventually took effect.   John Fitzralf,junior, and Altice Wales-
borough had issue a son, who died young, 8. P., and a daughter Elizabeth, who
married Sir Robert Chamberlain, knight, and had the manor of Bernham Broom
only, of the inheritance of the Mortimers.  Maude Fitzralf  inherited the manors
of Scoulton and Great Ellingham and Tottington, which passed through her to
the Conyerss, Lovells and Gurneys.
It might be conjectured from all this that   John Fitzralf’s second marriage with
Julian Broun was with  a person below his degree, and possibly the son of this
marriage (John  Fitzralf, junior, who married Alice Walesborough), was not le-
gitimate ;  in consequence Robert Fitzralf, the  brother, « noised about,” that  he
was the heir.  Hence, also, the payment of 400 marks for  the settlement by the
Earl of Suffolk; and moreover the terms of the settlement, which were favourable
to the daughters of John Fitzralf  and Julian Broun, who werelegitimate.
If  this  conjecture is  correct, it aceounts for the  Gurneys of West Barsham
quartering the arms of Mortimer and Fitzralf, of which Maude was thus the real
co-heiress in blood, but  the case is not a clear one.
In the Carlton Ride depository is an inquisition on the death of John Fitzralf,
Junior,  which  seems to  militate  against the   supposition  of  his  being  ille-
gitimate.

## p. 864 ##

By this inquisition, which was taken at Thetford, in  the county  of Norfolk,
on Monday after the  Feast of the 11,000  virgins, 18 Edward  IV.,  A.D.  1478,
before John Jenny  and  James Hobart,  Esquires,  the  King's  Commissioners,
and a  jury,  it  was found that  Sir  John  Fitzralph,  Knight,  and  Margery
his wife, daughter and one of  the heirs of  Sir Robert Mortimer, Knight,  and
cousin* and heir of Sir Robert Mortimer, Knight, father of the said Sir Thomas,
and one of the heirs of Sir  Constantine Mortimer, Knight,  grandfather of  the
said Sir Thomas, were seized of  the manor of  Skulton, and certain lands and
hereditaments,  parcel  or  members  of  the  manors   of  Tottington,  Castleacre,
Great Ellingham and Wormegay Honor, in  the county of Norfolk,  in  fee
tail, that is to say, the said Sir  John Fitzralph in right of  the said ‘Margery
his  wife  was seized to  him  and the  heirs of  the body  of the said Mar-
gery  Fitzralph issuing,  that  the  said  Sir  John  Fitzralph  and  Margery  his
wife had issue John Fitzralph, Esq., and that they died seized of the said estate
in tail, whereupon the said John Fitzralph, Esq., their son, became seized in fee
tail of the said manors and premises, and having issue John Fitzralph, junior,
his son, died so seized thereof, whereupon the said John Fitzralph,  junior, be-
came seized in fee tail as aforesaid of the same hereditaments, and having issue,
viz., Elizabeth Fitzralph, then the wife of Sir Robert Chamberlain, Knight, died
seized of such estate ;  whereupon the  said Sir Robert Chamberlain, and Eliza-
beth his  wife, entered into  possession of the premises, and became seized
thereof for an estate tail,  in  the right  of  the said Elizabeth Fitzralph.   That
the said John Fitzralph, junior,  the father of the said Elizabeth Chamberlain,
died on the Feast of  Saint Clement the Pope, 24th Henry 6th, A.D. 1445, and the
said Elizabeth Chamberlain was his daughter and heir, and cousin and heir of
the said John Fitzralph, senior, Esq., and also cousin and  heir  of  the said Sir
John Fitzralph and Margery his  wife, and also cousin and heir respectively ‘of
Sir Thomas Mortimer, Sir Robert Mortimer, Sir Robert Mortimer, Sir Constan-
tine Mortimer, and that the said Elizabeth Chamberlain was then aged 30 years
and upwards,

The will of John Fitzralph, Esq., is dated at Scoulton, on the 12th. July, 1440.
By it he desires to be buried in the church  there, and after bequeathing numer-
ous small sums to  various religious establishments, he leaves to the Lady Alice
his daughter, a nun at Thetford, five marks;  to the Lady Matilda his  sister, a
nun at Brusyerd, 20s.;   to  Thomas Fitzralph his brother,  5 marks;  to Julian
his  wife, all  his  silver  cups,  utensils, jewels,  &c.,  and appoints her executrix
together with   Sir   Thomas Tuddenham, Robert  Mortimer  and  William
Warner of Thompson, Esq. and John Holderness.  The will was proved in the
Episcopal Consistorial Court  of  Norwich on  the  4th  of  August, 1440.—
Register Doke, fol. 125°
The will of Julian Fitzralph is dated on the 15th of July, 1445, and in it she
describes herself as the relict of John Fitzralph, Esquire,  deceased, and desires
to be buried in the chancel of the church at Scoulton, next the tomb of her hus-
band ;  and she leaves to the Lady Matilda Fitzralph,  nun at Brusyerd, 6s. 8d.;
#   See Trevelyan Papers, published by the Camden Society, pages 87 and 81.

## p. 866 ##

and to the Lady Alice Fitzralph, nun at Thetford,  100s., and an annuity of —
silver marks per annum for life;  to Matilda Conyers, her daughter, 1 paxbrede,*
2 silver crosses, 2 silver candles, &c.;  to Joan Hotot, her daughter, and Robert
Hotot, her son, certain silver articles in the will named ; also a legacy to Robert
Fitzralph, her nephew.  This  will  was proved in  the Episcopal Consistorial
Court of Norwich on the 11th of April, 1446.—Register Wylby.
NoTE   144,—CONYERS OF  FINNINGHAM IN  SUFFOLK.
In Harl. MS. 805, fol. 195, is a translation of  an inquisition  taken in  the
Court at Durham, in  the reign of Henry IL. regarding the right inheritance of
Roger Conyers of  Sockbourne, in  the County of Durham;  by this it appears
that the said Roger Conyers was lord of Finningham in Suffolk, which proves
incontestably that the Conyerss of Suffolk were a branch of those settled in  the
County of Durham from the period of the Conquest. This inquisition is given
at length in Surtees’ History of Durham, vol. 3, page 407.   Finningham is erro-
neously written Rimingham, there being no such place in Suffolk.
The document is as follows :—
CoNYERS EBOR.
Henry by  the  grace of  God  to  the  Justices, Sheriffs, Ministers, and all his
faithfully [sic] of all Yorkshire and Durham and Haliwarefolk, Gieeting.  Know
ye that we have inquired by Barons and Knights in the Court of Durham, the
right inheritance of Roger Conyers, of which  Ranulph  the  Bishop  of Durham
enfeoffed Roger, the father of the same Roger.  To wit in Hoton,Norton,Holme
 Paxbread,—a 
small tablet, with a representation of the crucifixionupon it, presentedinthe ceremo
Of  THE  HOUSE OF GOURNAY.                                 867
and Holgrave for one Knight's fee.  And Ringtun, Grisby and Didenhale for
one Knight's fee;  and Bishopton and Stainture and Sockburne for one Knight's
fee.   And Elinchit, which he holds of the Honour of Bramepath [Brancepeth],
and West Auckland, and Evenwood, and Morlay, and Mayland for one Knight's
fee.   And Bedlington and Bedlingtonshire for two Knight's fees.  And Riming-
ham [Finningham}, in  Suffolk, which he holds of the Honor of Crayk [Clare],
for half  a Knight's  fee.  These aforesayd lands with the appurtenances by  an
inquisition which we have made by the foresayd Barons and Knights, etc., we
have granted and confirmed to the foresayd Roger Conyers and to  his heires,
by our Charter in  fee and in  inheritance, freely, quietly and honorably to be
holden and doing forraine services to the Bishop of Durham ofte [sic] his other
Lords.   We  forbid that any molest or implead the foresayd Roger or his heirs
of the foresayd tenements without  our  command upon  a great forfaiture to us.
These being witnesses Lucy then the King’s  Justice,* Reginald Erle of Corn-
wall, Richard Humet and others.
Surtees gives a pedigree of the Conyerss of Sockborne, in the county of Dur-
ham, from which, and from the Davy Suffolk papers, now in the British Museum,
vol. 13, the descent of the Conyerss of Finningham may be partially  collected.
Blomefield is not wholly  correct in his account of  this family  (vol. 1, p. 482).
I  am indebted to that excellent volume, the Supplement to the Suffolk Traveller,
by Mr. Augustine Page, for information respecting them (pages 462 and 704).
As far as I have been able to arrange it, the pedigree of the Conyerss of Finning-
ham appears to be as given in the next page.

## p. 869 ##

Note 145.—THE THREE   LoVELLS.  (page 441.)
Whether or net the Lovells of Barton Bendish were of the same race as the
Lords  Lovell  of  Titchmarsh  is  uncertain,  and  the  more so as the name  was
originally  a nickname which  might  apply  to   many  persons, Lovell  being
Lupellus, or the Little Wolf,  probably from the voracious appetite and savage
conduct of the person to whom the name was given in the first instance.  Hugh
Lupus, the great Earl of Chester, in the reign of William the Conqueror, was so
called from his being abandoned to  the grossest sensuality.—Ventris ingluviei
serviebat; unde nimi  crassitiei pondere pregravatus, vix ire potuit.   (Order.
Vit., p. 598, Maseres 253.)—See Dr.  Lappenberg’s Anglo-Norman Kings, by
Thorpe, p. 137.
The three brothers, Sir Gregory, Sir Robert and Sit Thomas Lovell,were amongst
the most eminent Knights of the age in which they lived.   Sir Thomas was the
youngest, and was seated at Ryhall, in the county of Rutland, in the capacity of
esquire of the body to Henrv VII.   He attended him at the battle of Bosworth,
and  on  the  meeting  of  the  first  Parliament of this  King at Westminster, 7th.
November,  1486,  Mr.  Lovell,  having  been  elected  one  of  the Knights of  the
shire for Northamptonshire, was chosen Speaker of the House of Commons. In
the next year occurred Lambert Simnel’s plot, and the battle of Stoke, in which
the Speaker and his two brothers distinguished themselves so much that Sir
Gregory and Sir Robert were made Knights banneret on the field of battle, and
Thomas, who  had  continued esquire of  the King’s  body,  was knighted,  and
shortly after was made a Knight of the Garter.  In 1483 Sir Thomas Lovell was
appointed Chancellor of the Exchequer for life;  and in  1502, Treasurer of the
Household, and sworn  of  the Privy Council ;  and was one of the executors of
Henry VII.   Under Henry VIII. he was appointed Constable of the Tower of
London, Surveyor of the Court of Wards, and Steward of the Household.  He
refounded the nunnery of  Halliwell,  in the parish of Shoreditch, London, and
died at Enfield 25th. May, 1524.  He was buried under a tomb of white mar-
ble in the Nunnery Church, Halliwell.
|  Manning's Lives of the Speakers, p. 127.
The will of Sir Thomas Lovell is given in Nicholas’s Testamenta Vetusta, vol.
IL, p. 640.   He  bequeaths the bulk of  his property to  Sir Francis Lovell  of
Herling, son of his brother Sir Gregory Lovell.

## p. 870 ##

On the wall of Halliwell Priory was inscribed :—
“ All ye nuns of Halliwell
Pray ye both day and night
For the soul of Sir Thomas Lovell
Whom Harry the Seventh made Knight.”
Sir Thomas Lovell built East Herling Hall, on a tower of which his arms still
remained in  the time of Blomefield, and a brass bust of his own likeness, sur-
rounded with  the garter.                           Blomefield, vol L., p. 322—324.

In Add. MSS., Brit. Mus., 5522, fol. 164, is a collection of Arms at different
places in Norfolk, among the rest at Hunstanton, where were the following :—
Strange sydeth L.Strange                           Strange & Hebert
Strange sydeth Risbrooke                           Strange & Gurney
Strange sydeth Calthorpe                           Strange & Ingoldthorp
Strange & Drury                                        Strange & Vaux
Strange & Heydon                                      Strange & Bell
Strange & Paston                                    Strange & Walkfare

## p. 872 ##

By this it appears that the  coat referred to  in the  note at page 450 was not
a mistake, but  that some marriage had taken place between the two families;
Heydon being also mentioned, as well as Ingoldesthorpe, both of which families
bore engrailed crosses. The date of the Arms given in the MS. seems to be of
the 15th. century ;  and there is a deed  at  Hunstanton, of Alianor, the widow of
Sir Hamon Strange, dated 4 Nov., 7 Henry V. (1419), with her seal, Strange
impaling a cross engrailed.  The deed respects the manor of Mustrells in Hun-
stanton, as is described as follows—“Counterpart of indenture of feoffment from
Sir John Colvyle, Sir John Ingoldesthorpe, John Drew, Clerk, and Henry Not-
yngham to Aleanor Strange, late the wife of Sir Hamon Straunge, of a manor in
Hunstanton called Mustrells,” &c., &c.     Witnesses :  John Wodhouse, Esq.,
William Shelton, Esq. and John Strange, Esq.—Dated at Hunstanton.
Seal of red wax bearing an escutcheon charged with the Arms of Strange, im-.
paling a cross engrailed.—Circumscribed “ Sigillum Dne. Alianore Strange.”
There is also a letter of Attorney from the same parties to Robert Wylly  of
Docking, and William Kelleworth of  Snettisham, their  Attorneys, to  deliver
seizin of the manor of Mustrells in Hunstanton, with the appurtenents, to Alianor

## p. 873 ##

Strange, late the wife of Sir Hamon Straunge.—Same date as the previous deed.
.  This Alianor Strange, widow, may have been a Gurney.  John Drew, one of
the feoffees in the deed, was rector of Harpley at this time, which is in favour of
this conjecture. On the other hand, John de Ingoldesthorpe being another feof-
fee, is in favor of her being of that family, which also bore an engrailed cross.
By the Hunstanton deeds, this Alianor Strange, widow, appears to have mar-
ried 2ndly. Sir Philip Radford, Knight, whose daughter, by an ancient pedigree
at Hunstanton, married  Hamon  Strange, 3rd. son  of  John  Strange and Alice
Beaumont.
It is singular that the pedigrees of the Lestranges do not mention Sir Hamon
Lestrange, the husband of Alianore, who bore the engrailed cross; probably be-
cause he died s. p. and v. p.
The descent seems to be thus :—
