Note 147.—FAMILY  OF  STUBBS  OR  STUBBE. (page 451.)
This family took their name from a place called Stubbs, in Loddon.   Henry
de Stubbs had an interest in  Charles’s manor in Loddon, which he surrendered
by fine 24th. Henry III. to Jeffry de Lodne.   Geffry de Stubbs gave lands in
Loddon to Langley Abbey.  Henry de Stubbs, son of Adam, conveyed lands in
Stubbs to Geoffry de Lodne, with rents, &c.
Blomefield, in Lodden, vol. X., p. 155.
Richard Stubbe, Esq., was one of the burgesses in Parliament for Thetford in

## p. 874 ##

«          Note 148.—FauiLY or  HoLDICHE. (page 452.)
This family appears to have been of much consideration for some generations.
The remains of their residence, Ranworth Hall, suggest the idea that it was origi-
nally a very fine mansion.
I have stated at page 447, that Francis Gurney, who married Helen Holditch,
was buried at Irsted; but by Henry Gurney's register it seems this is a mistake,
as he was interred at Great Eliingham ;  and the slab at Irsted ascribed to him by
Mr. Norris, was probably in memory of William  Gurney V. who married Ann
Heydon and received with her the manor of Irsted as her portion,

## p. 875 ##

Note 149.—HENRY GURNAY  I.  (page 454.)
In the Bodleian Library at Oxford, the Common Place Book or Register of
Henry Gurnay I., has been found amongst the Tanner MSS., marked 175, and
called in  the catalogue “ Account of  Courts held and Leases at  Ellingham,
Hingham, and Irstede, in the county of Norfolk, with a poem called the Anatomy
of Popery, &c.”   Bishop Tanner was Bishop of St. Asaph, but had previously
held preferment in Norfolk, and his third wife was a Norfolk woman.   His MS.
collections contain  very  much  respecting our county,  in which he had resided
for some years, which accounts  for his having possession of  Henry  Gurnay’s
Register and other Norfolk  manuscripts. The Register itself is a thick  small
quarto volume, written in a minute,  not very clear hand.   It contains copies of
various muniments, deeds and rent-rolls of the Gurney family,  some of a very
early date, and of which the originals were in  possession of the writer; also,
pedigrees, letters, and different memoranda, and withal a great deal of poetry,
written by  Henry  Gurnay on various topics,—one long  poem of 320 stanzas,
called the Anatomy of Popery.  Indeed, his  tendency was to turn all subjects
into verse,—amongst other strange matters even propositions of Euclid, a barren
soil for the muses to cultivate.  As I  have stated, Henry Gurnay was born on
the 21st of January, 1548, (O.S.,) being the son of Francis Gurnay and Hellen
Holditch;  and his  father dying in early life,  he  was heir to his grandfather,
Anthony Gurney, Esq., at whose death, in 1556,  he was about seven years old.
He inherited a considerable estate, which had been in possession of his ancestors
for many centuries.   He  married, at  Barsham, in Suffolk,  (near Beccles,)  on
Trinity Sunday, 10th June, 1571, Helen, daughter of John Blennerhasset, Esq.,
who had been member ofParliament for the city of  Norwich, and was of an ancient
family, originally from Cumberland.  At the time of his marriage, Henry Gurnay
was twenty-two years old and his wife twenty-three.     They had thirteen child-
ren, seven sons and six daughters.— (Register, fol. 22.)    Of these, two daughters
and one son died young.   Henry Gurnay did not reside at Ellingham immedi-
ately after his marriage, but  “came to dwell in that town in 1574.”   His eldest
son having been born at Barsham, by Beccles, the residence of his father-in-law,
John Blennerhasset, Esq., he probably lived  with  him at  first.   He appears to
have been a man of strong and decided religious tendency, with a most bitter

## p. 876 ##

feeling against the Roman Catholics.  He was also disinclined to the Puritans,
as he left a legacy to  his younger sons on condition    so that none hould any
fantasticall or erronious opinions, so adjudged by  our bishop or civil  lawes.”
He states that he took to versifying to amuse his solitary estate, which seems to
have been such from  deafness and perhaps ill health, and his wife and those
about him not  having been congenial to him.   They  do  not  appear to  have
much esteemed his poetry, if the following verses are meant to apply to himself,
of which there can be no doubt :—
AN UNHAPPY DISCRETE.
Unhappiness to him we may ascribe
Whom nerest firends do causeles oft deride.
To whom his falts through nature’s mere defect
before his wife, child, servant, foe or firend,
his nerest frends do usually object,
what can it cause however they Intende,
that even they should hould him in contempt
from whom thereof he ought to be exempt.
his eche devise in husbandry or buildinge
or otherwise, freind, servant or his wife
do cross & thwart but never shew their yeilding,                      }
yea furthermore to cause his irksome life,
ech ill success to him they will impute
but good, or wife, or servant, wholy bruit. (sic.)
Such poemes as to please them he did write,
& straungers do even very much applaude,
they rather will not once admitt to sight,
then take thereby occasion to Collaude,
Who  doth refuse an other's woorke to reade,
Envye or skorne their  judgements overspreade.

## p. 877 ##

Yet if they could by anchor rule or reason
avouch & prove how his conceyts do fayle
for matter forme conditio. cense or  season
in stead of griefe they should him much avayle
who  soner  would to  servants will dissend,
then thwarte the truth against his nerest frende.
of nature’s gifts who meanly is provided
and doth the same most readily aguize*
deserveth more & not to be derided
especially of kin or next alies
who rather should their little sparke of praise
for comforting to flame of fyre raise.
From these lines, and others of his poems,  it  appears  that his  wife  was  not
the most exemplary in temper and respectful conduct towards himself, and that
they were not a very harmonious couple.   The following verses illustrate this.—
By all his life wch. fifty yearest have lasted,
in vice or dice apparell or retinue,
no man can vouch and prove that he hath wasted
so much as tenth but of a yeare’s revenue;
whi should his wife in teeth him daily cast
with vague expence & of exceeding wast.
And though they have full twenty yeares & eightt
together lived while she even all did take,
& he to hir a Reconing ever straight
did yeld of all expence that he did make,
Although she can no wast in him detect
Yet still much wast to him she doth object.
* Aguise,—to adorn, toput  on.
t The writer being  fitty    years old, and    married twenty-eight  years,    proves  that this poem of Henry
Gurnay’s refers to hima   , a8 he was twenty-two when he married,      P

## p. 878 ##

When as he doth for any money aske
for needful use as either debts to pay
or subsedy, town charges, or the taske
wch. may not bide deniall or delaye,
.  Although she doth not flatlye them denye
Yet payes them not without some cross reply.
though chast she be & huswife greate withall
& doth suffice for favour witt & birth
Yet otherwise like wormewood or an awle
do make the rest to be of little worth,
for greater griefe the world cannot afford
then wives contempt in gesture deede & woorde;
most rare it is for man a wife to finde
that every wey shall well co’tent his minde.
And again,
28   Noveb.
by all his life of 60 yeares & more
who twenty markes was never known to wast
on cards or dice or doggs or hawkes or whore
or any wey w** modest measure past,
yet by his wife in teeth is dayly cast
with wastfulness although to prove the same,
she never could (nor any) reason frame
by all his life not twenty pounds he spent
whereof he did or iustly might repent.
Thus it would seem that according to  the  modern term Henry Gurnay was
regularly hen-pecked;  that his wife kept the money bags and never allowed him
to spend anything without  a  cross word;  that nevertheless he was prudent and
careful.   It is to be lamented that instead of writing verses of moderate merit, he
did not employ his  time and solitude in a personal  journal, which would have
been of high interest.  The list of his books (fol. 42,) proves him to have been

## p. 879 ##

a man of unusual research and reading for the period in which he lived, greatly
more so  than  country gentlemen then  or now usually are.   Several books he
had  given  to  his  son Edmund, who was afterwards Rector of Harpley and a
theologicalwriter of some consideration; tohimhe also bequeathed hisLatinbooks.
There is much of kindliness and almost singularity as well as learning apparent
in his writings.   He  was clearly erudite, well disposed and perhaps peculiar in
his ways of  thinking and expressing himself,  quaint and origina;l  of this his
proverbs, something in  the style of Epictetus, are a proof.   He was methodical
and regular in the management of his estates;  this is certain from the number of
deeds and documents relating to his property copied by him, and he often states
such muniments are to be found in boxes numbered. Many of these were of value
golely from their antiquity, and amongst other things he had relish enough for
them to prove a certain degree of antiquarian taste.   His strong religious feelings
and deep interest in his children are exhibited in the following passage respecting
such of them as were then born :—
‘  All the wch. beinge to. the  number  of  thre  sonnes & fowr  daughters were
this xiiii" of  October A° 1579 by gods mercifull  sufferans alive and in  good
helthe, whom also I most humblye beseeche to bless them with his holy spirite
that they may growe upp true & lively membres of his churche and that throughe
the whole course of  this life  they may with their godly conversation and con-
stant  christian profession so glorifye  thy holy name in this worlde that thou
vouchsafe for  the mercifull promise in  Jesus Christ  to  glorifye them in the
worlde of thy kingdom wch. never shall have end.   Amen.”
Although possessed of a considerable estate, Henry Gurnay found in difficult
to struggle on with his very large family, which he describes in one of his short
poems :—
By wanting welth sufficiently
for children to provide,
& hearing matters in my powr
well to adjudge & guide,
My God of grace & world of right
I  hope will me excuse,
though almoise or yet equitie
but slenderly I use.

## p. 880 ##

to both of which O Lord thou knowst
my hart is most inclined,
if thou therto hadst given the means,
as thou hast given the minde.
thy wisdom thus preventing will
to do that good it would,
thy grace will aid me as I  trust
to shunn all ill I  should.
I gather from this that his deafness prevented his administering “ equitie ” by
acting as a justice of the peace or in other public vocations.
He sold some of his property ;   for instance, the manor of Irstead and its ap-
purtenances on 24th  September, 1584,  to  Thomas  Gleane, alderman of Nor-
wich;  also the manor of  Hingham Gurney’s, one of  the most ancient of his
possessions ; but, on the other hand, he purchased the manor and estate of Harp-
ham,  also the advowson of West Barsham.   The former  of  these, however, he
re-sold  to  Richard  Hovel,  Esq.,  in  1610.    The list of his debts in 1580  and
1581 proves that  his  prudence prevented his contracting  any  engagements of
serious amount.  It is as follows :—
Debts owinge fro. me Hen. Gurnay the xij of August 1580.

The following account is for sums of money paid under the will of his grand-
father Anthony  Gurney,  (see page 420)  to his mother, for the education of
her children, six in number.  The  sum allowed appears small for nine years.
There is no proof that Henry Gurney was sent to either of the universities, al-
though it is probable that  he was, from  his  acquirements. His  antipathy  to
Roman Catholicism may have arisen in part from his mother’s second marriage
with one of the Jernegans, or Jerninghams, who, as is well known, did not favour
the Reformation.

## p. 882 ##

He left a legacy worthy of being imitated, viz., £10 to remain in the hands of
the churchwardens of Great Ellingham,  to be lent to necessitous persons in the
parish,  as  a  stock  for   ever, no  one  person  to  have  a  loan  of  more  than
twenty shillings, and interest to be  paid.    He  probably kept  up  the  honours
of a good old English gentleman in his mode of life.   Thus he mentions in his
will his  ¢    bason and ewer of silver”  and his  “chaynn of gould”  which he be-
queaths to his eldest son Thomas;  and in the list of  his debts is a sum due for
liveries.  Many guests appear to have frequented his house, but this was done
in a very primitive way.   In the account of repairs at Great Ellingham mention
is made of the “little house on the other side of  the moate  then used for the
cheefest geast chamber wherein Sir  Christ] Heydon & his ladie did often lie.”
This building still exists, but  the  part  of  the moat near it has been filled up.
He lived wholly at Great Ellingham, and does not appear to  have had a house
at  Norwich,—his grandfather, Anthony Gurney, having sold the old  family
mansion there in St. Julian’s parish called Gurney’s place.  Nevertheless, his
eldest daughter, Elizabeth,  was born  at  the parish  of  St. Martin at Palace, in
Norwich, in 1573;  and  in  1583  his  daughter  Margaret  died at Great Elling-
ham, “ we lying then at Norwich.”  He let West Barsham to his brother-in-law,
Thomas Goldinge, who had married his sister Elizabeth;  and afterwards to her
second husband, Leonard Dodington.  He  was left a widower, but it does not
transpire in what year;  but in the  testamentary paper  given at page 456,  and
dated 1614, he directs to be buried in the parish church of Great Ellingham, in
the north aisle, next his wife.   There are many receipts for different complaints
in the MS. ;  several for deafness, with  which  he  states himself  to  have  been
afflicted,—thus :—
“ Sent by Hir Ma®™  to y* L.. North who  was taken stone deaf so as he could
not heare by no meanes any speech—given me  by  Mr. Arkenstall of Hadman
in y* Ile of Ely  gentn.  where to y® late  lord  North & now  attendant of my L.
Chamberleyn—6 Sep. 1601.
»  Afterwards Lord Chief Justice Coke,

## p. 883 ##

‘“ Bake a little loafe of  Beane flowr and being  whot  rive it into halves & to
ech half pour in 3 or 4 sponefulls of bitter  almonds—then clapp both y* halves
to both y' eares at going to bed, kepe them close & kepe y* head warme.
“ this medicine he saith healed y°* L. North his m".
‘   A medicine for hearing tryed by                   y® miller of Gr. Eling.  (Great
 Ellingham) 
after  50  yeares  of  his  age  who  had  bene  deafe  the  space of  a
quarter of a year through an noyse in his head w® letted him to heare his mill
clapper being in the mill.
“In the month of May  before sonn rising he  tooke y°® dewe of y* grass by
skyming it with his hand &  pouring it  into his eare &  working it in with his
little finger w* he used by the space of a month & then w* a sodeyne Rush or
Flashe the wind brast out  of his head & he was cured, as himself tould me.
A° 1601.   Myself never tried y* because my deafnes is not  by a winde.”
Henry Gurnay, died 23rd February, 1615, (O.S.) aged 67.   I  have stated
erroneously  at  p.   455,  that  he  lived  until  he  was seventy-five  years  old.
I  was led  into  this mistake from supposing the year of his death had been
that in which administration was granted to Anthony Gurney, his fifth son, on a
testamentary paper, viz., in  1623, (see page 456,) nearly eight  years after his
death;  this paper is not signed nor any executors named.   I  cannot account for
the delay in its  being proved.  He wrote his own epitaph, but whether it was
ever piaced on his tomb is doubtfu;l  it  was as follows :—
So here doth rest y° corps of clay,
Of Henry Gurnay squyr,
Untill at Joyfull Judgement day,
to Soule yt shall retyre;
& then through God’s fre specyall grace,
by Christ shall both assend,
unto the most delightsome place,
and Joyes y* never end.
By Blenerhayset sole his wife,
he thirtene children gatt,
Seven sonns & daughters five in life,
at once about him satt.

With his usual tendency to versification, Henry Gurnay put into rhyme the
account of his children :—
The state & petigre of Gurnay & his wife
their childrens ages names & sex & w* remayne in life.
Henry Gurnay of Elingham the great
in Norffk. both Inhabita’t & lord,
w® Blen’hayset who did hould the seate
of Barsham nere to Beckles, did accord
to take to wife first daughter of his mate
child &  coheyr to Itchinghams estate.
on whom seve. sonnes & Girles one more than fyve
he gate, wherof saue daughters only twayne
in anno ninety six were all aliue,
& yongest did hir age of nine attayne,
the eldest at his age of twenty thre
with Lewknor matcht of woorshipfull degree.
Thomas, Henry, Edm®. & Bassingborne
Anthony ffraunces & Lennard for name
have those said sonnes &  were in  order borne,
as her before you see I  sett the same
lyke order now for daughter do I  frame,
Bess, Maude, Anne, Nan,  Meg &  Abigaile
Maude at seuenteene Meg at yeares age did quayle.
Thomas Gurney, the eldest son of Henry,  continued the line of his family at
Great Ellingham and West Barsham.  I  have stated, at page 462, that he died
in the life time of his father, but erroneously, as he survived him about a year,

## p. 889 ##

dying 25th February, 1616.  He married Martha, daughter ofEdward Lewknor,
Esq., of Denham, in Suffolk, and I  find the following poem, by Henry Gurnay,
on their marriage :—
Of T. G. & M. Lewk.
Then daughter of a godly man & wise
Of auncient howse good woorshipp rich and kind
with portion meete may meaner man devise
a better match or ever hope to finde,
wher parentes all & parties pleased be
their mannors faith love age & shapes agree,
Such chiefe respectes might bridegrome well allure
to see & sue to win hir love by love,
& will no less ech faithfull frend p’cure
to further or the Issue to approve,
when all assent for reasons most & best
how can yt be but God that match hath blest.
Henry Gurnay  directs that  he should  be  buried in the  north aisle of Great
Ellingham church, next his wife.   No monument of any of  the Gurneys exists
there at present (1856),  or inscription respecting them, unless covered over by
modern seats and pews.   There are, however, slabs to the memory of the family
of Potts, baronets, who  eventually inherited the Great Ellingham estate from
them.
Henry Gurnay gives a list  of  the members of  his family buried at  Great
Ellingham :—
Burialls within the church &  chappells of Great Elingham  senc the year of
our lord  1554 recorded to  the end no grave be made in those places till con-
venyent tyme.
Anthony Gurnaye esquyre lord of the towne buried in the north chappell ther
& in the middest therof overthwart y* dore entering into y° church tow. y° south.
Ja. 1555. about his age of 63.
ffrances Gurnaye his  sonn & heyre was buriedin  the south chappell about
the midest therof.

Ele Seyve wyfe of Christoph. Seyve gent. & daughter to y* said Antho. was
buryed in the south alley there whose feet lye within a yarde of  the chappell
A° 1589, at hir age 73.
Tho. Gurnaye son of ffrances &  brother to Hen.  Gurnaye lorde of the sayd
towne was buryed in the said south  alley whose feet are within  half a yard  of
the head of  the corse next aforesayd;  his coffyn was of very good & seasoned
oake of inch thick Aug. 1593. at his age 41, he left a sone named Hen. of y* age
of iij yeares &  a  daughter of ij  yeres, he died in good mynde p'fect memory
sence & sight till his last breath;  his heels lye within a yard of the head of Ele
Seyve.
Margett Gurnaye on of the daughters of Hen.  Gumaye lord of y* towne be-
inge a child of a yeare & half ould was buryed in the north alley,whose feet are
within ii yardes of the chappell or pue.

## p. 891 ##

The church is a handsome building, probably erected by the Lords Mortimer.
Tt is of the style  of  pointed architecture called perpendicular.  The arms and
most of the monuments existing in the time of Blomefield are gone.

I  was at Ellingham in 1856,  and  visited the hall,  which is surrounded by a
moat, which includes an acre and a quarter of land.
* Kellhouse,—kiln-house or furnace-house.
+  The Stallonde,—part of Ellingham  estate,  still so called. Hemplond  and  Haulland, other fields, or
parts probably.   Queere whether not stone land, hemp land, and haul or hazel lund.
The present proprietor, Mr. Barnard, recollects the entrance-hall, with a huge
fire-place and grate.   It  was not above nine  feet high, with cross beams, which
remain, and in it was the original oak stair-case, still existing.
A back passage led to the parlour, which was a large lofty room, now half pulled
down.  It had a woodenpillar in the middle of it.    There are many dark rooms
in the attic story, now blocked up.
Outside the moat, the building called the guest chamber is very apparent,
now a stable.   The barn and  mill house exist as mentioned in the manuscrip;t
the barn all of fine oak timber, thatched, in perfect preservation, with clay walls.

In the house is still a portrait of Sir Roger Potts, Bart., who was a descendant
of the Gurneys, and knight of the shire for  Norfolk.   He died in 1711.—(See
page 488).

## p. 896 ##

Mr. Barnard informed me that he has heard from his father that Sir Roger
lived here in great state,driving his coach and set of horses. The outbuildings were
occupied by his servants. Near the hall is a large stew pond.
Henry Gurney brought up his third son, Edmund, to the church.  Bassing-
borne and Francis as merchants. The professions of the others do not transpire.

A library of nearly a hundred volumes, in the possession of a country gentle-
man at the close of the sixteenth century,  when two or three books laid in the
hall window usually satisfied the family predilection for literature, must have
been  very uncommon.    This  collection was indeed what  we should now   call a
very small one, but it was  made  with  judgment  and  taste, and was doubtless
very useful at the  time it  was formed.   There  were four  editions of the Bible,
two in English and two in Latin ;  in English  there  was the  “ Great Bible”  of
1562, then recently printed in folio, and another probably of a smaller size ; and
there was what he calls the “Bible  great in  la.  of  hent. edition;  for  hent.
we should read Vent., meaning Venetize;  editions of  the Vulgate bible  were
printed at  Venice in  1481, 1484 and 1487 severally. The * Vatab. editio.”
1  Saravia was a native of Artois, but resided chiefly in England. He displayed great learning in defence
of    epi                                                            against Beza, and was employed by James L  in  the new translation of the Bible.
» Prodigiorut ac Ostentorum chronicon a Con. Lycosthene.—Basil. 1557. fol.

## p. 902 ##

is clearly the Biblia sacra Vatabli, printed by Robert Stephens at Paris, in 1545,
and a second edition the following year.   Vatablus was an eminent Hebrew and
Greek scholar of the period, born in Picardy.  There were also five editions of the
New Testament, two in English,  two in Latin,  and one in French.   One of his
English Testaments was that in quarto, with Beza’s notes;  the rest we cannot
determine from the list.                              |
Controversial divinity and law  were the  favourite  studies of our ancestors at
the period when Henry ‘Gurnay formed his  collection of books, and there was
accordingly a great preponderance of  works on these subjects in the library at
Great Ellingham.   In the former  were works of some of the puritan divines, as
well as of the defenders of  episcopacy, and in the latter were abridgements of
the statutes, decretals, and works of Linwood, Lyttelton, and others.
There were also works of  the fathers, chronicles, voyages, biography, and
various writings against popery; but  very little  poetry, which,  considering
the  predilection of  Henry   Gurnay for  versification, is  rather remarkable.
He had, however, Barclay’s Stultifera Navis and Sydney’s Book  of English
Poetry.
Some of his theological  works were given by  Henry Gurnay to his son Ed-
mund (“give Ed.” “to Ed.”)  as he was brought up to the church; others are
mentioned as having been lent  to  various friends,  and  others as being at West
Barsham.
There are many documents in Henry  Gurnay’s Register, which, being less
personal to himself, I  print in the following  note, No. 150;   also,  some of his
poems and proverbs;  but the following poem I insert here, as it explains how
he first began versifying.
The woorks w* I  at forty yeares admired
now that myself the lyke can undertake
I  thinke them not soe worth to be desired
we meanest witts through Industry may make
a proof whereof in me is playne decerned,
of weake conceit & very meanly lerned.

## p. 903 ##

In latyne verse I  have no skill   at all
nor any Rules in English had I  redd
when first I  did to versifying fall
nor nature had a promptness in me bredd
no helpes had I  save carefull observation
& Industry & carefull deservation.
for leysure & my  solitary state
w* som defects did iustly me p’cure
such tediousness the better to abate
this exercise to use dyd me allure
so p’tly first I  fell into this vayne
not dotingly nor yet for any gayne.
a distique w* I  stumble on by chaunce
well lyked of a very frend of myne
to further proof my mynd did first advaunce
& proofe therto my mind did more Inclyne
my leysure w® my solitary state
dyd very much me also anymate.
And thus you see a bundle now is framed
of sondry kynds of meeters & conceits,
all w* for art although they may be blamed
yet prysing them w* usual co’'men weight
my hope is, that as none they can offend
so som my payns & learning will co’'mend.
In the Patent Roll  14 Elizabeth (1571)  part  10,  membrane 43, is a grant
to  Henry  Gurney, under age at the time, and  therefore of an earlier date, of  an

## p. 004 ##

annuity to him through the Court of  Wards, and the right of  free marriage.
He is there stated to be son of Francis Gurney, son and heir of Anthony Gurney.
De wardo maritagio pro Henrico Gurney de seipso.
Regina omnibus  ad  quos &c. salutem.—Sciatis quod  nos  de  gracia  nostra
speciali ac ex certa   sciencia at  mero motu nostris dedimus et concessimus ac
per presentes damus et  concedimus dilecto  nobis  Henrico  Gurney generoso.
quandam annuitatem sive annualem redditum viginti  sex solidorum quatuor
denariorum assignandum per Magistrum et consilium curie nostre wardorum et
liberacionum, de et in manerio de Magna Ellingham alias Ellingham Hall cum
pertinenciis in comitatu nostro Norfolcie.   Necnon de et in omnibus messuagiis
terris  tenementis et  aliis  hereditamentis quibuscumque in  Magna EHingham,
Rokeland, Deepham, Morley,  Parva   Ellingham,  et   Attilboroughe,  in   dicto
comitatu nostro Norfolcie;   que fuerunt Anthonii  Gurney Armigeri defuncti
tempore mortis sue, et  que in  manibus nostris jam existunt aut existere de-
bent racione minoris  etatis Henrici  Gurney  consanguinei et  proximi heredis

Wringhay  per  servicium militare, Et que per, sive post  mortem dicti Antonii
Gurney ac racione minoris etatis predicti Henrici  Gurney ad manus nostras
devenerunt   seu  devenire   debuerunt, ac  in  manibus  nostris  jam   existunt  aut
existere debent, quoque modo  damus eciam et  concedimus prefato Henrico
Gurney custodiam corporis et maritagium sui  ipsius, Habendam gaudendam et
percipiendam dictam annuitatem sive annualem redditum viginti sex solidorum
et  quatuor  denariorum  aceciam custodiam corporis et  maritagium  sui ipsius
absque disparagacione prefato Henrico Gurney executoribus et assignatis suis;
Et hoc absque compoto seu aliquo alio  nobis  heredibus  vel successoribus nos-
tris  proinde reddendo solvendo seu faciendo ;  et ulterius  de  uberi gracia nostra
dedimus et concessimus ac per presentes damus et  concedimus prefato Henrico
Gurney executoribus et  assignatis suis predictam annuitatem sive annualem
redditum  viginti  sex  solidorum  et  quatuor  denariorum  a  tercio  decimo die
Januarii anno domini millesimo quingentesimo quinquagesimo quinto quo die
prefatus Antonius Gurney obiit, hucusque et extunc provenientem sive crescentem
de  redditibus  revercionibus  et  proficiis  maneriorum  terrarum et aliorum here-

In the year 1588, Henry Gurnay sold the manor of Hingham Gurneys, pro-
bably the most ancient possession of his family.    This appears by two deeds of
sale now  amongst  family  papers, at  Keswick, apparently  duplicates;  one  in
Latin, the other in English, the former containing by much the fullest particulars.
The manor was sold to Paul  Gooch, of Harpham (of  whom Henry  Gurney
bought the manor of Harpham in the same year), for £800.    The  following is
an abstract of these deeds of sale :—
This Indenture, made the 10® of Decr. 30® Elizabeth (1588), witnesseth that
Henry Gurney of Great Ellingham, for and in consideration of the sum of £800,
hath sold to Paul Gooche, of Harpham, all the manor of  Gurnay’s in Hingham
with the appurtenances, and the tenement commonly called Crosses with its ap-
purtenances, in Hingham, Great Ellingham and Rising, and all  the messuages,
tenements, cottages, &c.—with all the rents, suits, services,courts, &c., whatsoever
to the said manor and tenements belonging.   And also the said Henry Gurnay
for the consideration aforesaid hath  sold  unto the said Paul Gooche all patents,
charters, court rolls, extents, rentals,  books  of  survey, deeds, evidences, muni-
ments and writings  concerning the said manors  and  tenements,—all which the
saidHenry Gurnay covenants to deliver to the said Paul Gooche undefaced and
uncancelled on this side and before the feast day of the nativity of our Lord next
coming after the date hereof.  In witness whereof either of  the  said parties to
this present Indenture their hands and seals interchangeably have put, the day
and year above written.

## p. 9006 ##

The Latin deed is dated at Hingham, nine  days posterior to  the above, and
gives the abuttals of the several pieces conveyed, containing together by estima-
tion 123 acres.
In the Patent Roll 7 James I. is a licence to Henry Gurney to sell to Richard
Hovell, Junr., Esq"., the manor of Harpham with its appurtenances, &c.—the fol-
lowing is an abstract :—
By letters patent dated at Westminster 1st Decr.  7  James 1. (1610) licence
is granted to Henry Gurney, Esqr., to sell to Richard Hovell, Junr., Esqr., for the
need and use of the  said Richard, his heirs and assigns for ever, the manor of
Harpham with  its  appurtenances and 4 messuages, 4  cottages, 1 wind mill, 8
gardens, 6 orchards,  200  acres  of  arable land,   6   of  meadow,  100 of pasture,
4 of wood, 200 of  furze and  heath, and 50 shillings rental, with their appur-
tenances in Harpbam, Attleborough and Snetterton;  as also liberty of fold with
its appurtenances in Harpham;  and the advowson of  the church in  the same
place ;  all which were held of the King in capite.
See Patent Roll 7 James I;  part 17;  No. 35.
Note  150.—FUuRTHER EXTRACTS FROM  HENRY  GURNAY'S REGISTER.
(page 454.)
The previous note 149 contains extracts  from Henry Gurnay’s Register more
immediately relating to himself;  those I now give are of a less personal charac-
ter, and on various subjects.
Fuller, in his Worthies, states the inhabitants of Norfolk were much given to
litigation.   Henry Gurnay does not seem to have been clear of this :—
Stiles londes.
~                 “Notes out  of a booke wrytten with the hand of ould  James Style about the
yere of our Lord 1550 of such londes as he held free or by copye of the Mannor
of Elingham.
Memorandum that these notes were taken  out only of one  syde of the booke
the rest of the leaves beinge sealed upp by James the son when he delivered the

## p. 907 ##

sayde booke vnto me to  give in evydens against howlett for  the standyng of a
cross at gatisendweye at th’assyses 1578.
Howlett agreed with me & therfor y° suit seased he gave me a marke towardes
my  charges.”
It does not appear from the particulars given of  these lands in what manner
the standing of the cross depended upon them.   Perhaps this was in some way a
religious dispute.
The account of estrays at Ellingham is curious.   It must be remembered that
Norfolk was then an open sheep walk, with only a small proportion of land un-
der cultivation, so that the cattle and stock  straying must have been numerous,
and the possession of the estrays a manorial right of some importance.
Estrays of valuable  animals  belonged to  the king,  as lord  paramount of the
soil, or to the lord of the manor by  special grant  from the crown.    The estrays
must be proclaimed in the church and two market towns next  adjoining where
they are found, and, after proclamation a year and a day past, belong to the king
or his grantee.—See Blackstone, book I., ch. 8,  xv.—Witnesses to all this were
necessary.
“Streyes taken at Elingham magna & Inioyed by the lord as nere as I  can call
to reme’brance at this 8 Deceb. A°. El. 39. none of them or very few as I  think
recorded in the court throughe my follye in not rem’bring them yet most of them
prised by the Inhabitants as in my yerly new books may be found.
A grey ambling nagg  the first  yeare 1 came to  dwell in the towne  vidz.  A°
1574 that was Rich James of lynkwhite nagg till he sould him and after streyed
to the stallond from whence he was brought to the pound & Inioyed as a strey
by  me about a  dozen yeares after.  Huit  &  Jho. Jenken &  Robt. Sharpe
witnesses.
A Black nag or browne brought the ponde about ambling very well shaped &
buttocky but very slowe and dull Inioyed about a dozen yeares taker about A°
Dni. 1580.  Witnesses, Huet, Jno. Jenke. Ed. Gaskeyn, Rich Butler.
A graye or geldyng that kild himself with his shackles striving to leape a dyke
at the lay  close about  A°  1583.   Witnesses as before after Inioying ii or iii
yeares.
An other graye nagg or guildyng which had a kynd of horn  growyng out of
his hoofe behinde Inioyed about ii yeares then stoln from the lay close.
A little Gray nagg brought in by Jno. Jenken of the co'mon w* being       yeares
& more I   sould  to  my  brother  Atkins*  for  40°. w* was stolne fro.  him  at
Artham.
A dark grey brought in by Rich. Butler  about A° 1593 & w® I now Inioye
& is Imployed chiefely at Hargha. called the child horse.
A sow w™ certeyne piggs of on  supposed to be  of  Attleborough & ran away
about A° 1587.
Shottest vi brought in about hallowtyd fro. the stollen by Phill  Turnor A°
1594 the same yeare the great snow fell  on  the first  daye of  the springe w* I
inioyed.
Sheepe haf a dozen at one tyme of wethers suspectet to be stolne.
Divers others in other yeares by on or two w* I  rem" not, but p’haps are noted
in my yeare bookes vntill as yet I  do Inioy all strayes that happen without gain-
sayng of Tenants or cleyme by any other.”
The following passages from the court rolls of Great Ellingham shew, in the
first place, that the tenants were fineable for  non-performance of work done for
the lord, a certain number of days’ work being due to him ;  and,  in the second
place, that poaching existed in the reign of Edward III.   Here  we have John
Cosyn catching a hare,  and  Agnes Harvey  and  Matilda de Carbonel,  accom-
panied amongst others by  John Path, Rector of All  Saints, catching rabbits
where they had no right.   The fine or punishment is not recorded.

Robert Lovell, referred to  in  the following passage. Sir  John Mordaunt was
ancestor of the Mordaunts, Earls of Peterborough, now extinct.

.pcipall  was made &  was Indorsed with  the hand of the said Anth. by thes
wordes vidz. Sr. Jho. Mordt. his bonde.”
I have described (page 418) Gurney’s place in Conisford-street, in St. Julian's
parish, at Norwich.   How long this old mansion had been in the family  does
not appear, but  probably for some generations.  It  seems likely Edmund Gur-
nay, who was standing council to the city  of Norwich, temp. Ed. III.,  lived in it,
but Thomas Gurney II.  had his  Norwich house in  St. Gregory's parish, and
William Gurney IV. in Pockthorpe. Mr. Norris states Gurney’s place was sold
to the Gawdys after the death of Anthony Gurney, but it appears he sold it him-
self in the year 1548, and with it his lease of  a meadow belonging to the Cathe-
dral, for £140.   This shows the value of a large town residence at that time.

Harlso.t gen. & Isabell y*® wife of y® sayd Tho. Gawdy of Shotsha. on y* other

Henry Gurnay  let  West  Barsham to  his  brother-in-law,  Thomas Golding,
probably with the domain lands.  This  accounts for his  sending him the book
of particulars of the estate.
“Notes of West barsha.— (folio 5.)
I  sent a fild booke conteining all my landes in West barsha. northe barsha. &
houghto.* in p’ticular with the buttalling & allso all re’ts both fre & bonden for
eyther of the sayd manors to my brother golding to forneha. by Jhon Smith his
man the xxvj of march 1579 the boke co’teyneth 13 leaves & was made A°1553.
Also sent vnto him by the saide Jhon & on the sade day one re’tall & firmall
of all my sayd landes written with my grandfather holdich his owne hande dated
the second & third of Phill. & Mary conteyning v leaves written.
Also a rentall vnder myne owne hande taken by the accompt of Nich. fien
bayliffe there the vij® of March 1577.

to have bene written in y* regne of H. 4.
All these bookes I  receyved again of them att fforneha. the xxij of Decebr. 1579.”
At page 312 I  give a copy of a fine respecting half a knight's fee at Harpley,
dated 30th Henry II. (1184), on Wednesday before the feast of St. Luke (18th
October), between Matthew de Gurnaye and Rose his wife and Philip de Burn-
ham.  Henry Gurnaye gives a full copy of this fine, and adds :—
“this fine is extant  in  parchment  as yt was taken  out  of the court in these
dayes & lyeth w™  the deeds copied in the page insuinge.                box 18.”
This shows how carefully he preserved the original deeds he possessed, as do
also his copies of early charters.

Here we havea parish rate, for several purposes. Marshalsea money was the
county rate, so called,  it is supposed, as  being  partly  applied for the  relief of
prisoners in the Marshalsea or King's  Bench  and  other  prisons.—See Forby's
Vocabulary of East Anglia.

## p. 914 ##

¢““   COPIES OF LETTERS.
The Duke of Yorke.
Right trusty & welbeloved we greete you hertely well.  And of y* good mais-
tershipp & frendshipp w* ye have shewed heretofore at the  reverens of vs vnto
all our tenantes in Nozff. & namely vnto our tenantes of Gaysthwayte & yet doe
fro. daye to daye in all  such thinges as they have adoe in that countreye.  We
thanke you with  all  our  harte as we have notable cause, prayinge & desiringe
you of your  good  p’seueranc for y° w* you may trust  verraylie to finde us in-
clyned to shewe you our good lordshipp in  any thinge  reasonable that we may
doe unto your desyre in tyme for to come. Right trusty & welbeloved our lorde
have you in his keepinge.—Geven und® our  signet at our castell of fodringheye
the xviii® daye of Januarye.
No subscriptio. but as above.
the seale to  this  letter is  a manuell seale of  y° armes of inglande & is set
uppo. the outsyd of the letter.
the sup’scriptio. thus
To our right trusty & welbeloved
Tho. Gurnaye squyre.”
Although there is no date of the year in this letter, it seems written by Richard
Duke of York, father of Edward IV., and son of Richard Earl  of Cambridge by
Anne Mortimer, daughter of  Roger Earl of March, who, as the descendant of
Lionel Duke of Clarence, third son of Edward III.,  had a superior claim to the
crown to that of the house of Lancaster.   Fotheringay Castle was given by Ed-
ward III. to his fifth son, Edmund of Langley Duke of York, whose descendants
possessed it for  some generations. Richard Duke  of  York  was killed at the
battle of Wakefield,  in  1460,  and buried  at  Fotheringay.    There is no parish
named Gaysthwayte in Norfolk,  and I  do not discover what lands the Duke of
York held in our county.  The letter must have been written after the year 1420,
when he was restored in blood,  and probably to Thomas Gourney I., who was
clearly a Yorkist.   The house of  York had many partizans in  Norfolk.   Sir
Henry Heydon, of Baconsthorpe, was steward of the house to Cecilia Duchess
of York, widow of this Richard Duke of York,  and daughter of Ralph Neville
Earl of Westmoreland. In the next generation, Anthony Widville, second Earl

## p. 915 ##

Rivers, brother to Edward IV.’s  Queen Elizabeth Widville, married Elizabeth,
daughter and heir of Thomas Lord Scales, of Middleton,  in Norfolk;  and Ed-
ward IV. himself, during his adverse circumstances in 1470, took shipping for
Holland at Lynn,—it may be supposed from the interest he had in the neigh-
bouring district through the family of Scales.
“Right intyre beloved Cosynne. Ico’'mend me vnto you & for the great kind-
nes that I  have founde in you  &  specially in my  necessytye I in  most harty
wise thank you  prayinge  you  of  p’seuerance & require  you to take it to none
vnkindenes nor displeasure that your daye is not kept w* is right greatly against
my will but the defaut therof is for the non payme’t of such money as the plea-
sure of y® kinges highnes were not, I  longe or this should have had in this coun-
trye by reason of his assigneme’t. 'Wherfor I  desire & pray you of paciens for a
season & it shall not be longe to but I  trust to please you as more at lenthe my
right trusty councellor & servant Tho. Jennye can shewe unto  you to whom it
woll lyke you to give credeit in this behalf & God p’serve you.   Written in the
castell of Shireshotten y° xxvi day of Septbr.
Your loving Cosyn
Tho. Surrey.
the sup’scriptio. of y* letter
next above is thus
To our right hartely beloved Cosynne
Will. Gurnaye squyre.”
Thomas Earl of Surrey, the writer of this letter, was son of   John Howard,
first Duke  of Norfolk of the   Howard family, slain at Bosworth field in 1485.
Thomas Earl  of  Surrey was so created in the life time of  his father in 1483.
He  was at the battle  of  Bosworth,  and  attainted by Henry VII. in 1485,  and
imprisoned in the tower of London; but afterwards received into favour by that
king, restored to the earldom of Surrey in 1489, and createdDuke of Norfolk by
Henry VIII.in 1514,0b. 1524,and wasburiedat Thetford,in thepriory churchthere.
Shireshotten Castle I  think a miswriting for Sherry or Sherriff  Hutton or Hoton
Castle, in  Yorkshire.    This  letter  was  written  to William  Gurney IV.,  who
married Ann  Calthorpe, and was of  council  to  the  Duke  of  Norfolk,  and

## p. 016 ##

escheator for Norfolk.   The Earl of Surrey owed him money, as appears by the
letter, which he was unable to pay when due.   Whether the term  cousin used
in the letter implied a relationship, or was only  used as a matter of courtesy, I
do not discover.  Very possibly some relationship existed between the Earl and
William Gurney.   The Gurneys were certainly amongst the dependants of the
house of Howard.
“Y* Erle of Oxinford.
Right welbeloved I  comende me unto you.  ffor as much as it is shewedunto
me in y® behalfe of your Brother Jho. Gurnaye that your fader and his graunted
unto him in his lyfe by  his deed an annuitye of certeine rent yerlye w* as he
saith you keepe fro. him vnkindlye.  I  therfore doe desyre you  that if y* so be
y* ye will be to him good & loving brother  suffring him to have that to him be-
longeth of right by y® graunt & will of your sayd father y* rather at this my de-
syre & almighty God  keepe you.    Written  at  my castle of  Hedingha.  y* xvj
daye of Dece’ber.
Oxynford.
To my right welbeloved
Will. Gurnay esquyre.
All thes former letters lye in y° botto. of y* xi box.
This W. Gurnay lyved about y* begininge of H. 7.”
John de Vere, thirteenth Earl of Oxford of that ancient family, was attainted
in 1475, but restored to his honours by Henry VII.  in 1485, obtained a confir-
mation of the office of  Great Chamberlain in 1509, Lord  High  Admiral and
K.G., died 1514.  This nobleman was a strong partizan of the house of Lancas-
ter, and gave a sumptuous entertainment to  Henry VII.  at his castle of Hed-
ingham, in Essex.  The great family of De Vere gave twenty Earls of Oxford
in succession. It became extinct in 1702.   This Earl John writing in  a  way
so authoritative to William  Gurnay (IV.)  may have arisen from his having
custody during his  minority  of  the lands and person of  William  Viscount
Beaumont, under whom the Gourneys held fiefs, Viscount Beaumont being
descended from the Bardolfs and the elder line of the Gourneys. It appears,

## p. 017 ##

also, that William  Gurney  V.  and  Thomas Sefoul had  a  grant from  this
John  Earl  of  Oxford  of   the  custody of  the lands of  Roger  Wode,  of
East Barsham (see page 407), so that some connexion existed between them.
In  the  will  of  Thomas Gournay II.   father of  this William, (page 396),
to  whom  the letter is  addressed this  annuity  to  John  Gurnay,  also to
Edmund Gurnay, his  son, is  mentioned as having been given by the testator by
deed.   In the same will, half the  furniture and jewels are left to William Gur-
nay at the death of  his  mother, if  he conducts himself dutifully to her and
maintains the grant made to his brother;  so that Thomas Gurnay seems to have
anticipated that his son William would endeavour to avoid paying the annuity,
as it appears he did.
“ Right worshippfull I hertely comend me on to you advertisinge you that my
brethre. with mature  deliberatio. porposinge to doe your  mastershipp pleasure
be co’tent that y* have two  felles of  y® carr in Netisherde within  your lease of
plyeres in stead of to hundred woode that they had graunted to you yerlye for
the said yeres for y° w™  yeres they graunt you also the advocatio. of y° vicarege
of Netesyarde as oft as it shall be vacant after these p’sentatio. lately promised
to one Sr. Hamonde  Chaunte to whome if promise were not made both I  and
theye would gladly have done you pleasure at this tyme in that behalf as know-
eth god whoe ev" p’serve your good mastershipp at  St. Benetes the xxiiij daye
of October.
Yo* Beadma. W. Repps
abbott of St. Bennetes.
the sup’scriptio. thus
To the right worshippfull
Mr. Antho. Gurnaye esquyre
be dd.
In thes former letters may be noted a great differens betwene their age & ours
not only for the playn & simple ma’ner of inditinge but also for their familier &
affable manner of writinge whoe beinge great noble men are more gratefull in
courteouse & famillier speche to  them  that are many  degres their inferiors for

## p. 018 ##

small pleasures then meane men will in our dayes be for great pleasures to them
that be but in degre next them selves.”
William Repps, alias Rugge, the writer of  this letter, was son of  William
Rugge, ofNorth Repps, Norfolk ; lie was fellow of Gonville Hall, Cambridge, and
appointed abbot of St. Bennet of Hulme in 1530.   Six years after he was pro-
moted to the see of Norwich, and exchanged the temporalities of the bishopric
with Henry VIII. for those of the abbey, or rather, as it appears, for part only of
the abbey lands, retaining, however, the palace at Norwich.   He and his monks,
twenty-one in number, subscribed to the king's supremacy in 1534.   William
Repps was either deprived of his bishopric or resigned it before his death, which
occurred in 1550.  He was buried in Norwich cathedral.
« A Copye of a popish pardo. wherein maye be noted as well his rudenes in
writinge as error and presumptio. in graunting.  A° 1300.
Universis sancte matris  ecclesie filiis  ad  quos presentes littere  pervenerint.
Nos miseratione divina Adenulphus Consanus Archiepiscopus, Manfridus Sancti
Marci, Nicholaus Tortibuleij,  Barnardus Paceij,  Jacobus Calcedonius episcopi
salutem et sempiternam in  domino charitatem.—Quia pium est pro vivis et de-
functis exorare ut  a peccatorum suorum nexibus liberentur et celestis regni
dulcedinem perfectam mereantur, Gratum igitur  obsequium et  deo devotum
totiens impendere opinamur, quotiens mentes  fidelium ad pietatis et charitatis
opera excitamus ut exinde divine gracie reddantur aptiores.  Qua propter nos de
omnipotentis dei miseracordia, dulcisque matris sue marie clementia, necnon et
beatorum petri  et  pauli  appostolorum ejus, atque omnium  sanctorum meritis
precibus et auctoritati confidentes, omnibus vere penitentibus et confessis qui
pro salute salubrique statu  domini  Johannis de Gurnay et Matildis  sororis sue
Norwicensis diocesis dum vixerunt, et animabus eorum post obitum, et pro ani-
mabus domini Johannis de Gurnaye, margerie uxoris sue, domini Willelmi de
Gurnaye et Katerine sororis (qu. uxoris) sue, quorum corpora in  diversis locis
predicte diocesis requiescunt humata, et animabus omnium fidelium defunctorum,
orationem dominicam cum salutatione angelica quotiescunque quandocunque

## p. 919 ##

vel ubicunque pia mente dixerint,  Quillibet nostrum singillatim quadraginta
dies de injunctis sibi penitentiis (diumodo loci diocesanus hanc nostram indul-
gentiam ratam habuerit) miserecorditer in  domino relaxamus;  in  cujus rei
otestimonium presens scriptum sigillorum nostrorum munimine duximus robo-
randum.  Datum Lateran.  Anno domini millesimo tricentesimo. Pontificatus
domini Bonifacii P.P. octavi anno sexto.                                         |
The pope trusteth in  the merites of sainctes as though any could merit for
themselves much less for others.
The effecte of this paultrye pardon is that all true penitentes that have co’fessed
them selves & doe saye the pater-noster & ave Mary with a godly minde for the
good estate of certeyne of y° name of gurnaye that  were dead & all other soules
of y* faithfull shall have xl  dayes of p'do. of their inioyned penans fro.  every
one of  y°® above named bishoppes for so often whansoever & whersoever theye
shall say them so that the bishopp shall alowe of this sayd p’don.
This p’don semeth to have bene purchased with great travel & charge for it is
written  with a romish  hande & if p’dons  might be of any  value this is yet but
little worthe, because it is gra'ted with  such hard co’ditio’s namely y*® prayers
must be made by such as were truly penite’t w® they could hardly be that were
not rightly informed of their sinnes & besides the bishops alowa’s must be had
w® might also be uncerteine &  yet if all  were obteined what benifit hath y*
p’uiders hereof for all their charge more then all  y° cristien soules w™have as
much relife herof as they so as it ministreth
either p'dons ar nothing or not iustly  distributed.”
These pardons, as is well known, were very common during the middle ages,
and were one great means of  revenue to the court of Rome.   The pardoners
were the sellers of these indulgences. Chaucer thus describes the pardoner in
the Canterbury Tales :—
«“  He had a cross of Latine full of stones
And in a glass he had pigge’s bones ;
But with these relikes, when that he fond
A poore parsone dwelling in uplond,

## p. 920 ##

Upon a day he gat him more money
Than that parsone gat in months twey,
And thus with fained flattering and  japes,
He made the parsone and people his apes.
But truly to tellen at the last
He was in church a noble ecclesiast.”
I  suspect these lines  of  Chaucer apply to the case before us,  and that this
pardon was obtained by John de Gurnay II., rector and patron of Harpley (see
page 342), who was such in the year 1300, for himself and his sister Matildis,
whilst they lived and after death, “dum  vixerunt (qu.  vixerint) et animabus
eorum post obitum ;”  and for  the  souls of  John de Gournay and Margery his
wife, whom I  take to be John de Gournay I. and his wife, father and mother of
John de Gurnay, rector and patron;  and for the souls of William de Gurnay
and Katherine his  wife (sororis being erroneously written for uzoris), being
William de Gournay II.,  who  married  Katherine, and was grandfather of John
de Gurnay II,  rector and patron of Harpley, who, in short, probably purchased
this pardon for himself and his sister, and for the souls of his father and mother,
and of his grandfather and grandmother.  This great abuse of indulgences and
Martin Luther's resistance to them brought about, as we all remember, the great
Reformation in the 15th and 16th centuries.
Of the prelates at the head of the document, I conjecture “adenulphus consanus
archiepiscopus ” may be archbishop of Cozenza in Apulia;  the others may be
titular bishops in partibus. The last named, “ Jacobus Calcedonius episcopus,”
certainly is.   I do not discover of what places the other prelates were bishops,
whether real or titular.
Boniface VIII., during whose papacy this pardon was granted, was Benedict
Caietan, born at Anagni.   He occupied the papal chair from 1295 to 1303, and
was a man of considerable ability, but during his whole reign in troubled waters.
both at home and abroad.

This Thomas Gurnay, brother of Henry, married a daughter of Reams, by
Aylsham, (page 458,) probably of Overstrand. By this it seems she was a widow,
and Matthew Pratt, her son by her first husband. Thomas Gumnay’s daughter

## p. 924 ##

Elizabeth was born in St. Edmund’s parish,  Norwich,  11th June,  1591  (page
887).   He himself was buried at Ellingham, August, 1593 (page 890).
The family of Reams of Overstrand was  of great antiquity  (see their pedigree
in Norris MSS.),  and, according to  Blomefield in  Tasburgh, were anciently
called De Ramis.   Rainthorpe -or Ramisthorpe is a manor so designated from
them, in Florden and Tasburgh.   This family came to England at the conquest,
and had the honour of the barony of Raines or Reynes, consisting of 10 knight's
fees, in Essex.  They bore sable a chevron between three lions rampant argent.
—Blomefield in Overstrand.
See also Morant’s Essex, vol. II., p. 403, for an account of this family, which
appears to have taken its name from Rayne or  Little Rayne, in Essex.  Other
authorities say from Rennes, in Britanny, but that  seems hardly probable.
Their ancestor, Roger de Ramis, held lands in capite in Essex and Norfolk.
The following verses, exhibitingHenry Gurnay’s strong religious feeling, ought
to have been introduced into Note 149, which contains matter more personal to
himself.   They are, moreover, superior to his compositions in general :—
* O only God who beinge guifst & life
& every meane to soule & Boddies pleasure
whos blessings be most manifest & rife
surmounting far all man’s conceit or measure
Voutsafe me faith and thankfulness O Lord
within my heart them ever to Record.

the only meanes that may my synnes deface

atteine not to but only by thy grace,
such grace voutsafe me, lord, most humble craver
that I may live & dye within thy favor.”
“ O dives dives non omni tempore viues,

Burleigh.   He thus writes his encomium :—

## p. 931 ##

Manners, Earl of  Rutland, and the  three last Barons are not included in the
parliamentary summons, 39th of Elizabeth, 1597.  The Earl  of Rutland was
abroad at the time, and Lord Vaux a minor.    The reason why Lord Wentworth
and I.ord Saye  &  Sele were  not   summoned does  not appear.    It  is to be ob-
served in Henry Gurnay’s analysis of the house of peers, that there is no Duke.
The dukedom of Norfolk was under attainder since 1572, when Thomas Howard,
4th Duke of that name, was executed; and the dukedom of Somerset was in like
manner under attainder since the execution of  the Lord Protector in 1552;
these two were the only ones then existing.   Of these eighty-six peers, twenty-
six, or more than  one quarter, were spiritual peers, and before the Reformation,
when the mitred abbots sat in the house of peers, the proportion of ecclesiastics
was considerably more.   The numerous creation of lay peers since 1596, has in-
deed wholly altered the ancient proportion of  spiritual and temporal members
of the upper house.
‘“ Semper egenus eris si semper plus tibi queris
Cum contentus eris tum dives efficieris.
Rich smidge still    scraping more
is deem’d & so is poor
but well contented mynd
Inough doth ever fynde.”
MORTIMER  AND  FITZRALF.
I  must  refer to  note 143, page  862, where the  memorandum here given  is
mentioned. It shews the line of descent of the Gurneys from the Fitzralfs, and
that according to modern ideas of heraldry, they ought not to have quartered the
arms of Fitzralf or  Mortimer; but  it appears that anciently quartering of arms
was the representation of estates in possession, inherited, whether by heiresses or
not;  and I  doubt  whether younger sons, not  in possession of such estates, had

## p. 932 ##

originally the heraldic right of quartering the arms.  The memorandum itself is
a good specimen of English writing at the end of the fifteenth century.
Mortimers Petigree as by an auncient p’sident in p’chment vnder seale ap-
peareth (written in the same manner of English as hath the p’sident.—)
The descent of Mortimer of Attleborough from Sir William de Mortimer, 1296,
to the children of Sir Thomas Mortimer who died in 14086,is first given ;—these
children were
‘“ Robt. Cecilie & Margerye, w™* Robt. dyed at his age of ix yeares.
“The said Cecilie was maried to Sr. Jho. Harling Knight & he had w* her in
marriage iij° markes & 1" of the said Sr. Robt.” (his wife’s grandfather) “and Tho.
his fader.  And sone after Margerie sister of the said Cicelye was married to Sr.
Tho. flitzrauffe Knight and he had in marriage iij° marces of the said Sr. Robt.
& Tho. his fadir.”
And after the marriages of Dame Cicelie & Dame Margerye Sr. Tho. Morti-
mer fadir of the said Cicelie & Margerie went over sea & there disseased.”
Sir  Robert Mortimer, the  grandfather, gave all his  lands in  trust  to Henry
Pakenham, the elder, parson of Scoulton, and others, as appears by evidences.—
‘  And uppo. that fleo’'ment the said Robt. made his last will as appeareth by
the said will that all  his livelood  should be sould & graunted to  Sr. Tho. Har-
linge &  to Sr. Tho. flitzraufe &  to ther heires to be departed by  twixen them to
egally by the vice of the said ffeofours with this condition that thei should pay
or doe for to be paid to the said ffeofors p’forminge the last will of the said Sr.
Robt.  &  to  the foundatio. of  the College of  Attlborough iche of  them an  m'.
m'’rces—.
Accordingly after the death of Sir Robert Mortimer, Sir   John Herling and Sir
John Fitz-Ralph gave the feoffees security each for 1000 marks, and had the in-
heritance divided between them ;—
‘“ Except the Mannor of Ber’ham Brome the quech the s* Sr. Robt. Mortimer
gaff & graunted  to his sonne Constantin and to his heires male & for defaut of
heires male to terne agen to the said Sr. Robt. Mortimer & his heires as appereth
by a deed Indented.”
“ And  gwann the partitio. was made the said ffeoffoures madin an lawfull
estate to ich of  them of the said lyvelode & uppo. the  same estate a fine Reced
in  the King  Court as appeareth.”
“And of the said Jho. ffitzraufe & dame Magerie his wife of them to came Jhon
flitz Raufe esquier & enerited all the landes of the foresaid Sr. Jho. flitz Raufe &
dame Margery his wyfe. and after hir  dissease the said Jho. flitz Raufe esquire
stoode sole seased in all hir londes &  made an estate to  Rich. Bewchamp Erle
of Warwyke Sr. Jho. Howard Knight & to other as appereth by evidenc.”
‘“ Thann the said flitz Raufe esquyr was maried to the lady Bowsers* daughter
& of them came none Issue & so an none after the said Jho. flitz Raufe esquier
maried Julian Broun  & of them to  came Issue Jho. flitz Raufe esquyr Maute
Johann & Alice and whan he had thes children by ............... his wife his bro-
ther Robt. flitz Raufe noised the said children in the countrey that he should be
his brothers heyre & nought they & then was the said Jho. flitz Raufe esquyr his
brother wroth with him & sent for all his Councell to Skoulton that is for to seye

Raufe esquyer sould for his brothers langwage and yan after yat  time by the

raufe esquyr was maried to  Alis Whalsburgh cosen to my  lady of Suffk. & at
that time all the evidenc of the said Jho. flitz  Rauf esquyer londes & Mortimer
londes by the concell of my  lord of Suffk.  &  the counceyle of the said Jho.

& knowyn verily yat all his londes  stoode in  fee simple  and that knew it Jhon

treuth of the matter playnly.”
‘“ And then myn lord of suff. desired to have all the londes of the said Jhon ffitz

riage hing  allmost to  zeir} or  they acorded, yan at the last they accorded that

it  appereth  be the  taile  &  for  defaut  of  heyres  male to  Remayne to Maut  &

then the sayd councell by their good avyse chergeid the sayd Evydenc in lenten
season.  And at Lammas next  followinge all the  Remnaunte & at ech tyme &
day all the said councell wern ther  togithers thre dayes till that thev had tryed
out all the Evidence & they  foundyn  none  tayle of all the said  lordshipps but
all only of the mannor of fhingfildt the quech was forthwith seald bycause of
his brother Robt.’s langwage.”

The following loose pedigree of  the Norman Gournays seems to have been
taken from Battle Abbey Roll and other sources :—
« A  Copy  of a Petygree.
Hugo de Gorney fillius Hugonis de Gorney dat Ludouico de Gourney.
Hugo  de Gorney  temp. Will. Conqu. com. patit Battell Abby.
Rich. Comit Perch test.
Hugo de Gorey fillius  Hugonis de Gourney confir. le done de Ludovico de
Gorney et Tho. de Gorney fill. eius ad Robt. Swathing ibi Will. de Gorney.
Mathu  de Gorney marryed y° daughter of  Reginald fitzphillip and of (the
gift of) Hamlen Earle of Warren.”

## p. 036 ##

It  seems likely that some of the Gournays remained in Normandy after Philip
Augustus conquered it, and it  was separated from England.   The following
charter of Walter de Gournay, confirming  the grant of Richard,  his  father,  son
of Gwarin (de Gournay) of half the church and tithe of Verneuil, and the pre-
sentation to that of Puisaye, to the abbey of Jumieges, was formerly sent me by
M.  Achille Deville, at  that  time  residing  at  Rouen,  the  historian of Chateau
Gaillard, and author of other standard works.    The date of this charter is 1207.
Verneuil is at no great distance from the ancient fief of the Gournays at Ecouché.
Omnibus ad quos presens scriptum pervenerit Walterus de Gornaio salutem.

Here is the ancient name of Alberarda revived.
Among the preliminary verses addressed ‘  To the Author,” are these: —
¢    Of Hemp and wooll our country weauers make,
Such kind of cloth as keeps us whole and cleane,
This silken weeuer subtler loomes gin take,
And seu’n weeks web hath warpt with finer beam,
His cloth discouereth vice,
Adorning vertues lore,
‘Wherefore of greater price,
then weauers heretofore.
Ed. Gurney. Gent.”
This effusion was from Edmund Gurney, third son of Henry, Fellow of Cor-
pus College, Cambridge, and Rector of Harpley—(see page 463).   I  think it
likely the Weever, against whom it  is written, may have been the author of the
Funeral Monuments.—He was educated  at  Queen's College, Cambridge, where
Edmund Gurney had been originally placed, and some difference may have
arisen between them occasioning these satirical lines.
POEM  ON NORFOLK.
Of english Shires our Norffk. semes to yeeld
most pleasures & co’modityes for gayne
that may be reapt, by water gress or fild
w  lesser toyle through erines* & the playne
ech thing it hath wherwith to cloath & battlet
as wool & hemp, fish, foule, ech corne & cattle.
Not self alone doth Norffk. full suffice
Although it is as populous a shire
As any ells (yet of a smaler sise
then others are as mapps do make appeare)
But also doth of hir abundance spare
to other shires that not so flruitfull are.
for Kates} that serve to furnish out a bourd
excepting Salt w* all but Cheshire want
of every kinde that englond doth afforde
it rather hath a plenty then a skant
with eyther cloath for skynne & for y° back
what needfull thing then doth our Norftk. lack.
Ech Soyle it hath, land, pasture, meade & woode
the heath &  ffenn, the marish, lake & meare
to beastes & fowle a shelture & a foode
wherby y* doth so great a plenty beare
One half the sea the rest sweet streames surrounde
& so with fish no less it doth abounde.

but if they might be equally devided

finis 22 April, 1595.
#   Shack,—the right ofpasturage over other men’s lands in the winter months, chiefly  prevalent in Nor-
folk and Suffolk. “Lands   able to shack are called ‘ half-year lands,” being common      the year and
private the rest.   This still continues in many parishes where there has been no inclosure of commons.

## p. 044 ##

This  singular poem on  our own county contains much curious matter.  In
stanza 5, the statement that the produce of wheat in Norfolk was ten coombs an
acre is worthy of observation. Either the coomb or the acre is different in mea-
sure to what it now is,  or it  could only apply to  the richest portions of land,
where sheep had been folded, or  much manure used; perhaps the explanation
is  that  the  land was sown with wheat at  rare  intervals,  say ten  years apart.
Wheaten bread was not the food of  the masses at  that period, but oaten bread
or rye bread, so that the breadth of wheat cultivation was small.  Two horses and
one boy to plough and till the ten acres seems a large allowance considering the
slovenly methods of agriculture at that time.
One third  of Norfolk  being common land (stanza 13) was undoubtedly a
great evil, and Henry Gurnay’s arguments against such a system are sound.   I
presume the  right  of commonage became limited to certain houses in parishes,
after his time,—new comers not being allowed to interfere with the old common
right owners.  The introduction of turnips and other green crops in the winter
renders the system of half year lands incompatible with modern agriculture.
Fuller in his Worthies notices the tendency in  the inhabitants of Norfolk to
go to law.—* Such the skill of our common people hereof in our common law—
if I  must go to law, I  wish them  rather  of my counsel than my adversaries—,
but this peculiarity seems gone by.

& stone & gravell weyes to mend hath every wher Inough.”
The initials in the first stanza of those gentlemen in Norfolk who could afford
to spend  £1000 a year, I  conjecture to  mean Bacon,  Coke,  Calthorpe, Drury,
Gawdy, Grey (De Grey ),Jerningham,Kerville,Knyvett,Lestrange,Paston,Pettus,
Spelman and Townshend. I  should not have thought with Henry Gurnay, that
the proportion of  wealthy gentry and yeomanry either in  his day or at the pre-
sent was small as compared to other counties.

But English men the Somer next
in Spain did both aryve
And drave the Spaniards to their holde
As storme doth bees to hive
They sact their townes & burnt their corne
their vines they cutt and spoyled
and sixteen thousand but with six
they overthrew & foyled.*
®  The expedition thus referred to was undertaken in 1589 for the purpase ofdeterring the Spaniards from
attempting a second armada.  Sir John Norris and Sir Francis Drake, with an English force, landed at
Lisbon (Fortugal being than under the dominion of PhilipII. of Spain), anddestroyeda large fleet ofHanse
town vessels,     thathad come with the supplies for the Spanish fleet.   The sickness of a warm climate com-
Ppalledthem to return home, but the object was attained of preventing another attempt on England on the
part of   Spain.

## p. 956 ##

while thirty daves and forty miles
they marcht in Spanish land
they found no forc could them compel
but to recoyle or stand
thre dayes at least at Lisbon gate
they watch & warde did make
without Constraint by Spanish force
the trenches to forsake.
Till sicknes through y° heat of sonn
the change of foode & ayre
Compelled them for only helth
to England to repayre
with victory & little loss
by any their occasion ;
but otherwise befell to them
Intending our Invasion.
Two cities* in America
the noble Capteyn Drake
against the Spanyards did assaut
& valiantly did take
and with the treasure therein founde
his shipps did loade & freight
as much as they Conveniently
could bring away for weight.
He yerly sinc rich prizes have
from Spaniards taken some
®  Carthagina and St. Jago are probably intended, but Drake also obtained  possession of  St. Domingo0
and St, Augustin,

## p. 957 ##

as well that from America
as Calecut* doe come
thus diversly our prudent prince
through subiects faithful vnion
hath foiled the greatest Europe prince
for treasure and dominion.
317.
& lastly now in ninety six
our noble generull
the essex erle & Howards two
lord & vice admirall
the Welthy Spanish City Calez
did sack deface & hurne
& forct them forty shipps to fyre
before they did Retorune.t
w® being frovght with necdfull thinge
to serve his Indian Coast
such disappoyutyng to the kyng
causd gricle &  dumage most.
they two of his apposiles] tooke
the Phillip & the Powle
for Riches charge & goverume’t
the chiefest of them all.
Thes taken without anv loss
they safely brought awey
we prises did suffise the charge
of viage to defrey,
®  Calicut on the Malabar coast, originally a Portuguese settlement.
This expedition, in 1596, under the Earl  of Essex and Lord  Howard of Effingham, Lord High
Admiral, was completely successful in destroying the Spanish fleet and stores at Cadiz, and the town was
taken.  Lord Thomas  Howard was also one of    the generals in command,      ing                       the ‘ Howards two.”
The object of this armament was to prevent the Spanish government  from attempting another invasion of
England.                           {  Apostles,—twelve ships so named.

## p. 058 ##

beside the welth the Capteynes won
& Souldyers thendre get
through rifling of the City first
then Prisners free to sett.
thus diversely our prudent prince
with faithfull subiects vnio’
hath foyld the greatest Europe Kynge
for treasure or dominion.
All laud then lett us give to God
that thus so many weyes
Our quene & relme doth both defend
& unto honor rayse.”
This poem on England is interesting, as shewing the feeling of an English
country  gentleman on the public  affairs  of  the  country at the time.    The ob-
servations in stanzas 7 and 8, as to the several classes imitating the appearance
and habits of those above them, have been made at  all  periods, and it is to be
inferred, apply to human nature generally; but  they are curiously pourtrayed
in these verses.  The great advance in  prosperity  and  civilization in the reign
of Elizabeth is well  described by  Henry Gurnay, and in very great measure,
may be attributed to the vigour and wisdom of her government.
The more we reflect upon the responsibilities of her mission in the world, the
more we must be impressed with Queen Elizabeth's  capacity, and with the fact
of her having been a most important instrument in  the hands of Providence.
The internal peace which prevailed in her reign is  remarkable, and the security
of the husbandmen (stanza 14) and of all classes which resulted, created a large
accumulation of national wealth, which, whilst it increased the prices of eom-
modities (stanza 12) fourfold, increased in a still larger proportion the power of
purchase (stanza 13), the precious metals abounding, and plate and  jewels five-
fold what they  had been heretofore (stanzas 13 and 15).    So that, although
taxation was heavier in  amount, it  was lighter as a grievance (stanzas 16, 17,
and 18).

## p. 959 ##

The discovery of America, abont a century before, and of the passage to India,
by the Cape of Good Hope, were events which brought with them a vast change,
and to the nations of  commercial enterprize great wealth eventually.  They
must be considered as amongst the  most  striking  occurrences in the history of
the human race.  It is indeed a wonderful subject of contemplation that the dis-
covery of the precious metals in  America, and in  our own times in Australia -
and California, should, in each instance, have been cotemporaneous with a large
expansion in the circumstances and pursuits of the human family.
There are many rent rolls, leases, court rolls and other  documents copied in
Henry Gurnay’s register, principally of the three manors of Hingham Gurney’s,
West Barsham and Great Ellingham, also of Irstead.  The rent rolls of Hing-
ham Gurney’s are of much interest, from their great antiquity.   The earliest are
those of Edmund Gurnay, who lived in the reign of Edward III. and Richard II.
(p. 357 of our Record) ;  of  Thomas Gournay I.,  15th Henry VI. (1436) ;  of
William Gurnay IV.,  his son, 1st Richard III.  (1483)  and 6th Henry VII.
(1490) ;  of Anthony  Gurney, 33rd Henry VIII.  (1541),  also 2nd and 3rd of
Philip and Mary  (1555),  and of the years 1573, 1578 and 1580.   I  give short
extracts of some of these.

Ellingham Magna.
There are various rent rolls  of  demesne lands and others of this lordship ;
also, leases and indentures of  agreement. Amongst others a  fine, and the
lawyer’s bill for it.   This fine was the settlement of lands between Sir Edward
Chamberleyn, son of Sir Robert Chamberleynby Elizabeth Fitzralf, and Anthony
Gurney (see pedigree, p- 865),  according to which these fiefs came to theissue
of Maude Fitzralf, wife of Sir Robert Conyers, from failure of male issue of her
brother John Fitzralf.   By this it appears the estate of  the Gurneys at Elling-
ham consisted of 1860 acres and twenty pounds rent.

The bond tenants were those who originally held lands by villein  tenure, and
who have ended in modern copyholders it would seem. The ferme tenants were
those who paid the landlord part of their produce instead of rent in money.
