## p. 077 ##

Court Roules of y* manor of Irsted  from the first yeare of  K. Hen. y* VII.
-untill the xxj of Hen. y* VIII  contey. xxviij  Rolls deliuered by Christo. Seyve
gent. late fermor ther to Tho. Gleane Alderman of Norw® after he had bought
the mannor, deliuered on the 24® Sept.  A° 1584  as appereth in an noate vnder
the hand of the said Christ. Seyve written on the closure of y* first volume of y*
actes and monumentes.
The sayd mannor of Irsted withinsaid sould by Hen. Gurnay to Tho. Gleane
‘Alderman with covenant the  sayd rentes of  iij" xij* x!  (19 oates) 31 hens & i
capo. or may be yerly payd vnto the sayd Tho. Gleane as lorde.
Allso that ther 71 acr. lond med. past. merch demeane 60 acr. of water in som
sort severall. occup. by Jho. Bonselle.”
By these documents it seems that the manor of Irstead was about a century in
the possession of the Gurneys, having been the portion of  Ann Heydon, who
married William Gurney V.,  which marriage took place about the year 1484 ;
and Henry Gurnay  sold it to  Thomas Gleane in 1579.   It had been leased to
Christopher Seyve, who married Ela Gurney, aunt to Henry.
It is to be observed that  under the  feudal  system the lords of  manors held
their demesne lands  in fee, and let them to farm  tenants for money rents,  rents
in kind, or services, as might be agreed upon ;  frequently by leases obtained by
a fine ;  but generally retaining a large proportion of the demesne lands in their
own hands, the produce of which they themselves consumed or sold, as occasion
required.
Other lands held of  them by  free tenants (liberi),  by  nativi*  or  villeins
(villani),  were such as were subject to customary fines, payments or services,
which did not alter.   These have become in  modern times quit rents and fines
certain ;  the arbitrary  fines being  liable to change.   The villein tenure is sup-
posed to have originated the copyhold tenure, which was by copy of court roll
in the lord’s manor.  A very large proportion of the land was, however, lord's
waste or common.  Over this at remote periods the lords granted rights of com-
monage, which became vested eventually in certain houses of each village, the
"  ®Native tenentes,—sunt (ipsi etiam liberi) qui terram tenent nativam—hoc est nativorum servitiis obnoxiam.
~Spelman’s Glossary,
lord reserving the right of sheep walk,—the sheep and the wool being perhaps
the principal sources of revenue in those early times.   At a later period part of
the  common  lands  was cultivated,  which  created what  are called  ¢     half-year
lands,” which are arable lands,  over  which in the winter  months the common-
right owners had the right of feeding their stock. Henry Gurnay mentions that
in his day one-third of the whole county of Norfolk was waste or common.
Of the payments in kind occurring in these early rent rolls of the time of Ed-
ward III,  a hen or capon, or a pair of gloves, occurs;  and a day’s work or days’
works in autumn, the time of ploughing  and cleaning the soil.   For one acre,
three bushels of barley was the rent;   for four acres, two quarters of barley ;  for
three acres, one quarter four bushels of barley ;   rent of a cottage, twopence and
a day’s work in autumn.    The  monev  rents are strangely low:   4d. an acre for
a year’s rent of arable land ;  but a meadow at Hingham, called the Wromeadow,
of eight acres, let  for  fourteen shillings a year, or 21d. an acre.  Pasture land
inclosed produced a higher  rent,  from its rarity  and the value of the hay,  the
commons being the chief source to be relied upon for grazing cattle and sheep.
The valuation of Anthony Gurney’s manors (from the year 1556 to 1569 inclu-
sive),  given at pages 972-4, seems extraordinarily low,  and  can only be the
account of demesne lands let to tenants, exclusive of those in his own hands, and
exclusive of payments in kind and  services; at  least, I  can only so account for
the small amount.  I presume the variation in the rents from year to year must
be attributed to irregularity of payment.
Whitaker,  in  his  history of Craven, page 71, says that in the 22nd  year of
Elizabeth (1579), the  Earl of Cumberland gave a lease of a messuage and two
oxgangs of  land  for three lives for a rent of 13s. 4d. and a fine of £10,  which
would make the rent  rather more  than a shilling an acre.   He adds that at that
time the lords did  not receive more  than an eighth  part of the annual value of
their estates in money, and that the extreme lowness of rents was a principal in-
ducement with the lords of manors to retain so much demesne land in their own
hands.    These same lands in  question, in Dr.  Whitaker's time,  were let  for  40s.
an acre.   He considers one main  cause of the  influence of the great nobles in
the 16th century arose from the low rents and short leases, and the same obser-
vation would apply in a minor degree to the gentry.
In the 4th chapter of Sir John Cullum’s History and Antiquities of Hawsted

## p. 079 ##

are many interesting particulars of  the value and cultivation of land from the
11th to the 18th century.  I  copy that part  that relates to Anthony Gurney’s
tame:—
Page 229.—“ In the reign of Henry VIII.  (the year not specified), 31} acres
ef arable land were let for 1/ an acre, and 34} acres of arable land and 4} acres
of meadow for 42/, which is 1/an acre for the arable and 20d. for the meadow.
“In  1536, 4 acres of arable land  were let for 4/a year;   7 acres for 8/;   and
25 acres for 20/.
“In  1546, 2 acres 3 roods of meadow were let  for 14/ a year;  half an acre of
meadow for 2/6;  3 acres of pasture for 4/;  and 2 acres of arable land for 2.
“In  1572, 39 acres, consisting of  ‘londe  (i.e., arable land), meadowe, and
pasture,” were let for 21 years for £4 9/ a year, which is about 2/3 an acre.
“ In the same year, 14 acres 3 roods were let for 21 years for £2. 9. 2 a year,
which is about 3/6 an acre;  also 4 acres for 4/.
“In  1574 (page 232), 15 acres were let for 21 years for 18/4 a year, which is
less than 1/3 an acre.
“In  1577, 21 acres in the ‘ towne fylds and hamlets’ of Hawsted were let for
21 years for £1. 11. 8 a year, whichis  about 1/6 an acre.
“ In 1589 (page 235),  24 acres were let for 11 years for 41/8 a year, nearly
1/9 an acre.
“ By an inquisition taken at Bury, 24 Sept. this year, it appears that 40 acres
of meadow and pasture, in this village  (Hawsted), were worth £10 a year, which
is 5/ an acre;  and that wheat was 8/ a comb, barley 6/8, and rye 5/.”
And lastly, from an extract given at page 237, it appears that 231 acres 3 roods
were let for 3 years for £57. 13.9 a year, which is  nearly 5/an acre;  and 76
aeres 1 rood for £16 a year, which is  above 4/3 an acre.  Here the quantity of
meadow land is not mentioned, which being generally of greater value than arable
may account for the greater price per acre.
At page 238, Sir J. Cullum  observes: “Land,  from the above statement of
its rents,  seems to  have increased but  little in its  value  during the long  and
prosperous reign of Elizabeth ;  even the pastures of the Park, towards the close
of it, had only reached the rent of meadows 200 years before.”

## p. 980 ##

It must be borne in mind, however, how very little money was then in circu-
lation, and that what we call credit was at a very low ebb ; both of which circums-
stances would of course greatly  affect the prices  of  produce, and so the  value
and rent of land.
It  is difficult,  if not impossible,  to  ascertain the  relative  value of moneiyn
England at different periods.  This arises partly  from no good criterion being
obtainable to form a basis of calculation.  Wheat was not the food of the masses,
and its price, and that of all  kinds of corn varied, of course, with the quality of
the harvests. Therefore, no sound inference can be made from the comparative
price of grain.   Neither is there any  other article either of produce or of daily
use which is not subject to  nearly as much variation in value ;  and this applies
equally to  the prices of agricultural stock and horses.  The interest of money
might  be  supposed to be a fair test;  but here again the supply and demand of
money at the particular moment regulate the rate, and the state of the exchanges
with other countries has a main influence.  Possibly the price of sheep and of
wool might be the safest guide  in  an  investigation  of  the  value of  money  at
different periods; but even this cannot be sufficiently accurate to be fully relied
upon.
In various works this interesting subject is noticed, and particularly in An-
derson’s History of Commerce, where are tables of prices  from the period of the
Conquest to the present day.   Nevertheless, no really satisfactorv conclusion as
to the comparative value of money can be formed.                       oo
As a rough calculation, if the clear value of Anthony Gurney’s  manors in the
reign of Elizabeth was about £100 a year, according to the MS., and the rental
at that  time was about one shilling an acre,  the same land would probably now
let for thirty shillings an acre, and produce a rental of £3000 a year;  and if to
this is added the land paying rent in kind, and the commons now inclosed, such
amount would be doubled;  but this may be too favourable a view of the case,

## p. 081 ##

Amongst other peculiar conceits of Henry Gurnay, he composed rather more
than a hundred proverbs in English verse, the majority of them translations of
others in Latin;  most of which,  I  think,  were nevertheless his own.    Some of
these proverbs have much merit and originality, and I have thought it worth
while to insert them amongst the extracts from his register.  At all events they
are characteristic, although they may not entitle him to be considered a modern
Epictetus.
“ Supercilium no. nu’qua. deponendu.
to beare thy self
1.  To frame thy Jesture spech & cheare as tyme & place do fitt
May favor winn & not Impeach thy gravity or witt.
Loquaci parum credendu.
babblers regard not.
2.  Beware how thou doest leane thyn eare or be to credulous
vnto a como.bablinge ffoole or that is Infamous.
Ebrius vinum no. accuset.
3. If thou by drinkinge to much wyne becomes no man but beast
blame not the liquor but thy self or ells Refrayne at least.
Mors alterius non speranda.
4. Uppon the death of other men do thou no trust repose
sith thou thy self by many meanes thy life may soner lose.
Animus in dono estimandus.
5.  A Trifle from a p’fect firend that spareth what he can
is more of vallue then a perle giue. from a welthy man.
Mors non formidanda.
6.  To feare the death that endeth woe & maketh wey to bliss
doth shew despayre of life to come & yet small  joy of this.
SUPPLEMENT  TO  THE  RECORD
Amicorum ingratitudo ferenda.
. If thou doest Reape Ingratitude the fault must be thyn owne
for that thy self didst chose the soyle where thy deserts were sowne.
Ars arte deludenda.
. If that by feyned words or deeds a man doe woork the spyght
thy falt shall be the less if thou with like doest him requyte.
Blandiloque’tia suspecta.
. Of flatteringe clawbacke* or sweete woords I  wish the to be ware
least as by fowlers whistle birds so thou be brought in snare.
Liberi artibus instruendi.
If  thou hast children &  canst leave them neither welth nor lands
then leave them honest trads or arts to liue by witt or hands.
Culpata non facienda.
Such falt as in an other man thou doest reprove or blame
thou doblest in thy self as oft as thou comitt y° same.
Concedenda petenda.
Let thy requests be good & Just & moved in due tyme,
ells can they not performed be without  folly or cryme.
Nota ignotis non co’mittenda.
If thou dost trust but whom thou knows the less will be thy danger
in him thou may foresee the worst so canst thou not in straunger.
Obsequendu. amicis.
To gett or keepe a firend thou must have speciall eye & care
thy pleasurs & his displeasurs with him in part to share.
If as fro. fellowe thou forbeare to take all victorye
thy praise shall be no whitt the less yet gaynst his amytye.

O lord therefore how shall we us excuse

and as ou’ Joyes & princes fame abounde
so much the more gods holly praise resounde.
Some herevnto are proune by Inclinatio’
that have their minds affected to aspire
some forced be through skorne to follow fashio’
& not so much of will or ells desyre
Thus ten we see of fellowshipp to slide
to one that doth to Vertue Rise & byde.

I shall finish my extracts from Henry Gurnay’s register with the following-
prayer, which is  equally and singularly applicable to the present period, in
which the British crown is again worn by a queen, as it was in his.
* Prayer
for church our prince & peace
this prayer never cease.
Lord long preserve & graunt our noble quene
true wisdom faith feare love & earnest zeale
all heresyes & secret foes & seene
to quaile as oft as light shall them reveale
unto thyne owne most glorious praise & name
thy churches good our prince & country’s fame.”

## p. 1002 ##

It is to be observed that Robert Holditch, the father-in-law of Francis Gurney,
was steward to  the Duke of Norfolk,  which may account for  members of the
Duke's family being sponsors for Mrs. Helen Gurney’s children, independent of
any connexion which may have been with the Gurneys, who had been amongst
the dependents of  the house of Howard.   In  the * Memorials of the Howard
family,” by Mr. Howard of Corby, the ages of these sponsors to Francis Gurney’s
children are given, and it is remarkable how young they were when they under-
took  the  office of godmother,—Lady Jane Howard  being at the time  only ten
years old, and Lady Catherine only seven. Undoubtedly it must have been by
proxy ;  and I  believe it was not uncommon for persons of high consideration to
stand sponsors when they were themselves very young.  The widowed Countess
of Surrey was godmother to another of Francis Gurney’s children, a year and a
few months after her husband's execution, although Anthony Gurney, the father
of Francis, was still living,  who had been foreman of the Grand Jury  which
found a true bill against the Earl of Surrey.    The particulars are as follows :—
Lady Jane Howard,  born 1537 or 1538,—godmother to Frances Gurney, De-
cember, 1547.
Lady Catherine Howard,born probably 1541 or 1542,—godmother to Henry
Gurney, January, 1548.
Frances Vere, daughter of John 15th Earl of Oxford, married the Earl of Sur-
rey, 1532, who was beheaded 1547,—godmother to  Anthony  Gurney (son of
Francis), March, 1550.
There is an unravelled mystery hanging over the trial  and execution of the
accomplished Thomas Howard Earl of Surrey.
NOTE 152.—ANTHONY  GURNEY  AND   SUSAN  PALGRAVE.  (page 458.)
They were married  at Hempstead by Holt, in the county of Norfolk, as ap-
pears by the register there.—1586.
“ Mr. Anthony Gurnye & Susan Palgrave married y* 7th. of Apr.”
I have said his daughter Elizabeth married ¢“   Leedes, a preacher ;” but  this
does not, as I  supposed, prove a puritanical tendency.  The preachers were such
of the clergy as were licensed to preach, the bishops being indisposed to give

## p. 1003 ##

these licenses in  the early period of  the Reformation. It is possible that  the
person here intended was William Leedes, minister  and preacher of St. Mar-
garet’s church at Lynn, from the 1st of August,  1602,  to the 3rd of November,
1628.   He  was buried in St. Margaret's church, and had this inscription over
him  on a brass plate :—*“ Here lieth  the Body  of  William Leeds, who was
‘minister and preacher in  this church 26  years.  He lived 80 years, and died
November 3rd, 1628.”
Note  153.—EL1zZABETH  GURNEY.  (page 458.)
Elizabeth Gurney’s first husband was Thomas Golding, Gent.   She is said to
have had Bendyshe of  Bumstead,in Essex, for her second husband, but this is an
error, for her second husband was Leonard  Doddington, Gent.   Both  her  first
and second husbands lived at West Barsham, which  they  hired of  her  brother
Henry Gurney, as also did she herself in her widowhood.
Henry Gurney’s Register.
Frances and Anne Gurney, her sisters, married, the former William Bendyshe
of Bumstead, in Essex, and the latter Francis Bendyshe, his cousin.
Pedigree of Gurneys—H. Gurney’s Register.
Note  154.—THE FAMILY OF  BLENNERHASSET.  (page 462.)
The branch of this  family  which settled in Ireland received large grants of
land from Queen Elizabeth, on the  forfeiture  of the  Earl of Desmond’s estates.
Most of  these they still  possess. They have intermarried with  many of  the
principal families in the county of Kerry; and were created baronets in the last
century.—(Playfair's Family Antiquity, vol. VIL., p. 897.)
There were in England many offshoots from the parent stem in Westmore-
land ;  one of these was possessed of Ludham Hall, in Pettistree, in Suffolk, and
held lands in Campsey Ash, where they gave the  Communion plate in 1641.
The Blennerhassets  of  Barsham,  in  Suffolk, were a junior  line  from  those
.  at Frenze, in Norfolk.   They possessed Hasset’s Hall, in Pockthorpe, near Nor-
wich.

## p. 1004 ##

The  above engraving of  Hasset’s Hall was taken from a picture of the old
mansion in my possession. It  stood on the site of the present hospital of the
modern barracks, in the part of Norwich called Pockthorpe, and in the parish
of St. James's.    This  mansion,  formerly called  the Lathes,  was  leased  of  the
Dean and Chapter of Norwich by the Calthorpes, and afterwards by the Blen-
nerhassets, William  Blennerhasset having obtained a lease of it  in  1547.—
(Blomefield, vol. iv. p.  428.)   It  was described to  me by  an old  man and
woman named Nelson, who are Poctorians (natives of Pockthorpe), as a brick
building with a court-yard before it, and a flight of stone steps up to the door.
It was surrounded by a large walled garden, of which some part remains at  the
back of the barracks.  These elderly people remembered the old house well before
it was taken down about 60 years ago.  It was supposed to be haunted, and the
soldiers in the hospital of the barracks have been often disturbed by  strange
apparitions!  The brother of  my  informant (Nelson)  was, when young, in
Hasset’s hall, and saw an apparition of a dead body roll across theroom!   The
old woman told me, with a grave face, there was a  closet in  the old mansion

## p. 1005 ##

which never had been opened, and no one knew what was there; and the doors
of two rooms had been plastered up, in attempting to  open which  two people
had been struck blind.   The strange tale ran that old “ Hasset” often “rode”
in his coach and four over Bishopgate and over the tops of houses—the coach-
man and horses without heads, and when the whip was cracked, flashes of fire
came out which illuminated the whole city !*
Hasset’'s Hall was the manor house of Pockthorpe, which was originally a
separate manor, and suburb of  Norwich—having been severed from Thorpe—
hence its name, meaning little Thorpe. Pockthrope contained within it the mo-
nastery of White  Friars, founded by William  de Cowgate, in 1268.   William
Gurney IV. in his will (page 403), calls himself of  Pockthorpe, and I  do not
doubt lived occasionallyin  this  old  house, with  the  Calthorpes, who then held
it, and one of whom he married, it being the custom for the various branches of
a family to have rooms in their mansions—meeting in the hall for meals—some-
thing in the style of  our  colleges at  present.  William  Blannerhasset, Esq,
obtained the lease of this mansion called the Lathes, from the Dean and Chapter
of Norwich in 1547, on the surrender of  the ancient lease by Dame Jane Cal-
thorpe, his relation, she having been daughter of John Blennerhasset, Esq.
Close at hand to Hasset’s Hall,  but  on  the opposite side of the river, stands
Hasset’sTower now remaining.—]It is in St.Helen's parish, and is more commonly
#*  A like tradition exists at Barsham,in  Suffolk,  whereit is believed  ‘  Old Blunderhazard” drives out
every Christmas eve,  just before midnd ht, in a coachand six, to visit Hasset's Tower at Norwich, and to
return to Barsham before he ma;           ' the
the moming air.   The Barsham story makes the horses without
heads and yet fire flashing out

## p. 1006 ##

called Cow Tower.  It was probably named Hasset's Tower from being inha-
bited by some of the Blennerhassets, at some period;  or perhaps the command
of it entrusted to them, it  being the principal tower of  defence of  the city of
Norwich on the river side.  It was re-built by the city in  1378—and called
the  Dungeon.—It  is of brick,  the  windows  cased with  stone, and  was large
enough to  hold  a somewhat numerous garrison.—It is  circular, with  a  cir-
cular stair-case on one side.
In one of the parish books of  St. James’s are some curious entries respecting
the Blennerhassets.—They are licences from the curate and churchwardens to eat
meat during the time prohibited—Ilong subsequent to the reformation.—They are
as follows :—
« Memorand—That I,   John Barnham, curate—
in the city of Norwich, so far as in me—
the law in that case intends, do give lic—
daugh’. of Mr. Raph Blenirhasset of the—
and in weake state of body, that—
law exhibited for the form and letter—
The licence formerly made was inserted in  the church register February 27,
1621.
JOHN BARNHAM, Curate,
NICHOLAS HERNE, Churchwarden.
Memorand—That I, John Barnham, Curate of  the prsh of St. James, have
given licence to Raph Blenerhasset, Esquire,  and  to  his daughter Rebecka, to
eate flesh in the time prohibited—the  state of their bodyes requyring the same,
the law so pmitting, and have in the register booke of  the saide parish entered
the record thereof in the time by the law appoynted so to do, being after eight
days of the granting of the said licence, and have hereunto set to my hand Mar.
22, 1632.
JOHN BARNHAM, Curate of St. James,
NICHOLAS STRICKTON, Churchwarden.

## p. 1007 ##

Memorand.—That I, John Barnham, Curate of the prsh of St. James, accord-
ing to the lawe* in  that case pyded,  have give license to Rebecka, the daughter
of Ralph Bleverhasset, Esquire, of  the same prsh, to eate flesh the tim of this
Lente, during the time that the disease of her body shal continue.  The same
here recorded by me, March 20, 1636.
JOHN BARNHAM,
Curate of St. James.

There were Hassetts, or  Blennerhassets, at  Lynn, as appears by the parish
registers.               |
Walter Hassett, son of William Hassett and Frances Parker, was baptized at
St. Margaret's church, 20 March, 1609-10.
Robert Hassett, son  of  Robert Hassett, baptized  at  St. Nicholas’ chapel, 26
May, 1643.
And in  the church of Caistor, by Yarmouth, is a costly monument of John
Blennerhasset.  ¢    Stirpis  inter  Icenates ultimi.,”   He  died 11 Cal. June, 1704,
aged 52.
It  appears, by Blomefield, vol. 1, p. 146, that John Blennerhasset, buried at
Caistor, was the last of the family seated at  Frenze.    At Mr. Hill's, at Caistor,
whose sister he  married,  Blomefield  saw an  ancient  canvas, surrounding  two
rooms, painted with the matches of the Blennerhassets.

parishioners licenses to eat meat on fish-days under certain circumstances, particularly in case of sickness.

In  the will of  Thomas  2nd  Duke  of  Norfolk,  Sir Thomas Blennerhasset of
Frense is called his servant, and was named one of the executors.—Test. Vet.,
vol. II., p. 604.
In the will of Sir  John Cormwallis, Knt.,  John Blennerhasset of Barsham, in
Suffolk, is mentioned as his son-in-law.—Test. Vet., vol. II., p. 715.
John Blennerhasset was buried at Barsham,  near his wife,  29th June,  1573.
—Dodsworth’s Coll., Bodleian Library, No. 154.
Edward Etchingham of  Barsham married Eleanor or  Anne, daughter of
Erasmus Paston of Paston and Mary Windham.  Erasmus was the eldest son
of Sir William Paston, the Judge, and Bridget Heydon.
*  This Ralph Furneys, or his wife,is  clearly * my cosen Furnise,” to whom Henry Gurney lent Saravia’s

## p. 1009 ##

Note  155.—THoMAS GuURNAY  III.  (page 462.)
Thomas Gurnay was born on the 10th of May, 1572, at Barsham by Beccles,
the residence of his grandfather, John Dlennerhasset, Esq.   Thomas Blenner-
hasset, his uncle, and Thomas Colby, Esqrs., were his godfathers, and the Lady
Warner, godmother.    He   married,  at Denham  in   Suffolk, 10th of  November,
1596, Martha daughter of Iidward Lewknor, Ilsq.—(See page 884.)
It is stated that Thomas Gurnay died in the life time of his father; but this is
a mistake, as appears by the inquisition held  at Watton, of which an abstract is
given below.   We there sce that Henry Gurney died 23rd February, 1615, and
Thomas Gournay, his son, about a year after, that is 25th February, 1616.
INqQuisiTiO  Post  MORTEM.
Inquisition was held npon the death of  this Thomas Gurnay III., at Watton,
25th May, 14 James I. (1616), in  consequence of  a brief directed to the Es-
cheator ofNorfolk, dated 22nd March, 13th James I. (1616); by which it appears
that Henry Gurnay his father was seised of the manors of West Barsham, Southall
and Gauntshall with their appurtenances ;  and of the manor of Ellingham Magna
or Ellingham Hall with its appurtenances, with court-leet or view of frankpledge
belonging to  the said manor; as also of 60 acres of land in Ellingham Magna,
Depebam, Attilborough, Hyngham, and Rockeland, together with  the Advow-
son of the minor  part  of  the Church  of Attilborough; and of divers parcels of
land in West Barsham and Skulthorpe.  And  that a  fine was levied between
Edward Lewkenor, Lisqr., Robert Jermyn Knight, and John Heigham, Knight,
querents, and Henry Gurnay and Helen his wife deforciants, of  the aforesaid
manors, lands, tenements, and advowson, to  the need and use  of  Thomas Gur-
nay and Martha his wife, and of their heirs lawfully begotten ;  failing which, to
the heirs of the said Henry lawfully begotten.  This to take effect after the death
of the said Henry Gurnay according to an Indenture made between the respec-
tive parties above named, dated 25th Octr. 38 Elizabeth (1596).  Moreover that
Henry Gurney was seised of  the Rectory of the Church of West Barsham, and
of  the Advowson of  the Vicarage of  the same church, and that by Indenture
bearing date 7th Jan. 45 Elizabeth (1603) these devolved by way of  jointure
on Thomas Gurnay and Martha his wife.   And that Thomas Gurnay was seised

## p. 1010 ##

of divers other tenements and parcels of land in Great Ellingham and West
Barsham;  and that by deed bearing date 19th Feb. 13 James I. (1616) he
enfeoffed Robert Osgoby of the Rectory of West Barsham with its  appurte-
nances, together with  the Advowson of  the Vicarage of the same church, with
the tenements  above  mentioned,  to  be held for  the use of Martha his  wife and
her assigns for sixteen years, and then to the use of Thomas Gurnay, son of the
said Thomas and Martha, for ever. And the Jurors further say that the aforesaid
Henry Gurnay died at Great Ellingham 23d Feb. 12 James I. (1615), and that
Helen his wife had died before him, and that Thomas Gurnay (his son) named
in  the brief died at  Great Ellingham  25th Feb. last past (1616); and that
Martha his late wife survived him, and was living at Great Ellingham ;  and that
Edward Gurney was his  son and heir, and at  the time of Thomas Gurnay’s
death was under age, being seven years, eleven months, and twenty two days old.
And  the  Jurors say further  that  the  manors  of West  Barsham, Southall,  and
Gantshall, with their appurtenances were held of  Sir Edward Coke, Lond Chief
Justice, as of  the honour of Castor and manor of Castleacre, at one knight's fee
and a half, and were worth £20 per annum;  and that the manor of Ellingham
Magna with its appurtenances, and the Advowsoa of the Church of Attilborough
was held of Robert Rich, Knight, and (dame Frances his wife as of the honour of
Wormegay at the tenth part of a Knight's  fee, and were worth £13.  8s. 8d. per
annum;  and that the premises in West Barsham, together with the Rectory and
Advowson of  the Vicarage of  the Charch there, with their appurtenances were
held of the King as of his honour of  East Greenwich in Kent, in fealty and in
free soccage, and  not in  capite,  or  by military service, and werc worth 6s. 8d.
per annum ;  and that the two pieces of land in Skoulthorpe were held of Thomas
Fermor in  free soccage, and were worth 12d. per annum;  and that the other
tenement with its appurtenances in  Ellingham Magna was held of  Sir Philip
Knyvet, Bart., as  of his manor of Le  Close,  at the annual rent of 4d., and was
worth 6s. per aunum.
From the original in the Public Records in the
Custody of the Master of the Rolls, and pre-
served in the Rolls Chapel ; to wit :—Escheats
15 James I;  part 2, No. 126.

## p. 1011 ##

Again, in consequence of a Commission dated at Westminster 26th. Feb. 15
James I. (1617), Inquisition was taken at Harleston 23rd March in  the same
year, upon the death of  Thomas Gurnay, Esqr.   And the Commissioners say
that divers parcels of land in West Barsham were lately bought of Thomas
Farmer, lisqr., by Henry Gurnay, father of Thomas Gurnay.  And also that the
Rectory of the church of West Barsham, with its appurtenances, and the Advow-
son of the Vicarage of the same church, which by a former Inquisition were said
to have been held of the King as of the manor of East Greenwich in Kent, in free
and cominon  soccage, are now held in capite by military service, but  at  what
part of a Knight's fee the Jurors know not.
From the original in the Public Records in the
Custody of the Master of the Rolls ;  and pre-
served in the Rolls Chapel, to wit :—Escheats
16  James I;  part 2, No. 10.
Nore 156.—HENRY GURNEY. (page 463.)
This Henry Gurney was probably dead before the year 1633, as his name is
omitted in the account of his fainily by his brother Francis.—See page 524.
Nore  157.—EDMUND GURNAY. (page 463.)
His  tutors  at  Queen’s College,  Cambridge, were Mr. Robert Rudde, of  St.
Florence, iu South Wales, and  Mr. Henri  Godby,  of Onehouse, Suffolk.—See
preface to his work called ‘  Corpus Christi.”
He was fellow of  Corpus Christi  College, but  vacated his fellowship upon
marrying, as appears from the following memorandum, in an old order book be-
longing to that College :—
Magister Gurnay pronunciatus non socius Collegii per consensum magistri et
septem sociorum com ratione omissionis necessarize ob matrimonium. Cessionis
voluntarize testibus litteris  propria manu scriptis et ad Magistrum Osburne*
* Thomas Osburne, afterwards minister of St. Peter Mancroft, Norwich, who married Edmund Gurney's
sister Aane.
1012                                  STPPLEMENT TO THE  RECORD
missis.  Indulgentia pro fruitione stipendii usque ad Annunciationem proxime
sequentem.”
The Master of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, when Edmund Gurney
was fellow there, was Dr. John Jegon, Chaplain in Ordinary to Queen Elizabeth,
Dean of Norwich in 1601, and Bishop of the  same in 1602;  so that Edmund
Garney’s differences with him were both as Master of the College and as Bishop.
Fis discipline at  college was so severe that  with  the fines inflicted on  the
nndergraduates he repaired the College Hall,  on the screen of which one of the
sufierers wrote this couplet :—
“ Dr. John Jegon of Benet College Master
‘            Broke the scholars’ heads, and gave the hall a plaster.”
Which being seen by the Doctor, he added :—
“ Knew I but the wag that wrote this in his bravery
I'd commend bim for his wit, but whip him for his knavery.”
Was Edmund Gurney the wag >—See Manship’s Yarmouth, page 251.
From a MS. account of all the livings in the diocese of Norwich by A. Harri-
son, for the use of Jegon, Bishop of Norwich, 1603,  it appears that at the time
Edmund Gurnay was Rector of Harpley the living was worth £22 a year.

Edmund Gurney was buried, and probably died, in St. Peter's Mancroft
parish, Norwich, where Thomas Osborne, his brother-in-law, had been minister
(p, 709).   This is certain from the following extract from the Register of Burials
of that parish, 1648:     May 14th, Mr. Edmd. Gurny  Minister of Harpley in
Norfolke.”
From the entry in the Institution  books at Norwich, it appears that  George
Heyhoo, A.M.,  was instituted to  the living of Harpley, vacant by the death of
* 8y.—Synodals. {  The purcio Lewensis was what had formerly belonged te the priory of Lewes.

Edmund Gurney, Clk., on the presentation of Robert Heyhoo, of Watton, Gent.,
on the 15th of May, 1648, only one day after Edmund Gurney was buried.

With regard to the inscription outside the chancel of Harpley church (see p.
466), in examining the stone on which it is cut,  my conjecture is confirmed, as
in the defaced part, after the word Protestant, the letters — RN  AY   are trace-
able, and there is little doubt that G V preceded them.
Edmund Gurney was probably in  needy circumstances—in an ancient ac-
count book at Hunstanton of  Alice Lady Lestrange, it  appears that £20. was
lent to him.   “1637. Nov. 7.   Lent to Mr. Edmund Gurney to be payd on the
Sir John Hobart, to whom Edmund Gurney dedicated his work against images,
was member for Norfolk in the Long Parliament, and took the Solemn League
and Covenant.—Lady Frances was daughter of  the Earl of Bridgewater.—Sir
John’s  brother,  Sir Miles  Hobart,  was a  strong  Parliamentarian;  his son, Sir
John Hobart, 3rd baronet, married Mary,  Oliver Cromwell’s youngest daughter,
and widow of Col. Hamond, who had the custody of Charles I. at Carisbrook.
The Hobarts intermarried with  the Heydons and Blennerhassets, and in that
way were distantly related to  the Gurneys of  West Barsham.—See Noble's
House of Cromwell, vol. II., p. 127 ;  also Anecdotes and Traditions, published
by the Camden Society, p. xxiii.
John Stubbe, probably of the family of Richard Stubbe, who married Eliza-
beth Gurney, was a  strong puritan in the reign of Elizabeth,  and published a

## p. 1014 ##

pamphlet against her marriage with the Duke of Anjou.   In consequence of this
he suffered amputation of his right hand and imprisonment in the Tower.—See
Marsters Hist. of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, old edition, p. 427.—
This John Stubbe was of Thelnetham, in Norfolk.
Note  158.—ANTHONY  GURNEY.  (page 467.)
To this Anthony Gurney administration was granted of  his  father's will  or
testamentary paper, dated May  1614, 31st October 1623, nearly eight years after
Henry Gurney’s death, he having died 23rd February 1615.
On the 17th November, 1603, a suit in Chancery occurred between this An-
thony Gurney, his  father Anthony Gurney (obviously so called by mistake),
and Andrew Womocke, of a house and lands in Harpham, the lease of which
was claimed by Robert Rowse, of I{arpham.
Anthony Gurney was twin  with  his brother Francis, and born  September
Nore  159.—EL1ZABETH   GURNEY.  (page 467.)
In an old register book of St. Martin's at Palace, Norwich,  is the Baptism of
this Elizabeth Gurney :—
« Elizabeth daughter of Henry Gurney Gent. baptized 9th August 1573.”
She married, 7th February, 1591,  Thomas Moulton,  son and heir of Auditor
Moulton, of the Exchequer, who after marriage “sould his law and reserved all.”
Henry Gurnay’s Register.
Auditor Moulton was lord of the manor of Wicklewood, in Norfolk.   Thomas
Moulton, his son, sold this in 1695.   Unless — Salford was her secondhusband,
her marrying to that name is erroneous.
Note  160.—THOMAS  OSBORNE.  (page 468.)
Blomefield gives the arms of Osborne arg. on a bend between 2 lions rampant
sable three dolphins of the first.
Thomas Osborne was fellow of Corpus Christi College,  Cambridge,  and had
a dispute with Jegon, the Master.—(See Lamb’s Masters’ Hist. of Corpus Christi
College, p. 341.)—He was afterwards assistant minister of St. Peter's Mancroft,

## p. 1015 ##

Norwich, where he died, and was buried 2nd November, 1642, and his son Tho-
mas 5th November, 1864.   This  family  were of  Seething, and John Osborne
was Mayor of Norwich in 1661.
Note  161.—ABIGAIL GURNAY. (page 4638.)
Abigail Gurnay appears to have married — Capps, and to have had a daugh-
ter, Elling Caps.   This is shewn by the will of Susan Gurnay of West Barsham,
her niece, (to be given afterwards,) in which  she and her daughter are legatees :
—  Item I  give to my aunt Abigail Caps five pounds, and to her daughter Elling
Caps forty shillings, both to be paid within  three months after my decease—.”
See page 477.
Nore  162.—PEDIGREE OF  LEWEKNOR. (page 470.)
Three alliances by marriage took place between the Russells, of Chippenham,
and Oliver Cromwell's family.   Sir  John  Russell married Frances, the Protec-
tor’s daughter, and widow of the Honorable Robert Rich.
See Noble's House of Cromwell.
Also Anecdotes and Traditions published by
the Camden Society, p. VII.
Note  163.—EDWARD GOURNAY. (page 471.)
It  appears by  the Inquisition  taken  on  his father’s  death, which happened
25th Feb., 1616 (see p.  468), that Edward Gurnay was then seven years,
eleven months and twenty five days old.  He  was therefore born 5th March
Licence of Alienation.
In the Patent Roll,  6 Charles I. is  a licence to Edward Gurnay to settle 54
acres of land in  West Barsham on Frances his wife.—Trustees Richard Hovell
Esq. and Richard Hovell his  son.—The following is an abstract :—
By letters patent dated at Westminster 1st March, 6 Chas. 1. (1631), licence
is  granted to Edward Gournay, Esq., to convey to Richard Hovell, Esqr., and

## p. 1016 ##

Richard Hovell  his son, for  the need and use of Frances his wife, in  full satis-
faction of her dowry, and after her death for the need and use of the heirs male
of  the said Edward and Frances lawfully begotten, and in defect of such heirs
male, for the need and  use of the lawful heirs  of the said Edward for ever, fifty
four acres of land with  their appurtenances in West Barsham, which were held
of the king in capite.
See Patent Roll 8 Charles I ;  part 21, No. 76.
Edward Gurney lived at the beginning of the melancholy troubles of the civil
wars; but died in August, 1641, before the conflict had assumed the form of  abso-
lute warfare.—In 1639 the state of Scotland had induced Charles]. to raise an army
to oppose the Covenanters ;  and I  find in the Tanner MSS., in the Bodleian li-
bary, No. 177, a roll or list of such knights and gentlemen of Norfolk who sent
horsemen and  money  to aid the royal cause on this occasion.   Amongst  these
Edward Gurnay contributed one harquebusier, complete, named Thomas Baker,
and £20 in money.  The Lord General of the army was Thomas Earl of Arundel,
Earl Marshal, and Lord Lieutenant ofNorfolk ;  the Earl of Essex was Lieutenant
General.  They marched to Berwick, where the King placed himself at the head
of the troops; but after some skirmishes a treatv was begun and peace concluded;
in consequence, the King’s forces were disbanded.  The great differences between
the King  and Parliament were not  fully developed in  this year, 1639.  It will
be observed that the Earl of Essex, afterwards the great Parliamentary General,
was second in command;  and  in the  list of those who in  Norfolk at that  time
contributed men and money to the royal army, will be found names of persons who
afterwards were zealous partizans of the Parliament. I have thought it worth the
while to print this roll entire,  as a document of much interest as it respects our
A Rol or List  of the names of such Knights  and Gentlemen in the Countie
of Norff.  that  find and sent the service of Horse to his Excellence the
Lord  Generall for  the  service of  his  ma® in  the Northerne parts.
