## p. 1023 ##

Wee have receiued L'res from his sacred Ma™. our  gratious king and also
from y* right  honorable y* Earle Marshall Lord Generall, our Honorable Lord
Lieutenant of this County of Norff: requireing us to signifie unto yo® how ac-
ceptable his Ma™. tooke yo* free and voluntarye furnishing of the service.
This letter at the end is without signatures, but  I presume it was from the
Commissioners of array for  Norfolk, addressed to  each individual contributing
men and money.
INQuisiTIO   PosT MORTEM.
By virtue of a brief, dated at Westminster, 16th August, 17 Charles I. (1642),
and directed to the Eschaetor of Norfolk, inquisition was held at East Dereham,
13th October following, upon the death of  Edward Gornay.   And  the jurors
say that at  the time of  his death,  the  said Edward  Gornay  was seised of  the
manors  of  Great Ellingham, West Barsham, North Barsham,  otherwise called
Southall in  North  Barsham, and Houghton, otherwise called  Gantshall in
Houghton, with their appurtenances ;  and of all the messuages, lands, tenements,
&ec., in the aforesaid manors ;  as well as of 54  acres of land with their appurte-
nances in  West Barsham, which Henry Gornay, Esqr., his grandfather, lately
purchased of Thomas Farmer, Esqr.   And that by indenture made 23rd March,
8 Charles 1. (1631), between the said Edward Gornay, Esqr., on the one part,
and Richard Hovell, Esq. and Richard his  son on the other part, for and in con-
sideration of the love and affectionhe had towards his wife, the daughter of  Richard
Hovell, senr., and for a certain sum of money paid to him by the aforsaid Richard
Hovell,  the  father, and  for her  jointure  and  competent  perferment, &c., &ec.,
according to forms of law by fine the said Edward Gornay secured to the said
Richard Hovell  in  favor of Frances his  daughter, wife of  the  said Edward
Gornay, the manors, lands, tenements, &c., aforesaid, for the need and use of the
said Frances during the term of her natural life, in full satisfaction of her dowry,
and after the death of the said Frances, then for the need and use of the heirs
male of the said Edward Gornay and Frances his wife, &c,, &c.   And the  jurors
further say  that  the  said  Edward  Gornay made his  last will and testament in

## p. 1024 ##

writing, on   the  6th  Nov., 1639, and appointed Frances Gornay, his  good
wife, Susan Gornay,his eldest sister, and WilliamDavy, Esq., his brother-in-law,
and also his sister, Ellen  Gornay, his  executors ;  and  that  he died  on the  6th
of August last past before the taking of  the Inquisition ; and that Henry Gornay
was son and next heir of the said Edward  Gornay  by  Frances his wife, and at
the time of the death of his  father was nine years, seven months, and fourteen
days old.   And, moreover, the jurors say that the manors of  West Barsham,
North Barsham, and Houghton,  at  the  time of  the  death of  Edward Gornay,
were held of Robert Coke, Knight, as of  his manor of Castleacre, by military
service, but  at what part of  a Knight's fee they know  not, and  that they were
worth £13 per annum;  and that the 54 acres of land in West Barsham were held
of the king in capite by military service, but at  what part of a  Knight's fee
they know not, and that  they were worth £1  per  annum;  and  that the manor
of Great Ellingham, &c., was held  of Robert Rich, Earl of  Warwick, as of  his
honour of Wormegay, by military service, but at what part of a knight's fee they
know not, and was worth £6. 6s. 8d. per annum.
From the original in  the Public Records, in
the custody of  the Master of  the Rolls, and
preserved in  the Rolls Chape;l to wit :—Es-
cheats xvii. Charles I. ;  part 3, No. 60.
Edward Gurnay also left  a son named Edward, mentioned in the will of his
aunt Susan Gurnay—but probably posthumous. (See his will, page 477.)
Nore  164.—THOMAS  GOURNAY.  (page 476.)
By inquisition taken after the death of  his father, the Rectory of West Bars-
ham with its appurtenances were settled on this Thomas Gournay,—(see page
1010), after sixteen years, from 19th Feb. 1616, when he perhaps came of age,
and would therefore have been born about 1611.

## p. 1025 ##

The arms impaled by this Thomas Gurnay, on his wife’s monument in Nor-
wich Cathedral, are those of  Thwaites, of Hardingham.  It seems that Bridget
was probably daughter of Anthony  Thwaites, Esqr., of  Hardingham.    She was
heiress with her eldest sister Elizabeth, who married Jeffrey Cobbe, of Sandring-
ham, Esqr.   But  in  Norris’ MSS. it  is  stated  obiit ccelebs ut  videtur.”—
Anthony Thwaites settled his  estates upon his  father and brothers, and away
from his daughters.
It  appears by  the Register of Salle, in Norfolk, that ¢     Thomas Gurnay, Gent.,
and Bridget Thwaits, were married there 29th Decr., 1641.”
NoTE 165.—StusaN GURNAY.  (page 477.)
The will of  Susanna Gurnay, of  West Barsham, single woman, in  the office
of the Archdeacon of Norfolk, dated 10th June, 1642, and  proved 19th August,
1645, gives legacies to her sisters and their children; and to her elder brother's
children, Henry,  Francis (a  daughter),  and  Edward ;  ten pounds  to Thomas
Gurnay, her godson, son of  her uncle, Francis Gurnay;  and a legacy to her
aunt, Abigail  Capps, and her  daughter, Ellen  Caps;  appoints her brother,
Thomas Gurnay, Esq., of Lincoln’s Inn, and her sister, Elling Gurnay, execu-
tors.—Her signature, “ Susanna Gournay ;"—the  seal an  engrailed cross in a
lozenge.
WILL    OF   SUSANNA  GURNAY.
In the name of God Amen,—the tenth day of  June in the yeare of our Lord
God one thousand six  hundred fortie & two.   I  Susanna Gurnay of West Bars-
ham in the Countie of Norffe singlewoman being in perfect health understand-
ing & memory att this present time (thankes be to God for it)  doe make ordaine
&  declare this my last  will & testament in  manner following First I bequeath
my soule to Allmighty God my most mercifull & loving Father humbly intreat-
ing  him  for the merits of  his  deare sonne Jesus Christ my  only  Saviour to
pardon & forgive me all my sinnes & so long as I  shall live & continue in this
world so to direct & guide me by his Holy  Spirit that I  may live to his glory &
to feare him in all my waies words & actions, that after this sinfull life ended he
may receive my soule amongst the rest of his  elect & chosen servants, my body

## p. 1026 ##

I  committ to the care & discretion of my Executors & friends  to be buried after
the usuall & ordinary manner of Christians in  hope & certaine assurance of the
resurrection of it into eternall life att the great and terrible day of iudgement—
Item  I  give &  bequeath unto Martha  Smith my  deare and  loving  sister five
pounds to buy her a Ring to  weare for my sake as a  remembrance of  my love
Item  I  give  &  bequeath to  Elizabeth  Crowe my  deare &  loving  sister five
pounds to buy her a Ring  to  weare for my  sake as a remembrance of  my love
Item  I  give & bequeath unto Margaret Davy my  deare and loving sister five
pounds to buy  her a Ring  to weare for  my sake as a remembrance of  my love
Item I  give &  bequeath unto  Francis Gurnay  my deare and loving  sister late
wife to my brother deceased five pounds to buy her a ring to weare as a remem-
brance of my  love Item I  will and bequeath unto Henry  Gurnay my  eldest
Brother's sonne twenty pounds to be paid him when he shall be eaighteen yeares
of age as a  remembrance of  my love Item I  give and bequeath unto Francis
Gurney my Elder  Brother's daughter twenty shilling to buy her a Ring to wear
for a remembrance of my love when she shall be ten yeares of age that she may
remember that  she had such an Aunt Item I  give &  bequeath unto Edward
Gurnay my Eldest brother’s sonne and my Godson fiftie pounds to be paid him
when he shall be eaighteene years of age and fortie shillings every yeare untill he
come to eaighteene years of age and then to have his fiftie truely paid him Item I
give and bequeath unto Christopher Crow & Elizabeth Crow my brother Crow’s
children twenty shillings a peece to be paid to them att ten yeares of age to buy
them Rings to weare for my sake in  remembrance of my love and to remember
they had such an Aunt Item I giveand bequeath unto  Martha Crow my sister
Crow’s daughter and my Goddaughter ten pounds to be paid her when she shall
be eaighteen yeares of age Item I  give & bequeath unto my loving Cosen Judith
Cattaline* my  Aunts’  daughter twenty  shillings as a remembrance of my love
Item I  give unto Mary Davy my sister's danghter twenty shillings to be paid her
when she shall be ten yeares of  age to  rem’ber me Item  I  give unto  Thomas
Gurnayt my Uncle Francis Gurnay’s Sonne and my Godson ten pounds to be paid
him when he  shall be eaighteene yeares of age Item I   give and bequeath unto
Elizabeth Ringstead my  God-daughter twenty  shillings to be paid her within
* Elizabeth Lewknor, sister of Susan Gurnay’s mother, married Thomas Catlyn, Esq.. of Blofield,
t  Ancestor, apparently, of the late Sir   John Gurney, Baron of the   Exchequer,

## p. 1027 ##

one yeare after my death if she live  so long Item I   give  & bequeath to all my
god-children that my sister Elling Gurnay knows off to some rings of ten shillings
apeece & to the rest Bibles as she shall  thinke most fitt I  leave it to her to per-
forme if she shall  live a yeare after my  death, if she should die before, I  desire
my brother Thomas Gurnay to doe it for me Item I  give unto my Aunt Abigail
Caps five pounds & to her daughter Elling Caps fortie shillings both to be paid
within three months after my decease Item I  give unto the poorest in New Wal-
singham ten shillings, to the poorest in  Fakenham ten shillings, to the poore in
Sculthorpe ten shillings  to y* poore in  West Barsham twenty  shillings, to  the
pore of  North Barsham five  shillings, to y°* pore of East Barsham five shillings
to  be paid  within six or seaven daiss after my decease Item I  doe ordaine &
make Thomas Gurnay my brother of  Lincolns Inn Esqr. & Elling Gurnay my
sister sole Executors of  this my  last will  and testament & I  give unto them all
my Sheepe Monies Bonds & all  other my  goods &  Chattels whatsoever they
truely paying out of it all legacies given by me in this my Will.
Susanna Gournay.
Wittness
Fredith Cattline
Tho. Russell
Nore  166.—DoRroTHY GURNEY.  (page 477.)
From the Register of St. George's Tombland, Norwich, it appears that « Mrs.
Dorothea Gurney was buried Novemb. 14. 1641.”
Nore  167.—ELLEN GURNAY.  (479.)
From  the parish Register of  Southburgh, Norfolk, it  appears that  * Robert
Long Esqr. and Mrs. Ellen Gurnay were married the 24th day of August, 1670.”
As Thomas Gurney, father of this Ellen, was dead before 1616, there must be
some error here, as she would have been near 60 years old at her marriage, and
could not  have had  three children.   I  am disposed to think  the Ellen Gurney,
who married Robert Long,  was daughter of  Thomas Gurney and Bridget
Thwaits,—See page 476.—This doubt is confirmed by the fact that Ellen Long,
who married Richard Ferrier, was born in 1675.
Turner's Yarmouth Sepulchral Reminiscenses.

## p. 1028 ##

Mr. Long, of Spixworth, descended from Robert Long, has no documents re-
specting this marriage.
AA  AAAAAL
AAI    AIAN,
From Add.MSS. Brit. Mus. 14. 417 {. 108, Phil cxcvii,—on Norfolk funerals.
“ An escucheon g. a saltier eng. or. on a chief or three cross crosslets gules
imp®. ar. a cr. ing. gules.
Robert Long  of Remerston in the com. of Norfolk  Esq. dyed the 6th Decr.
1688 and was buried at  Remerston aforesaid—he had 4 wives first ................
daughter of  ............ Miller  Alderman of Lyn in Norf.  by whom he had 3 sons
vid‘. Robert, Charles and Thomas.—2'% married .................... daur. of  Judge
Bacon by  whom he had Francis Long.—3™¥ daur. of  Sir  Wm. Hovell Knt.
Relict of Gourney of Barsham by  whom he had no issue.—4*7 Ellen daughter
of Thomas Gournay of West Barsham in Norfolk by whom he had Ellen—the
sons and daugther all living—at the funeral was 12 escucheons in silk.”
The family of Ferrier possess a seal Gurney arg’. a cross ingrailed gules im-
paling Smith of  Yarmouth gules on a chevron arg'. between three handfuls of
barley, each containing five ears or as many bees proper.  I  find  no  record of
this marriage.
By the execution of this seal it seems of about the latter part of the 17th cen-
tury.    Another more ancient, simply the coat of Gurney, was given to me by a
member of that family.

## p. 1029 ##

Nore  168.—MARGARET  GURNEY  AND   WILLIAM   Davy.  (page 478.)
Margaret Gurney was the youngest* sister of Edward Gurney, after the death
of whose children she became co-heiress with her sisters of  the estates of the
Gurneys.  West Barsham was leftby Henry Gurney, her nephew, to his wife;
at least, so I  understand his will (page 485).    Great Ellingham fell to the share
of Margaret Gurney and her husband; I  suppose partly by purchase. These
two estates appear to have been all that remained of the ancient territory of the
Gurneys on the death of Henry Gurney II.,  the last of the West Barsham line,
who  died  in  1660.   William Davy was of an  old  Norfolk  family, settled  at
Gunthorpe and Ingoldesthorpe.  He was a barrister of Lincoln’s Inn,  and ap-
parently in considerable practice.  He was greatly in the confidence of the Gur-
ney family, several members of whom appointed him their executor.
In the collection of Norfolk letters in my possession, mentioned at page 840,
are many of the Davy family.   Those of this William Davy I have consideredit
worth while to insert here;  and there is one of Margaret, his wife.    The contents
of them are of some general interest, and illustrate the tone of the period.  Several
of the early ones are from West Barsham, and of the latter ones from Great El-
lingham, of which WilliamDavy was eventually in possession. Most of these let-
ters are sedled with the arms of  Davy,—sable a chevron engrailed ermine between
three annulets argent. Some of the letters have lost the address,but they are all
written to  members of  the Gawdy family, chiefly to Bassingbourne Gawdy,
Esq.,  of  West  Harling.   The Gawdys were seated there and at Gawdy Hall,
near Harleston.   They descended from a French knight,  who was taken pri-
soner in the wars of Edward III., and established himself in Norfolk.   William
©  These sisters are not numbered somrectly in  the  * Record of the House of Gournay,” at  page  474.
They are all mentioned in the will of Edward Gurnay, doubtless according to their ages.                    ‘

## p. 1030 ##

Davy and his wife address the members of the Gawdy family as kinsmen.  I  do
not discover what the relationship was.  Gawdy bore for arms, vert a tortoise
passant argent.
The great cause in the Court of Star Chamber, alluded to in this letter, issued
in the severe punishment, by fine and  imprisonment, of Bastwick, Prynne, and
Burton ;—Prynne was put in the pillory a second time, lost the remainder of his
ears, and was branded.
I have spoken with Mr. Fountaine concerning theManner of  Iiccles w* was
p'fered to S* Charles  le Groos who did  refuse it, the p'ticular being  ouer rated.
But since Sergeant Reeue hath desired y* refusall of it, p’tending he would buy
it for S* Charles Groos.  But  I  beleeue he doth like y* Booteman, roweth one
way & looke  another, but y° euent will  expresse what  he aymeth at.   Howso-
euer I  am advised by  Mr. Fountaine to  let it rest a little  & I  shall  heare from
him as occasion shall offer it selfe, But I  p’ceiue by him  (though I  haue not a
p'ticular (vnder writeing) y* it is rated vij*. et modis at 19 year* purchase at neare
fourescore pound ¥ ann. but he saith and doe beleeue y* it is ouer rated.
(Mens hominis nouitatis auida est)  wherfore to  satisfie you® desire I  have
resorted to y* most eminentest places wher 1 might heare some, videlicet Paules
&  Westminster, wher I  am informed y* y* title of  y® bookes w™ were burnt in
Smithfeild was an introduction to a deuoute life made beyound y* seas in latine
& translated out of latine into English by y* last Archbpp.  Abbots  chaplin,  And
came now to be reprinted againe & to be approued by y* now Archbpp®. chaplin
who did  correct many things therein, but  it was afterward by some indirect

## p. 1031 ##

dealeing inserted againe; The Archbpp. did desire it might be publikely spoken,
in Starchamber y* he had noe hand in it,  for what with y° papist & y* puritaine
he is much spoken of though it be undeservedly.
The great cause in Starchamber for y* p’sent is ag" one D*. Bastwick a phisi.
tion, M". Burton a diuine, Prinn, Peter Wetherick’s sonne of Norwich & others
for diuulgeing of bookes ag* y* Bishpp'. amongst whom Bastwicke in Star.
chamber ore tenus did humble desire he might answer for himselfe viua voce
(being ag*  y* Bishopp® & y* he  might be  suffered to  puttin  a crosse bill ag®
them) for w* he had a p’sident, eauen S'. Paul Act. xxvj. who was p'mitted to
speake for himselfe he ag" whom ye text more at large doth sett downe.  The
sicknes (Laus  sit  deo) did  decrease Last  Weake xxvj.    And here is nothing’
y* speaking y* ther is  in  y° country therof yet  I  doe not heare but y* they are
verie  careful  & verie strict in punishing  delinquents, for at this p’sent ther is a
fishmunger putt in  prison haueing as much irons of him as he can beare both
setting vpp & lying downe for  opening his  shopp & selling his com’dities, one
of his  house being dead of y° infection, & both he & y*° residue of  his seruants
goeing  vp & downe w*® might be a cause of further spreading of it, & what fur-
ther punishment can be afflicted vppon him I  beleeue he will find it.   Ther is
a strange opinion here amongst y° poorer sort of  people (as I  am informed)
who hould it a matter of conscience to visite ther neighbour® in any sicknes yea
though they  know it  to be  the infection, w™ must  need*® be a great cause of y*
further spreading therof, wherfore as farr as I  can heare y° cheife of y* sicknes is
amongst y° poorer sort.   I  haue spoken with Mr. Brampton about you® land in
Garboldsham, who saith y* he feareth at  last you® best course will be to settle
y* respect of  Homage & y* you  haueing you" assurance both  from y° Earle of
Arundell &  y* feofes w® being  a double  conueiance you will be  inforced  to a
double p'don, But if you  purpose to walke y* way let  me heare from you & I
will doe you y* best assistance I  can.
Since my first  writeing of  this letter Mr. Fountaine hath brought S* Thomas
Hopton to me who doth hasten Mr. Fountaine to know Sergeant Reeus answer,
for he hath  need of  pfsent monie & much  desireth  one intire payment toward
y* payment of S* Owin Smith's debts, for some of his debter* be verie clamarous.
I had some conference with  him about it who is  verie willing I might haue it
easpesially because from me as ther necessitie require all y* monie will be p'd

## p. 1032 ##

downe.  I  shall heare more before y* next weake wherfore I  desire to haue some
directions from you.   And I  shall doe as for myselfe.
The maulting busines for Norfl. stagereth & is generally thought will p'sently
fall to nothing.   Here are many monopolies spoken of but what successe they
will haue it is doubtfull, amongst other® ther is meanes y*          may* haue y*
sole selling of seacoale throughout England ;  I  see a letter from a frind of mine
wherin was written of a Minister in  Lei’stershire who vppon  Easter day last  at
the time of y*® receiueing of y* Sacrament did p’swade his parishioner* to follow
his example to drinke healths in  y° church, who did begin 3, y* one to the father,
2 to y® sonne, 3 to y° H. G. and ther is now an other minister about putting him
out of his liueing.   Soe with my best wishes to you  & your® I rest
You" assured loueing friend
to dispose quo ad vires
from y* Golding Anchor in Fleet Street                                       GUL. Davy.
right  ouer St Dunston’s Church 9 die
Maii 1637.
A postscript
Ther is a p’clamation come out for y*
restraineing of goeing into New Iingland.
No. 2.
I have sent downe but haulfe y° quantitie of oliues & caper* you desired because
ther are as yett none soe good as ther will be about y* latter end of the Tearme.
And then if I  shall heare from you what your desire is I  shall doe accordingly.
I  did lodge at Newmarkett vppon Munday at night wher noe lodgeing could
be afforded at y* signe of y® graiehound to any, it being wholely taken vpp by
S* Edmund Bacon S* Drue Drury & M* Barrow all going for London,S* Thomas
Richardsont also lodged in y* Towne y* night also, soe y' I had companie suf-
ficient to  conduct me safely ouer Newmarkett heath w™my  charge, wherby
those feares were taken away w* you did putt me into. etxc.
The greatest & truest newes here is of y°* morocco Ambassador whose p’sent to
*  In  1687, the right ot buying coal from Newcastle and the adjoining parts was farmed by a company,
who paid a shilling   per chaldron to the King.—Pictorial History of England, vol. IIL, p. 569.
t  Sir  Thomas Ri           n, of Honingham, in Norfolk, Father  of  Thomas Richardson, Lord Richardson
and         on

## p. 1033 ##

his Ma™ of  Barbarie horses & saddles is of great value And also a great quan-
titie  of  Barbarie gold  (vt  loquuntur nonnulli).  Because his  Ma   Shipps
this last su’mer did great service in driueing out certaine pyrates out of an Iland
belongeing to y* Prince, soe y* it is thought it will be verie com’odious for Eng-
land in ther Trafficke ther.   His  audience at Court  will be vppo. Sunday next,
he hath not been well since he did arrive in England.  Here is a Poland Ambas-
sodor also and hath been heere longer than y* Morocco, But cannot have audi-
ence, wherat ther is much exception taken And it is thought he will depart in a
discontent, But  as Iam  informed his Ma  will haue him take  notice of  some
wrong y* was offored by y* King of Poland fairely p°tending a match w® y* Pal-
segraues sister but afterwards p’ceeded in y°* Emperour® familie.
Ther was a Bill this Tearme p*ferred ag™  y* Bpp. of Lincolne but it was taken
of y* file,   But it is said y* an other will be putt on verie suddenly.
Mr. Caesar one of y° six Clarks died uppo. Sunday last and great means ther
is for his place, He hath left a rich widow behind him & child lesse, yett young
—My  Brother &  my Cousen Webb* doth remember ther respect vnto you, so
with my best wishes to you" selfe & y* rest of my loueing friend® with you I rest
You" assured loueing friend &
kinsman.   Wu.  Davy.
I  desire y* you  would  not  expect a Letter from  me vppon  Thursday  next,
But y* Thursday after (Deo non  p’hibente) I  will  not  faile &  then I  shall
write voto you of new matter.  iteru vale.
from y* golding Anchor in
Fleet Street iijdie 9** 1637.                                           (No address.)
Wheras S* Thomas Hopton y® Lady Smith & M* Smith of Winson had
agreed vppon a day for y° counsell of all part’ to have seen y* euidences, wher'by
they might p’ceed to y° finding of an office, ther being a day also agreed vppo. on
all sides: soe it hath happ’ned out y* all  y° writings were taken out of  S* Owin
Smith’s closett (as the report is by y* Lady  Smith) wherfore ther can be noe
p'ceeding as yett  to y*  finding  of  an office,    And  S" Thomas was gone for
¢    William Davy’s mother was Elizabeth, daughter of Richard Webb, ofIxworth, Suffolk.

## p. 1034 ##

London y*day I  came to Norwich, neither did  I  speake with  him, wherof I
thought good to signifie voto you y* you might not keepe you" mony any longer
for Eccles but weyt of y° next opportunitie that shall be offered voto you.
I  have sent you here inclosed M* Mingay his acquittance for y* receipt of  vj*
w I  did p’mise to lay out fof you.
The constant report in Norw™ is  y* Breda is  yealded vpp, soe with  my  best
wishes to you'selfe & y* rest of my friend® with you 1 rest
You" assured friend
& kinsman to dispose
Wu.  Davy.
from West Bassam
die S* Michaelis 1637.
Sir Owen Smith, of Irmingland, mentioned in the above letter, was connected
in  some way with  Francis Gurney, of London  (ancestor of  the Gurneys of
Keswick).—See appendix C., page 584.   They  appear to  have entered into
speculations together, which were not successful, and Sir Owen Smith probably
died  in difficulties, as this  letter  seems to testify.    The Smiths, of  Irmingland,
intermarried with the family of  General Charles Fleetwood, Oliver Cromwell's
son-in-law.  Blomefield vi., p. 325.
I  would desire you to deliver voto my man that bagge of fiftie pound®
w*® I left w* you & this my Letter shall be a sufficient testimonie & discharge, soe
with my best respect to you'selfe I  rest
Your assured friend
& kinsman to dispose
Wu.  Davy.
from West Bassam
To his worthy and much
respected friend & kinsman
Framlingham Gawdie Esq.
at his house in West Harlinge
these,

## p. 1035 ##

No.  5.
These are to informe you y* S* Tho. Hopton had been at my lodgeing
before I  cam to London & left word ther y* he would speake w*®me, vnto whom
I  did repaire, who tould me y* y°* Lady Smith  had been verie earnest with  him
for to sell y* m™  of Eccles & aston she being much solicited by y* Creditor* of
S*Owin Smith &  by some of  them sued, wherfore she hath p’cured M* Jermie®
Sonne y* Counsellor to buy  both those  manner® y* price vnto him  sett is 2300"
& he offereth 2200" & to take them both  uppon seeing noe  other writeing® then
y® conueiance from  S* Owin Smith to  S* Thomas & S" Arthur  Hopton, but I
beleeve he  will see more before he doth part w® his monie: But S* Thomas
Hopton p*ferreth me before other* & telleth me y* his mind is y* I  shall haue it
but what y° event will be I  cannot tell.
The newes here is y* y* diuine who accused Judge Hutton of  treason was
fined at King’s Bench 5000" for offering offront to y* place of Justice & also to
weare a paper  in his  hatt  written w® certaine Letter* w® w* he  is  to goe into
Westminster hall & into euery Court of Justice & imprisonment ad placitu Reg.
The Judge also is left to his action at law.
I  haue spoken w* y* clarke in y°®Exchequor concerneing vp’hall busines who
cannot  stay ‘issues (though I  p’mised him  content)  & y*y* Shriefe must  pay
them w* he  speaketh will come to viij®.   I  was at  M* Bafeild® Chamber  last
night who is not as yett come to towne.   He is y* onely man may make a stay
heerof in y* alienation office for thar it is to be done I  will repaire voto him when
he is come to towne, and will doc therin us for myselfe.   If I  be not at Harling
by Thursday night in y°® next weake you" Liecles busines will be the  cause for
ther will  be somecthinge concluded this  tearme as I  thinke Barronett Wiseman
is  fined  tenn  thousand pound® to  his Ma™ S’uce. to  y*  L? Keeper,  one  to
Judge Jones and  also  he  is  to  pay  a  1000" to  M® Tomson  y° IL? Keeper's
One Pickering a Recusant is also censured in  Starchamber to  loose his eares
& to be boared through y* tounge for saying the King was a Papist in his hart &

## p. 1036 ##

y* all Protestants were Heriticks & makeing a Hoges sty of y* Church yard soe
not troubling you any further, w* my best wishes to you'selfe I  rest
kinsman to dispose
from y* golding anchor in Fleet Street
right ouer St. Dunston’s Church 7 die
Junii 1638.
I  sent my man  the other day vnto  you to desire you y* some servant
of you™ might be sent to Woodrisinge vppon Tuesday next by 9 of y* clocke to
take part of y° bucke w*® I p’mised you, and y* I  would send my man to be ther
also y° night before : But my man telleth me y* you did send word vnto him v*
morneing he came away y* you did  intend to meet  ther you'selfe I  shall desire
you to send me word directly by this bearer and I  will meet you ther myselfe if
I  can possible.
I haue receiued a note now from Mr Bafeild  what monie he hath laid out for
you  busines concerning Garboldsham w® cometh vnto  neare iiij® w*® I  shall
show vnto you when I  mcete with  you soe with my  best wishes to   you'selfe
I rest
You’ faythfull friend
& kinsman   Wu.  Davy,
from West Bassam 7
The following letter is without date or  address, but  was probably written in
the year 1639, when the question of  ship money was in  agitation, and the
mutiny in Scotland occurred, mentioned at page 1016 :—
According to my p’mise y* this Thursday I would writte vnto you wherin you
may vnderstand y* I  haue deliuered you lette® to M* Ashly Brampton who haue

## p. 1037 ##

p'mised me  before this time to  repaire vnto  me  wherby wee might  doe  some
thinge in you" busines for the longer it be delayed the worse it will  be because
vppon every licence of  alienation ther  shall be  aurum Regina paid  w® as I   am
informed is the x part of y* w® is p’d to his Ma“  vppon euery licence of aliena-
tion, I  haue mett w* M™ Brampton againe and he hath appointed to come to me
as to morrow y* wee may p’ceede therin :  Howsoeuer  thar shall be noe  default
in me.   I  haue not  spoken w® S* Thomas Hopton as yet what he doth intend
to doe about y* sale of Eccles.   But I expect y* he should send to me or at least
speake to me about it.
The Morocco Ambassodor hath had audience who was brought through London
to Temple Barr  vppon y* 5  of  9™ in  y* afternoone by y° citty  captaines the
p’sent being 4 Barbarie horses ledd before y* Ambassador by 4 moroccoes. And
he following after on horseback attended with some 8 moroccoes on horse backe.
And y° militarie band mett him at Temple  Barr & soe conducted him to White
Hall wher he had audience And about six of y* clocke his Ma® Coach brought
him backe againe.  The last weake & this hath y* Shipp monie been argued in
y: Exchequor But it is concluded y* in a case of necessitie his  Ma" may  call
vnto his subiects for monie, And y* his Ma™ is y*iudge of this necessitie But y*
judges will  deliver ther  opinions before y° latter end of  y* tearme.  I  heare y*
it is delivered for law that those marshes w* are over flowen ¥ altos fluxus maris
are in iure coronze And I  am  certaine y* most of  o" Norff. men doe compound
w® y* King.
Ther is a new building  now at White  hall for masques & showes right ouer
ag” y* pulpitt in y° yard verie neare finished.   The Temple church is lockt vpp
y* none are suffered to  walke ther as they  time vsed to doe, & at  diuine service
morneing & euening wax candles burneing vppo. y°® com’union table, And y*
pulpitt removed to y* side of y* church.
Ther is a great mutinie in Scotland at this p'sent about oF forme of prayer w*
they should vse & other matter® concerneing Religion  soe w® my best wishes to
you'selfe & y* rest of my friend®* w® you I  rest
Your assured loueing friend
& kinsman to dispose
Gur. Davy.

## p. 1038 ##

The Queen Mother, whose entry into  London is  described in the  following
letter, was Mary of Medicis, widow of  Henry IV., of France, and mother of
Henrietta Maria, Queen  of Charles I. ;  she died  in  1642.    Anne  of  Denmark,
mother of Charles I., had died 1st March, 1618.
The  Bishop  of  Lincoln, here  mentioned, was Dr. Williams, who had  been
originally patronized by the Duke of Buckingham. He was Lord Keeper of the
Great Seal, in 1621, and  a  man  of  extraordinary  talent; he was convicted of
corrupting witnesses in the Star Chamber, and imprisoned in the Tower; he died
Archbishop of York, in  1649, after various fortunes and vacillations in his poli-
tical course.
You" desire is a command to me w* was y* I  would write down vnto
you some newes.  Suffer me to use a little Court holy water, for y* High Shreif of
Suff.  and my  selfe haue spent almost this whole day  ther, where I   did see the
Queene Mother who is a comely matron like woman who came through London
vppo. Wednesday last, it being rayled in on both sids y® Street from Allgate to
Temple barr (as I  am informed), the Liuerie on y° one side and y* Artillerie on
y° other, she doth  liue  as yett  at  St. James but  it  is  vox populi, y* His Ma®
will  purchase Hatton house &  giue it  y* Queen Mother, I  am tould  y* his
Mat   at  y°  first  meeting  of  y® Queene Mother  did  speake vnto  her  of  her
troubles, she answered y*' they were nothing in respect she was come to y* place
whar now she is.   I  am glad to here y* she shall haue out of France a hundred
thousand pound  sterling ¥  ann. wherby his Ma*  shall not be burthened.
Ther was in y*® Bill for Norff. Shreifs S* Thomas Richardson M* Pecke & Mr
Wimondham for whom means being made a 4 man was chosen namely M* Holl.
Ther was in the Bill for Suff. S* W™ Haruie M* Warner & S* Simon Dewis for
whom likewise  meanes being  made  a 4  man  was chosan namely S* Richard
Brooke, Ther is chosen one Chaworth an Irish Baron High Shreife for Nottingha.
whervppon ther is much dispute.
His Ma" intend® to be at Newmarkett vppon y° 20* of this p’sent mounth,
The B’pp of Lincolne as I  am informed is confined close prisoner.
I  cannot giue you y* least account of any of you busines hauing not had any

## p. 1039 ##

time to p’forme it, soe w™* my well wishes to you~ selfe & y° rest of  my alliance
& friend* with you I rest
You’ assured friend
& kinsman to dispose
Wu. Davy.
from y* Golding Anchor
in Fleet Street, 4 day 9
1638.
To his worthy & much respected
friend & kinsman Framlingham
Gawdy Esq at his house in West
Harling.
These.
No. 9.
I  haue receiued you~ letter wherby I  p’ceiue y* you  continue in the
same mind for  the buying of  the moietie of  the  Mannor  eccles, I  had written
vnto you by M* Rawlins but y° place could not accom’odate me w* conueniends
for writeing:  But I p’ceiue by  you" Lett" y* M" Rawlins did sufficiently relate
my mind vnto you, S* Ralfe Hopton® Solicitor did write vnto me y* S* Ralfe in-
tended well vito me to doe me any curtese. But since I  came to Norwich I  was
advised by M* Bacon to send y*® order to Judg Reeue out  of y* Court of Ward*
for the deliuering of y* writeings concering Eccles to the Feodarie of the county
of  Norff.   But  answer was returned y*it  did  not  satisfie him,  for they were
deliuered to him  by y° consent of  S* Charles Grosse who is not a partie to y*
sute:  But if time  doth  p’duce new  matter betwixt this &  y° 19 of this mounth
w® may occasion you" comeing to  Norwich you  shall heare from me But if y*
writeing® be not seene 1 cannot p’swade you to p’ceed soe w* my well wishes to
you and your® I  rest
You" assured friend
& kinsman  Wu.  Davy.
from y* kings head in Norw.

## p. 1040 ##

I  haue receiued this p'sent day an order w* have been shewed to Jus-
tice Reeue and he is willing vppon that order to deliuer into my hand® y* write-
ings concerneing eccles But y° order is y* after y° office be found I  am to deliuer
them againe into y° hand® of Justice Reeue ;  wherfore I  doe thinke itt verie fitt-
ing y* you  would come to Norwich  vppon Thursday  next or vppon Friday y*
you’ counsell & you'selfe may see y°® writeing®, y* you may resolue w* you'selfe
whether you will goe forward w* y® purchase or noe, for ther are other® about it
But I  am still   p'mised y* refusall of  it, soe w* my  well wishes to you'selfe &  y*
rest of my good friend® & allies w* you I  rest
You" assured loueing
friend &  kinsman  to
dispose WM, Davy.
from y° Kings head in Norwich
This bearer is agreed w* for iiij*. for less I
could not gitt him come.
[In the margin.)
I  desire to heare from you whether you will come or noe because I  will keepe
y* writeing® in my hands vntill I  heare from you.
I  haue inquired what is to be done about o" Tickitts & it is reported y*
y® Clarkes demand vj? in  y* pound:  wherfore I  desire to heare fro. you  what
you will  order  me to doe herein  for  yo'selfe  1  desired M' Thomas Jermin to
repaire  to you  to doe me  the favor  to lett him haue 30" & 8° in  siluer for  soe
much gold w* he  had of  mine  wherin S'. you  canoit suffer & I  shall giue you
siluer for y* same when I  come downe & take it as a favor  from you soe w* my
well wishes I rest
Your® to dispose
WiLL"  Davy.
At y* Golding Anchor
Fleet  Street  31  Jan.
1653.

## p. 1041 ##

No. 12.
Cousen Gawdy
I  haue paid yo* Eson* Rent due at o Lady last w** cometh vnto neare
43" I  shall desire you  to bring  y° colledge acquittance made for y°® rent  due at
Mich. last  for the p’sent  Bursar saith  ther  is some small arreare of  rent  then
vanpaid but y® acquittance will make it appeare.  I  was at S* Rob* Drury* lodge-
ing to speake w™ him about y*® r:nt  charge of  Garboldsham land® at Mich. last
but I   could not  find  him  ther, I  shall shew you a discharge of  all arrear® therof
vato Mich. 1656  & I  hope  you will  discharge me, I  desire to  heare from you
when you come to Harling  y* I  may haue a further debate w" you about other
busines soe w* my  well wishes to yo"selfe & children I  rest
Yo* well wishing
No.  13.
Cousen Charles
I  would desire you to make payment of  y° 9'. 10° w*® is  due to me
from my cousen W™ Gawdy w* you  know of  & also if  he hath written to you
to pay me any other su'me of monie y* you would take order therin I  shall be at
home any day this weake except Friday of y° forenoone for  I  would willingly
speak with you  about other busines soe with my well  wishes to yo'selfe & my
friend® with you I rest
Yo’. assured loueing
frie’ld & kinsman
No. 14.
Cousen Gawdy
My  occasione calleth me to  M® Spelman® of  Narborough all  next
weake soe y'I  canott come to Harling, as I  intended wherfore lett me desire you
to take 30° of S*. Willm. Gawdy w’ch is  due to me as I have informed you and
that you  would pay  yo'selfe for yo sheep out of  y' monie soe with my well
wishes to S* W® Gawdy yo'selfe & y* rest of y° familie I  remaine
Your® assuredly
®  Eson,—Easton.

## p. 1042 ##

I  haue noe monie at London if I had I  would willingly serue you therin
or in any other way y* beeth in my power         I  doe intend for London this
tearme but  shall not  be vntill y* middle of  y* next mounth  if I  had  been  ther
sooner I  should willingly haue gone & paid yo’ rent          for you but if  you be
putt  to  a  strait I   will  write to my  cousen Robt  to  gitt my  cousen Thomas
Webb to goe and for y* returning  of yo" monie to them I  haue a friend at Nor-
wich shall doe it  y® charge will be little  soe with my  well wishes to yo'selfe &
cousins I  remaine
Yo’ kinsman & servant to comand
WiLLM.   Davy.
Ellingham Mag.
If you Intend I  shall write to my
cousen Webb lett me hear soone fro. you.
No.  16.
Cousin Gawdy
I  would desire you  to send me by this bearer the note wherby Robt
Steward had of you by my  order 20' and also lett me heare from you  whether
you will goe with me to Lynn to buy coale for I  doe hear y* the fleet is expected
euery day I  can goe with you of  Thursday next  or of  Munday come seaven
night being y°  14  instant or the Tuesday after &  to meet   vppon y° road what
houre & place you will appoint &  w’ch shall best agree with your occasions but
I  thinke the  last time were  best hoping y® fleet  may be  come by y* time, soe
with my well wishes to yo'selfe & friend® with you I  am
Your* assuredly to
dispose n= Wu,  Davy.
2 Octob. 1666.
I  think y® Swan in Watton may be convenient to meet at.
For Mr. Charles Gawdy at his
house in West Harling these.

## p. 1043 ##

The following letter is from Margaret Gurney, wife of William Davy.   Lady
Ratcliffe, mentioned in it, was natural Daughter of  the Earl of  Sussex of  that
name, and married to Sir Alexander Ratcliffe, Knight of the Bath :—
No. 17.
I  received your letter in it I  am  satisfied my  cosen mall is  very well
I  pray god to continu it : I  can not send her petticoats for it is in y® chest y* you
haue y* key  of:  as for y°  other busines my dayly  expecting  of  M™. Bartlets
coming heather haue made mee  forbeare as  yet knowing y* shee is  one y* can
informe mee as much as any can and will doe as much to firthar it : I  haue sent
to her to let her know y*'I would faine see her here and shee sent me word as
soone as y* Lady Ratlife  was gone from Besthorpe she would com :  and then I
shall giue you  an acount till then and ever none shall be more redy to sarue
you or yours then my selfe and shall ever be
Your louing kinswoman
to dispose
MARGARET  Davy.
NoTE 169.—AccouNTs AT   HUNSTANTON.  (page 471 and seq.)
In Hunstanton Hall are various account books and expense rolls, some of
great antiquity;  of the  latter, the household disbursement rolls, of the  time of
Edward II.  exist, and are of great interest.   I  doubt whether any so ancient of a
private family are to be found elsewhere. Of the account books, some extracts
were sent by me to   the Society of  Antiquaries, and published by  them in the
Archeeologia, in  vol. xxv.—Alice Lady  Lestrange was wife  of  Sir  Hamon
Lestrange, of Hunstanton, and nearly related to the Gurneys, of West Barsham,
according to the following pedigree.  She married in the year 1605, and died
in 1656.   There are several account books at Hunstanton, in her hand-writing ;
from one of these I  insert entries  relating to the Gurneys and their  connexions
of the generation of Edward Gurney, who married Frances Hovell (page 471 of
the Record).  It is to be observed that Nicholas Lestrange and Ann Lewknor,

## p. 1044 ##

as appears by these accounts, were married at St. Gregory's parish, in Norwich,
and that Lady Lestrange mentions going to Malden, in both which places the
Gurneys resided.

Note 170.—HENRY GURNAY II.  (page 485.)
Henry Gourney II. was born at Hillington, his mother having been Frances,
daughter of Richard Hovell, Esq., of  that  place, and wife of  Edward  Gurnay,
Esq., of West Barsham.  He was educated at Gressenhall by Mr. Daniel Greene,
who was rector there from the year 1634 to 1658, and was afterwards a Fellow
Commoner  of  Caius  College, Cambridge,  to which society he presented a sil-
ver cup on his entrance.    These particulars appear in  the matriculation book of
that college, as follows:—
‘ Henricus Gourney filius Edwardi Gourney de West Basham in  Comitatu
Norfolciensi armigeri defuncti, natus apud Hillington,  in eodem Comitatu, lite-
ris grammaticis institutus apud  Gresnall sub M™. Greene, et novendecim  annos
natus, admissus est in  Collegium nostrum Pensionarius major in Commeatum
Sociorum 18 Feb. 1647-8  fide jussore M° Ffoster et soluit  in  ingressu poculum
argenteum.”
The age assigned to Henry Gourney in this document is  erroneous, as it ap-
pears by the inquisition taken at his father’s death (see page 1023) that he was
born 23rd January, 1631-2.  He was the last of his family in possession of the
West Barsham estate, dying in 1660 without children.
®Elizabeth one of the daughters of Sir Hamon Lestrange married Sir William Spring, of  Packenham,Bart.

## p. 1048 ##

Note  171.—SIR LESTRANGE CALTHORPE, KNT. (page 486.)
Lestrange Calthorpe was knighted at Whitehall, 26th Feb., 1674.   He married
Anne, daughter of Arthur Turnour, serjeant at law, and father of Sir Ed. Turnour,
(ancestor of the Earl of Winterton,) by whom he had Arthur andLestrange, who
died S.P., and James, drowned at sea in 1691, and who by Elizabeth Cooper, his
wife, had one son  and two  daughters who  all  died  S. P.—(From information
given by  J. R. Planché, Esq., Rouge  Croix.)   Perhaps there is  some error as to
these children of  James Calthorpe dying S. P., as Blomefield states that West
Barsham came to the Morleys from the Calthorpes.  Sir Lestrange Calthorpe is
said, by the same authority, to have purchased West Barsham of Henry Gurnay
IL. ;  but it was in fact sold after his  death, probably by his widow, to whom he
bequeathed it.
NoTE 172.—Potts oF  MANNINGTON.  (page 488.)
Sir John Potts, Bart., father of Sir Roger, was one of the members of Parlia-
ment for the county of Norfolk during the civil wars, and took the solemn league
and covenant.  There is mention  of  him  or  of  his  son in  ‘  Gests and Anec-
dotes,” see page 482.   I  visited Mannington in  1852.   A part of the ancient
house remains,—probably a gate-house,—apparently of the 15th century.    The
house was surrounded by a moat, and near it was a chapel, now in ruins.  Man-
nington is now the property of the Earl of Orford.
The family of Potts were, I   think,  somehow allied  to the Gurneys of West
Barsham before the marriage of Mary, the daughter of William Davy, Esq, and
Margaret Gurney, with  Sir  Roger Potts.   In West Barsham Church is a slab,
with the following inscription :—
Hic jacet corpus Barbare primogenitae
Rogeri Godsalue ar. quond.uxoris Johis
Potesei ar. cui prolem peperit Annam, Will,
Et Barbaram. Moritura vitam
Filiz  dedit et nomen,—Dolorem
Marito sed tamen pietatis ex-
emplum.  Obiit die Martis etatis
sue anno vicess’. quinto. 1615.

## p. 1049 ##

Roger Godsalue, Esq., was of Buckenham Ferry.
I  have stated that   Susan  Potts,  the  heiress  of this family, married Matthew
Long, Esq., of Dunston.   The present family  of  the Longs is not descended
from this marriage, all  the issue of  which  is  extinct, but  from Judith, the sister
of Matthew Long, who married Henry Davy, of Swardeston, and their descend-
ants assumed the name of Long.
NoTeE 173.—ROBERT  GURNEY  OF   AYLSHAM.  (page 497.)
In the year 1626, October 10th, Robert Gurneye of Aylsham, Gent., conveyed
to Henry Croft of the same place, Gent. two pieces of land in Aylsham ;  one of
three acres for forty pounds, and another of two acres tor forty-five pounds. The
price of £22 an acre seems very high for  the period;  it was probably land in,
or close by the town.
NoTe 174.—GURNEYS  OF  DARTMOUTH  AND  LoNDON.  (page 498.)
A Richard Gurney, perhaps the Richard named in the pedigree, was rewarded
for services done in the war in Scotland, in which he was Captain of Hadington.
He received two hundred marks, by order dated 22nd May, 1550.
Great disputes arose on the death of Richard Gourney, Alderman of London,
in his family.   His widow Anne (not Jane) married again to  John Durninge,
between whom  and  Thomas  Gurney the  son, a suit  in  Chancery arose, 22nd
January, 1604.
“Page 39.   Richard Gurney of London p. R. Cooke Claren. y* 20 of March
1577  entered in  the Visitation  of  Essex a® 1634  by George Owen and Hen.
Lilly.—Arg. a cross eng. gules—in first  quarter a cinquefoil blue,—crest—on a
cap of maintenance or turned up erm. a fish in pale head downwards gules.  All
Essex arms and pedigrees collected out of two
visitations, by Samuel Walker, 1672.—Raw-
linson MSS. Bodleian Library, No. 304.

## p. 1050 ##

SiR THOMAS  GURNEY
was Knighted at Theobalds, 22nd March, 1612-13.—See names of Knights, Le
Neve MSS. No. 107.—Queen’s College, Oxford.
The arms of Gournay, of  Dartmouth, Devon, were arg. a cross engrailed gu.,
on the dexter side a cinquefoil vert.
Ordinary of arms by R. Glover.—MSS. Queen’s
College, Oxford, No. 83.

## p. 1051 ##

Note  175.—VENTA IcENORUM.  (page 506.)
It appears that Norwich was the Venta Icenorum inhabited by the British and
a British town, and Caister was the Roman fortified camp near at hand.   Wil-
liam of Poictiers,  who was chaplain to William the Conqueror,  and  Ordericus
Vitalis, a contemporary, both  state that he built  a  strong fortress at Genta:
“urbs nobilis atque valens,” “ opibus et munimine nobilis urbs,” are the words
of these two authors ;  and there can be no doubt  the castle at Norwich was the
one here mentioned.    The  name, North wic,  or north fortress, may have been
so given by the Saxons from its position with  regard to Caister.  There were
three  Ventas  in  England:   Venta  Belgarum,  Winchester;   Venta  Silurum,
Caerwent, in  Monmouthshire; and Venta Icenorum, Norwich.  The latter is

## p. 1052 ##

clearly intended by Gwenta, as both  authors state the object of building the
castle there was to keep in check the northern part of  the kingdom.—See letter
to Dawson Turner, Esq., on the Venta Icenorum, by Hudson Gurney, Esq.
Note   176.—CRrROMWELLS AND  RuUSSELLS. (page  509.)
There were three marriages between the Cromwells and Russells; and Edward
Lewknor, nephew of Mrs. Martha Gurney, married Elizabeth, daughter of  Sir
William Russell ; whose grandson Sir John Russell married Frances, Oliver
Cromwell's daughter.  She was widow of the Honble. Robert Rich.
Noble’s House of Cromwell.—Anecdotes and
Traditions ;—Camden Society, p. XVII.
Note  177.—MarTHA HEATHCOTE.  (page 509.)
Martha Heathcote was a grand daughter of Gilbert Heathcote, M. D., an emi-
nent quaker physician, by Frances his wife, sister of  Sir  John Rodes, the last
baronet of the family.   She was sister of Gilbert Heathcote, who succeeded to
the estates of his great uncle Sir John, and took the name of Rodes. The family
of Rodes were quakers for  some generations, and were buried in  the quaker’s
burying ground at Hensworth-Woodhouse, Yorkshire.
These particulars from Dr. Joseph Hunter, F. S. A.
NOTE   178.—BRANTHWAITES.  (page 510.)
Henry Branthwaite of  Hethel, married Mrs. Davy, grandmother of old Lady
Potts, who was daughter of William Davy, Esq. and Margaret Gurney.
Blomefield vol v. p. 111.
The name of Branthwaite occurs in the parish Register of St. Gregory’s, Nor-
wich, and members of  the family  were present at the early marriages of  the
Gurneys, according to the custom of the Society of Friends.

## p. 1053 ##

NoTE 179.—CaAPTAIN LAWRENCE.  (page 510.)
Captain Lawrence's granddaughter Sarah Lawrence married Samuel Gurney,
who died in 1783, leaving two daughters, Mrs. Kett and Mrs. Hoare.
Note  180.—MR. ARDERON.  (page 521.)
Arderon was the miller at the new mills  at Norwich; a fellow of the Royal
Society;  an uneducated man, but of much curious observation.
Note  181.—FRraNcis GURNEY of LoNDoN. (page 524.)
Francis Gurnay, sixth son of Henry Gurnay, Esq., and Ellen Blennerhasset,
ancestor of the present family of the Gurneys of Keswick, was born 13th Sep.,
1581, being twin  with  his  brother  Anthony Gurnaye (see page 886),  Mr.
Francis Wodehouse of  Breckles,  John  Blennerhasset his  uncle, and his aunt
Elizabeth, wife  of  Thomas  Golding, Gent., being  sponsors.   * Lower than his
brother Anth. at their age of 16 years  just four inches, his voice unchanged whereas
his brother Anthony’s changedalmost a year since.”  This is his father’s account
of him at  that period (1597).   He was bound apprentice in London to Henry
Tryme, a member of  the Merchant Taylors’ Company, and * assigned over”  to
William Smooth, another member, as appears by the documents of that Com-
pany,  and moreover that     Francis Gurnay, son of Henry Gurnay of  Great
Ellinggam, in the county of Norfolk, was admitted and sworn to the freedom of
the  Merchant Taylors’ Company, on  the  16th  day  of  June, 1606.”   He
seems to  have been a man  of  somewhat unsettled and speculative habits.
He and Sir Owen Smith,  of Irmingland in Norfolk,  were certainly connected
in purchases of land  together, as is proved by  the copy of deeds, page 584.
Sir  Owen Smith was related to  the  Gurneys through his  mother Dorothy
Hopton, grand daughter of Sir Owen Hopton, by Anne, daughter and co-heir ot
Sir  Edward Etchingham, Knight,—Mary, the other co-heir of Sir Edward Et-
chingson, marrying John Blennerhasset, Esq. of Barsham in  Suffolk, whose
daughter Ellen  was this  Francis Gurney’s mother.  Sir  Owen Smith died in
great difficulties in 1637.  This is apparent in the letters of William Davy, Esq.

## p. 1054 ##

(pages 1033 and 1035), and some of  the estates which he had purchased were
then sold.   Francis Gurnay, I  have stated, (page 526), was also in difficulties
about the year 1625, when Sir Hamon Lestrange paid £100 to the corporation of
Lynn, to whom he was bound  for him, as was also Sir William Yelverton, they
having been parties with Francis Gurney and others in setting on foot a manufac-
tory in the desecrated church of St. James's, at Lynn, and which proved a failure,
Sir Henry Spelman supposes, in  consequence of its having been attempted in
what had been an  ecclesiastical building.— (History  of  Sacrilege, p. 184).—
I have given copies of  the documents respecting this  transaction at pages 531
et seq.
Francis Gurney was occasionally resident at Norwich and Lynn.   His agree-
ment with the corporation of the latter place on 11th October, 1622, being that
he and his partners Ambrose Tompson and Martyne Hill should be resident in
Lynn for three years from that date; and by the account books of Alice Lady
Lestrange, his relation, it is clear that he was resident at Norwich occasionally,
and frequently at Hunstanton.  I have given extracts from these ancient account
books at  pages 529 and 1044; but there are other entries respecting Francis
Gurney before omitted which I  now insert :                                 |

By these accounts it is evident Francis Gurney  acted as a sort of banker and
agent to the Lestranges; and probably to others;  but his condition in life ap-
parently became deteriorated after his failure in 1625.
I  do not discover in what year he married; but his  eldest child mentioned in
the parish register of St. Benet Fink, London, was Dorothy, born in 1619.
It is likely that his eldest son Roger, born in 1621, died young, as there is no
mention of him in the account of the family  given by his brother, Francis Gur-
ney, of Malden, in  1664.— (Page 539.)
It seems that Francis Gurney had an annuity of  £8  a  year charged upon
his  lands by  his  father-in-law (apparently), which he had  sold to Thomas
Browning.   This is mentioned in ¢    Royalist Composition papers,” H.M. State
Paper Office, 2nd series, xiv., 779 :—
Thomas Browninge of Essex clarke—His delinquency that he deserted his
dwellinge & went  &  lived in the  enemies quarters—&c.   He is seized &ec. of
lands in Rayleigh &c.—He claims to be allowed one annuitye of £8 per annum
to ffrancis Gourney charged upon his said lands by the last will of his grand-
father dated the 23rd April 1635—to hold during his life &c.   3 7°  1646. fyne
is 318",
I  have not  found in what  year or where Francis Gurnay,  of  London,  died.
There is no notice of his death or burial in the 1egister of St. Benet Fink.

## p. 1057 ##

Nore  182.—THoMAS GURNAY. (page 527.)
This Thomas Gurnay was a legatee in the will of Susannah Gurnay, of West
Barsham, his  cousin, to  whom he was godson.—Will dated 16 June 1642.
“Item I give unto  Thomas Gurnay my uncle Francis Gurnay’s sonne & my
Godson, ten pounds to be paid him when he shall be eighteene years of age.”—
See p. 477.
Note  183.—FaAmILY oF  BrowNING. (page 531.)
I  find in St. Gregory's parish register, at Norwich, the parish where John
Gurney afterwards lived :—
“ Adam Browning son of Adam Browning & Rebecca his  wife, bap. 13th
Also in the books of the Society of Friends at Norwich, amongst the burials :
Mary Browning ofPulham, widow.”  (No date).—See page 708, where the
burial of William Browning, son of William Browning, in 1558, at Irstead, is
mentioned.
It seems likely these Brownings, originally of Irstead, settled at Norwich and
Maldon, and had all along some connexion with the Gurneys.
Note  184.—SIR RICHARD GURNEY.  (page 534.)
He was created a Baronet 14th Dec. 1620, as appears by a copy of the patent,
in the patent roll 17 Chas. I. part 4, No. 6.
Note  185.—FraNcis GURNEY  OF  MALDEN. (page 536.)
Francis Gurney was appointed an alderman of Malden in 1662, under a royal
commission, pursuant to an Act 14th Chas. 11. for the «  well governing  and re-
gulating of  corporations.” The aldermen then appointed were Reuben Robin-
son, Gent., John Hart, Gent., Francis Gurney, Thomas Horsenaile and Henry
Symonds.

## p. 1068 ##

NoTE 186.—DANIEL GILMAN. (page 540.)
RoBERT  Grima, of Hingham.

GILMAN, of Hingham, from
whom   this   information,—
August, 1848.
The Gilmans were of Caston and Hingham for many generations. A branch
of this family emigrated to America, and founded New Hingham, and Gilmans-
town, and have been high in office there.
Some of the descendants of  Daniel Gilman appear to have been of St. Gregory's
parish, Norwich ;—in  the register there I  find  “John  son of John Gillman &
Hannah his wife, bap. 20 feb. 1660.
“ Daniel son of   John Gilman & Ann his wife, bap. 9 April 1693.”
Note  187.—JoHN GOURNEY.  (page 542.)
John Gourney was a.man of  good education, and this is farther proved by a
book in my possession which once belonged to him, with his signature, and the
date 1699, the  title of which is  “A   godlie and learned exposition upon the
whole epistle of Jude, containing three score and six sermons preached at  Cam-
bridge, by  the Reverend and faithfull  man of God, Master William Perkins,”
&c., &c.   This is a work of much learning and research, and which he would
not have possessed unless well  instructed.  It is said that he was an easy man
as to his affairs, and that his wife urging him against his will  to  * take stock,”
they found to their surprise that they were possessed of considerable opulence.
John Gurney had  the  erroneous idea that  he  was entitled to an earldom  or
barony by inheritance.  This no doubt originated in his descent from the Anglo-
Norman Barons of Gournay, or perhaps from the Lords Mortimer.

## p. 1059 ##

Nore  188.—EL1ZABETH   SWANTON.  (page 550.)
Elizabeth, the daughter of William Paston,  of Paston, and his wife, — Stal-
ham, married John  Swanton.  She had a daughter and heir,  who married —
Walsham; and they had a daughter and heir, who married — Lovell.
See account of the Paston family in a MS. be-
longing to the Duke of Newcastle, by Francis
Worship, Esq.,  in  “ Norfolk  Archaology,”
vol. iv,, p. 13, note.
NoTE 189.—BARTLETT GURNEY. (page 555.)
Bartlett Gurney was the individual who very much extended the Gurneys’
bank in  Norfolk.   He  was  a  man of strong  Liberal politics,  and was on one
occasion a candidate for  the representation of Norwich, but unsuccessfully, as
he did not  feel  disposed for  parliament,  and  went  away  before  the  election.
There is a good portrait of him at the Norwich bank, by Vandermin, a Flemish
painter of some merit in his day.
Bartlett Gurney died in 1803.   Subjoined are engravings of him and his first
wife, Hannah Chapman, of the Whitby family.

## p. 1060 ##

Nore  190.—MIppLETON   OF  BELSAY  CASTLE. (page 558.)
A new house has been built at Belsay by the present owner, Sir Charles Mid-
dleton Monk, Bart., but the ancient  tower  remains, and some part of the house
that was attached to it of about the time of James I.   Belsay castle appears to
have been built in the early part  of  the fourteenth century.  It is one of those
border fortresses of the better sort, of which several remain.
Note  191.—Tyzack FAMILY.  (page 561.)
Peregrine Tyzack married Elizabeth Middleton, sister of Hannah Middleton,
Joseph Gurney’s wife.   The Tyzacks were a  French Protestant family, who
emigrated to England, together with  two other French families, —Henzay and
Tittery.   They were all three, it  appears, noble families at the Vosges, in Lor-
raine, and were related to each other.   On their coming to England, they settled
at Newcastle-on-Tyne, and in Staffordshire and Worcestershire,—and established

## p. 1061 ##

the broad or window-glass manufacture in England.—(See “Gentleman's Maga-
zine” for November, 1856, p. 592;  for December, 1856, p. 728 ;  and for Jan.,
1857, p. 72.)—The exercise of this art of glass making was in no way deroga-
gatory to the dignity of nobility in France.   Those who practised it were called
“¢  Gentilhommes Verriers.” The family of  Tyzack was calledDu Thisac in Lorraine.
Note  192.—PoEM BY   —  Rack  oF   THETFORD.  (page 56.)
From the ¢     Gentleman’s Magazine,” vol. XL,  p. 220.—Junc 1770.
To the memory of the late  John Gurney of Keswick.
To Gurney’s shade, the muse her tribute pays,
To Gurney’s shade, she sings in pensive lays.
If sense sublime, with ev'ry virtue  joined,
That can exalt, or dignify the mind,
If gentle manners, and a soul sincere,
(Which o'er affliction dropt the melting tear)
Can move the passions, or the heart amend,
Let Crito mourn the exit of his friend.
Graceful his form, and in that form inshrined,
As in its temple, dwelt a purer mind.
By nature fram’d in social life to please,
With true politeness, dignity, and ease.
By  pure religion taught, he strove t’ excell,
In all the noble arts of acting well.
With affluence blest, that affluence he bestow’d,
In acts of private, and of publick good.
No weeping orphans from his gate he spurn’d,
No friendless suppliant from his door he turn'd.
"T was his delight pale misery to relieve,
And make the pensive mourner cease to grieve.
Celestial charity’s benignant flame,
Glow’d in  his breast, and eterniz’d his name.
Serenely mild, as autumn’s evening beams,
His temper shone, with sweet enliv’ning gleams.

## p. 1062 ##

Within his breast did no fierce passions rowl,
Or break the calm composure of his soul.
No mean deceit disgrac’d his ample mind ;
No selfish passion there a place could find ;
But  wise, and great, benevolent and good,
A fair example to mankind he stood.
By  all  respected, by  the  best reverd,
The  virtuous lov’d him, and the vicious fear'd.
Fach noble virtue grac’d his manly mind,
He liv’d a publick blessing to mankind.
Thus through the vale of time he humbly trod,
The paths that lead to virtue's bright abode;
Till now released his spirit wings its way,
In purer regions of eternal day.

## p. 1063 ##

Nore  194.—THE GATHERING  OF   THE  Havs.  (page 577.)
From circumstances which have come to my knowledge, I  am convinced that
this poem and the account of the Hays published with it are altogether fictitious
and not to be depended on.
Note 195.—GAZELEY PAYNELL. (p. 583.)
Gazeley Paynell was the last  of  the family  of Paynell of Belaugh, as here
stated;  he was born about 1680.  The Paynells of Belaugh were a branch of
the Paynells of Boothby Paynell, in Lincolnshire, and settled at Belaugh temp.
Henry  VII.   The  great grandfather of  Gazeley Paynell, Henry  Paynell, of
Belaugh, married Bridget, daughter of John Walpole, Esq., of Houghton.
Norfolk  Pedigrees MS.,  p.  70, in  Sir John
Fenn’s writing.

## p. 1064 ##

Note 196.—Hawisa DE  Gour~ay.  (p. 602.)
The following Charter of Hawisa de Gournay was probably given during her
widowhood, after the death of Roger de Clere, her second husband :—
“Vicecomiti  et  Comitatui Sussex Awys  de Gurnaio Salutem.  Sciatis me
recognovisse pro deo et salute Anime mee et omnium ad me pertinentium, Priori
et Conventui ecclesie sancti Pancratii de Lewes jus  suum quod habeant de tota
terra de Athelingeworth et omnibus pertinentiis suis ex donacione Rogeri de Clera
mariti mei. Quapropter mitto Walterum de fferda senescallum meum et Johannem
de Benetfeld militem ad  faciendam eis  coram vobis plenariam seisinam de tota
terra predicta cum instauramento et homagiis  horum  et omnibus rebus et perti-
nentiis suis;  Habendam  et  tenendam sicut  cyrographia inter  me et predictos
monachos testatur.”—Fol. 188°. (no date.)
From Chartulary of Lewes Priory.
Dodsworth’s MS. Coll. Bod. Lib. No. 65.
“3  oa.«  ati

## p. 1065 ##

Note 197.—Avus AND  Avia. (p. 607.)
The words Avus and Avia are frequently used in the mere sense of Ancestor.
Note 198.—RAoUL DE  GOURNAY. (p. 610.)
Raoul de Gournay different  de  cet  autre  Raoul  dont  nous avons déja parlé
vivoit en meme temps que Robert, dont il  etoit peut-étre le pére, ou au moins
proche parent.  Le  Roy Henry  lui  envoia ordre aussi-bien que Robert de
Gournay et aux autres grands du  royaume de se trouver en armes a Shrowes-
buri (apud Salopiam) pour aller a 'expedition de Galles :  nous ne s¢avons rien
davantage de ce Raoul.
MS. Hist. des Seigneurs de Gournay.
Raoul was probably another son of Eva de Gournay.
Note 199.—ARMS oF HARPTREE. (p. 613.)
The arms of Harptree were argent a cross florée gules;  these were quartered
next to those of Gournay on the monument of  Sir   John Newton at East Harp-
tree.—See p. 694.
Note 200.—RoBERT DE  GoURNAY II. (p. 614.)
Dugdale, in his Baronage, calls this  Robert de Gournay son of Anselm de
Gournay, a younger son of  Hugh de Gournay V., and quotes two authoritie:s
1st, “ Ex  Coll. R. Gl. S.;”  and, 2nd, “Pipe Roll 9 Henry III.  Somerset.”
The first means Ex Collectione Roberti Glover, Somerset. These collections
are at the College of Arms, and  have been examined for me by Sir Charles G.
Young, Garter.  He  finds mention of Robert de Gournay in  transcripts of the
two  Charters given at page 607, which prove Robert to have been son of Thomas
de Harptree and Eva de Gurney.

## p. 1066 ##

The extracts from the Pipe Roll  have been obtained for me by Sir Francis
Palgrave, and are as follows :—
Pipe Roll, 9 Henry III.
‘ Robertus de Gurnaco filius et hares Thoma de Harpetrie reddit Compotum
de xxv  libris et  x1 denariis pro licentia  concordandi sicut continetur in Rotulo
secundo. In  Thesauro xxxv  solidos.  Et  debet xxiii  libras et  viii  solidos
et iiij denarios sed respice infra. per annum lxxii  solidos.
Robertus de Gurnay hares Evee de Gurnay debet x  marcas pro  predicta
Eva de Fine ejus ne distringatur ad se maritandam quae requirebatur supra super
Mauricium de Gand.
Robertus de Gurnaco xxiij libras et viii solidos et iiij  denarios pro  licentia
concordandi sicut supra continetur.  Et de x marcis de fine suo pro Henrico de
Vey'm.   Kt de iij marcis pro habendo chyrographo. per annum Ixx solidos.
It is remarkable that the two authorities quoted by Dugdale should refute his
statement.—Many subsequent writers have followed him in stating this  descent
erroneously.
The Newtons of Barr's court bore as a crest “a king of the Moors, habited in
a robe, and crowned kneeling, and surrendering with  his   dexter  hand  a  sword
proper.” —See p. 694.—This is said to be derived from Sir Ancel Gorney, their
ancestor, who at the battle of Ascalon was with Richard I., and took  a  Moorish
king prisoner.—See Lower’s curiosities of Heraldry p. 167.—This appears to  be
an heraldic fable.
Robert de Gournay is mentioned in  the  Paris  MS. History  of  the Lords of
Gournay.
«  Sous le regne de Henry III. Roy d’Angleterre fils et successeur du Roy Jean
il y a eu un Robert  de Gournay  qui  tenoit  rang  parmi  la  noblesse Angloise,
nous  trouvons  mention de lui a I'occasion des guerres que Henry eut a soutenir
contre Lewelin, prince de Galles, fils de Griffin.”
MS. Hist.  des Seigneurs de Gournay; from
the list of  their collateral descendants.
Robert de Gurnay’s name occurs frequently in the public documents.
Fine Rolls, Vol. 1., p. 205.

## p. 1067 ##

Et  mandatum est Vicecomiti Somerset quod accepta ab eo securitate de centum
libris pro quabus finem fecit cum Rege, pro relevio suo, de terris quas idem
Mauritius habuit in balliva sua, unde idem Robertus propinquior hares ejus  est:
ipsi Roberto plenam seisinam habere faciat retentis in manu Regis maneriis de
Poulet et Were de quibus contentatio coram Regem orta est quis propinquior
heres inde sit.   Teste ut supra.”—(15 Hen. III., 1230.)
This contest as to  Poulet and Were seems to have been decided in favour of
Robert de Gournay, as appears by Fine Rolls, vol  1., p. 207, 15 Hen. III., 1230.

balliva sua sine dilatione plenam saisinam habere faciat.  Teste Rege apud
Westmonasterium xxviij die Novembris.”

In Hallam’s ¢    Middle Ages,” vol. II., p. 337, Qto edition, Monsieur Mayhewe
Gournay occurs as a member of the upper house in the first parliament of Henry
IV., as voting for the detention in safe custody of  the late King Richard, the
names of those so voting being given.   Mr. Hallam  says the  knights bannerets
were occasionally summoned to the upper house, and had the title of Monsieur,
and the barons that of Sire;  as le Sire de Bergavenny, le Baron de Greystock,
&c.; but  by the following Note 205 it appears Matthew de Gournay had the
status baronis et honor vexilli before his death.  Hallam  quotes Parl.  Rot., vol.
IIL, p. 427.
Norte 205.—PHILIPPA  DE   GOURNAY.  (p. 679.)
It seems that Philippa was widow af Sir Robert Asheton, who was constable
of Dover Castle.  This appears by a record in the Carlton Ride, London, where

## p. 1073 ##

it  is  shewn that  she  was  the  executrix to Robert  Ashton, being then wife of
Matthew de Gourney; and the MS.  Harl.  972 is quoted erroneously below,
the extract being as follows:   Philippa  olim  uxor Robert Asheton Militis
dudum et nuper relicta Domini Mathei de Gorney nobilis viri dum vixit et modo
Johannis Tiptoft militis. ob. 3 Maii 5 H. 6.
An  Inspeximus Charter of  the King of a Charter of Philippa wife of John
Tiptoft Knight, and Thomas ffelde doctor of Laws &c.

67, Patent 9 H.  14.

vestre notum facimus per presentes quod nos Philippa predicta
"    dudum nuper relicta nobilis viri dum vixit Mathei de Gornay qui  statu Baronis
et Honore Vexilli dum in humanis agebat predictus existebat &c.—Nos prefato
Johanni Tiptoft damus et vendimus omnia et  singula terras, Baronias, Dominia,
et flortelicia suprascripta que Edwardus nuper  Rex  Anglie et ffrancie dedit
prefatis Dominis de Gose et Seynar Morepue &c.”
These were probably domains in  Gascony.—I suppose * Honor Vexilli,”
means Knight banneret.
Nore  206.—STOKE-UNDER-HAMDEN. (page 680.)
I  was at Stoke-under-Hamden in 1856, and was surprised to find considerable
remains of the mansion of the Beauchamps and Gournays, especially after both
Leland,* in his Itinerary, and  Collinson,  in  his  history of  Somersetshire,} had
stated  that  it   was to  a  considerable extent  in ruins.   I  think it likely, how-
ever, that after the dissolution of the chantry which it contained, it underwent a
complete repair, and  was altogether altered and transformed, in the reign of
Elizabeth, as I  found  the  date 1595 on the panel of  one  of  the rooms.   It  is
now  a  farm house, with  much of the  ancient mansion remaining;  the  whole
of  stone, taken from the adjoining Hamden hill.  It  is  close to a road and
entered by an arched gateway.
®  Itinerary, Vol. 2, p. 98.   t  Vol. 8, p. 816.

## p. 1074 ##

AN  wry
hogs   4
On entering the court yard is a porch which leads into an inner court, with
a belfry on the  top  of a  gable  on  one  side;  and  opposite, on  another gable,
an ancient sun dial.
EET   2

## p. 1075 ##

The choir  of  the Chapel in  which Sir Matthew de Gournay was buried is
traceable, the  east window and  a piscina  being obvious; but  the  body of the
chapel is totally altered and is now a cider cellar.
Passing through the porch, the inner court yard is entered, where the outside
of the ancient chapel is visible.

## p. 1076 ##

The barns and outhouses exist, all of stone ;  also a circular dovecot of singular
construction.
Leland me tions  that the provost of the Chantry “hath  a large house in the
village of  Stoke thereby.”  I  think  it likely  the Flower de luce public house
is here intended, which is an ancient stone structure with a very beautiful arched
door way of the early English character.
The church of Stoke-under-Hamden is a very interesting one.  There is much
Norman architecture in  it;  and over the door-way some very early carvings,
being two signs of the Zodiac, Sagittarius and Leo.
Nore  207.—HucE DE   GOURNAY. (p. 686.)
Sous le regne du meme Roy Edouard III il y a eu un Hugues de Gournay le
seigneur vint & France avec Henry evéque de Lincolne qui etoit envoi¢ de la part
d’Edouard pour declarer la guerre au Roy Philippe de Valois.  Pendant ce voiage
nous trouvons que Hugues recut du  Roy d’ Angleterre des lettres de protection
ou de sauve garde pour I’ espace d’ un ans datteés de Havering atte Boure le 10
Avril 1338.—C’ est tout ce que nous s¢avons de lui.
MSS. Hist. des Seigneurs de Gournay.

Note  208.—Thomas de Gournay. (p. 689.)
Somerset.—Indentura facta inter Thomam Ap Adam Dominum de Beverston
et  Thomam Gornay filium  Hugonis  Gornay pro manerio de Donseval in
comitatu somerset,—hamlett de Betteslee cum Passagio et reditu in Panbere,
welewe et netherwere manerio de Grof  justa strogaile, et manerio de Estarptre.
Dodsworth  MSS.  Bod. Lib.  No.  85,  clause
Rex &ec. dilecto et fideli suo willelmo de Thorp salutem.—Volentes certis  de
causis certeriori super indictamento facto coram vobis et sociis vestris Justiciariis
nostris ad diversas transgressiones et felonias in comitatu Somerset audiendas et
terminandas assignatis de quibusdam feloniis et maleficiis in comitatu predicto
factis unde Johannes de Mohun Chivaler,Thomas de Gourneye Chivaler,Paganus
de Mohun, et Patricius de Mohun indictati sunt ut dicitur vobis mandamus quod
indictamentum predictum cum omnibus illud tangentibus nobis in  cancellariam
nostram sub sigillo vestro distincte  et  aperte sine dilacione mittatis et hoc breve
ut  nos  ulterius inde  fieri   faciamus quod  de jure  et  secundum legem et
consuetudinem Regni nostri  Anglie fuerit faciendum executione indictamenti
predicti  ulterius   faciendi  usque  ab   quindenam Pasche proxime  future
supersedeatis.
Teste Rege apud Turrim London. xxii. Marecii.
Claus. Rot. 18, E.III. (1344). M. 10 indorso.
Note  209.—NEwTONS   OR   CARADOCS.  (p. 694.)
In a shield of arms formerly on  the monument of Sir   John Newton, were the
quarterings of Newton.   Amongst others, paly  of six or and azure, Gournay ;
and argent a cross florée gules, Harptree.
A  branch  of the Newtons of  Barrs’ Court settled in Lincolnshire;  and there
is an idea that Sir Isaac Newton came of this stock.   There is a pedigree of  his
family in the History of Grantham.
